4.3

Four-Body Decays

Currently four models are implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
, which provide
four-body decays of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
into three mesons for a total of twelve

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay channels. The first hadronic current is used specifically
for final states with three pions, while the second two hadronic
currents are generalised currents which can be used for final states
with both pions and kaons. The final four-body hadronic current
includes an explicit photon for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
channel. The
general hadronic current is given by,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}\propto

\displaystyle\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{q^{\mu}q^{\nu}}{s_{1}}\right)\bigg((F_{3}-F_{2})q_{2}+(F_{1}-F_{3})q_{3}+(F_{2}-F_{1})q_{4}\bigg)^{\mu}

(138)

\displaystyle+F_{4}q^{\mu}+iF_{5}\epsilon^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})

from
Ref.
[
jadach.93.1
]

, where
F_{i}
are form factors specific to the
model,
\epsilon
is the permutation operator, and
q
is
q_{2}+q_{2}+q_{3}
, where
q_{2}
,
q_{3}
, and
q_{4}
are the four-momenta of the three
mesons. The centre-of-mass energies are defined as,

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle=(q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4})^{\mu}(q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4})_{\mu}

(139)

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle=(q_{3}+q_{4})^{\mu}(q_{3}+q_{4})_{\mu}

\displaystyle s_{3}

\displaystyle=(q_{2}+q_{4})^{\mu}(q_{2}+q_{4})_{\mu}

\displaystyle s_{4}

\displaystyle=(q_{2}+q_{3})^{\mu}(q_{2}+q_{3})_{\mu}

where
s_{1}
gives the centre-of-mass energy for the three meson
system, while
s_{2}
,
s_{3}
, and
s_{4}
provide the centre-of-mass
energies for the combinations of meson pairs.

4.3.1

CLEO Current

The
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
and
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay
channels are modelled using the current from the CLEO collaboration fit
of
Ref.
[
cleo.99.2
]

which first proceed through an
a_{1}
resonance, and
then an additional scalar or tensor resonance. Both the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
and
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay channels can proceed
through scalar
f_{0}
, tensor
f_{2}
, or scalar
\sigma
resonances. The

F_{4}
and
F_{5}
form factors for the CLEO current are zero, and so
only the first three form factors need to be specified.

The form factor
F_{1}
for the
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel is
given by,

\displaystyle F_{1}^{-}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}\Bigg(-{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{p}BW_{p}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

(140)

\displaystyle-\frac{{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}}{3}BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})(s_{2}-s_{4})\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+w_{f_{2}}\Bigg(\frac{s_{4}-s_{3}}{2}BW_{d}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{18s_{3}}(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{3})(s_{1}+s_{2}-m_{2}^{2})BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})\Bigg)

while the first form factor for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
channel is given by,

\displaystyle F_{1}^{0}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}\Bigg({w_{\rho}}_{i}^{p}BW_{p}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

(141)

\displaystyle-\frac{{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}}{3}BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})(s_{4}-s_{2}-m_{4}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4}m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{w_{f_{2}}}{18s_{4}}(s_{1}-m_{4}^{2}+s_{4})(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{4})BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

where
{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{p}
are the complex couplings of the
p
-wave
\rho

resonances to the
a_{1}
,
{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}
are the
d
-wave
\rho

couplings,
w_{f_{0}}
is the
f_{0}
coupling,
w_{f_{2}}
is the
f_{2}

coupling, and
w_{\sigma}
is the
\sigma
coupling.

The second form factor,
F_{2}
, is given by,

\displaystyle F_{2}^{-}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}\Bigg({w_{\rho}}_{i}^{p}BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

(142)

\displaystyle-\frac{{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}}{3}BW_{p}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})(s_{3}-s_{4})\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+w_{f_{2}}\Bigg(\frac{s_{4}-s_{2}}{2}BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{18s_{2}}(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{2})(s_{1}+s_{2}-m_{2}^{2})BW_{d}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})\Bigg)

for the
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel while,

\displaystyle F_{2}^{0}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}\Bigg(-\frac{{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{p}}{3}BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

(143)

\displaystyle-{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}BW_{p}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})(s_{4}-s_{3}-m_{4}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{w_{f_{2}}}{18s_{4}}(s_{1}-m_{4}^{2}+s_{4})(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{4})BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

is the second form factor for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
channel.

The third form factor is given by,

\displaystyle F_{3}^{-}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}-{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}\Bigg(\frac{1}{3}(s_{3}-s_{4})BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

(144)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3}(s_{2}-s_{4})BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\Bigg(w_{\sigma}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})+w_{f_{0}}BW_{s}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{f_{0}},\Gamma_{f_{0}})\Bigg)

\displaystyle+w_{f_{2}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{18s_{2}}(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{2})(s_{1}+s_{2}-m_{2}^{2})BW_{d}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{18s_{3}}(4m_{2}^{2}-s_{3})(s_{1}+s_{3}-m_{2}^{2})BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})\Bigg)

for the
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel and,

\displaystyle F_{3}^{0}=~

\displaystyle BW_{a_{1}}(s_{1})\sum_{i}{w_{\rho}}_{i}^{d}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{3}(s_{4}-s_{3}-m_{4}^{2}+m_{3}^{2})

(145)

\displaystyle BW_{p}(m_{3},m_{4},s_{2},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}(s_{4}-s_{2}-m_{4}^{2}+m_{2}^{2})BW_{p}(m_{2},m_{4},s_{3},{m_{\rho}}_{i},{\Gamma_{\rho}}_{i})\Bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{w_{f_{2}}}{2}(s_{2}-s_{3})BW_{d}(m_{2},m_{3},s_{4},m_{f_{2}},\Gamma_{f_{2}})

for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
channel.

All the complex couplings are calculated from a phase and amplitude
using
Eq.
136

. The on-shell widths and masses along
with the phases and couplings used in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
for the
\rho

resonances are given in
Table
4.3.1

, while the
scalar
f_{0}
and
sigma
, and tensor
f_{2}
parameters are given in

Table
4.3.1

. Both sets of parameters are
based on the fits performed by the CLEO
collaboration
[
cleo.99.2
]
.

The Breit-Wigners used in the form factors
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
, and
F_{3}
are

s
,

, and

-wave Breit-Wigners of
Eqs.
127

,

128
, and
129
. The Breit-Wigner for the

a_{1}
is given by,

BW_{a_{1}}(s)=\frac{m_{a_{1}}^{2}}{m_{a_{1}}^{2}-s-\Gamma_{a_{1}}(s)}

(146)

where
m_{a_{1}}
is the on-shell mass of the
a_{1}
. The running width

\Gamma_{a_{1}}(s)
is the weighted sum of the three partial widths

\Gamma_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}}(s)
,
\Gamma_{\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}}(s)
, and
the
s
-wave contribution
\Gamma_{KK^{*}}(s)
,

\Gamma_{a_{1}}(s)=w_{\pi}\big(\Gamma_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}}(s)+\Gamma_{\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{0}}(s)+w_{K}\Gamma_{KK^{*}}(s)\big)

(147)

where
w_{\pi}
and
w_{K}
are the pion and kaon weights given in

Table
2

, and
\Gamma_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}}(s)
,
\Gamma_{\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}}(s)
, and
\Gamma_{KK^{*}}(s)

are piece-wise fitted functions given in
Table
4.3.1

with the parameters given in ﬁTau:ThreePions.A1Parameters and

4.3.1
.

4.3.2

Three Mesons with Kaons

The model of
Ref.
[
finkemeier.95.1
]

by Finkemeier and Mirkes is
implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
to provide
K^{-}\pi^{-}K^{+}
,
K^{0}\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}
,
K_{S}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{S}^{0}
,
K_{L}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{L}^{0}
,
K_{S}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{L}^{0}
,
K^{-}\pi^{0}K^{0}
,
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}K^{-}
,
K^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
, and

\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}\pi^{0}
decays for the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
. For this model the
F_{1}
,

F_{2}
, and
F_{4}
form factors are non-zero, while the
F_{3}
and
F_{5}

form factors are zero. The form factors for this model are given in

Table
4.3.2

for
F_{1}
,

Table
4.3.2

for
F_{2}
, and

Table
4.3.2

for
F_{4}
. These form factors use
the weighted sums of the intermediate resonances,

\displaystyle t(s,\vec{m},\vec{\Gamma},\vec{w})=\frac{1}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}w_{i}BW(s,m_{i},\Gamma_{i})

(148)

\displaystyle t_{p}(m_{0},m_{1},s,\vec{m},\vec{\Gamma},\vec{w})=\frac{1}{\sum_{i}w_{i}}w_{i}BW_{p}(m_{0},m_{1},s,m_{i},\Gamma_{i})

where
\vec{m}
is a vector of the resonance masses,
\vec{\Gamma}

are their widths, and
\vec{w}
are their real weights. The lengths of
these three vectors are the same, and correspond to the number of
resonances being modelled. Here,
t
is a sum of fixed width
Breit-Wigners, given by
Eq.
126

and
t_{p}
is the sum of
p
-wave
Breit-Wigners, given by
Eq.
128

.

\captionabove

ﬁTau:ThreeMesonsWithKaons.F1 through

4.3.2
for
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays into three
mesons where one or more of the mesons are
kaons.

resonances

\vec{m}~[\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}]

\begin{pmatrix}1,&\phantom{-}\frac{1}{20}\end{pmatrix}

The channels containing a single pion can proceed through an initial

a_{1}
resonance, similar to the three pion channels modelled with the
CLEO current. The Breit-Wigner for the
a_{1}
is now defined as,

BW_{a_{1}}(s)=\frac{m_{a_{1}}^{2}}{m_{a_{1}}^{2}-s-im_{a_{1}}\Gamma_{a_{1}}\frac{g_{a_{1}}(s)}{g_{a_{1}}(m_{a_{1}}^{2})}}

(149)

where,

g_{a_{1}}(s)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{if }s<(3m_{\pi^{-}})^{2}\\
\begin{aligned} &4.1(s-9m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})^{3}(1-3.3(s-9m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})\\
&+5.8(s-9m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})^{2}\end{aligned}&\mbox{else if }s<(m_{\rho}+m_{\pi^{-}})^{2}\\
s(1.623+\frac{10.38}{s}-\frac{9.32}{s^{2}}+\frac{0.65}{s^{3}})&\mbox{else}\\
\end{cases}

(150)

is an
a_{1}
phase-space factor as given in
Ref.
[
kuhn.90.1
]

. The
channels with a single pion can also proceed through initial
\rho

resonances which are modelled with
p
-wave Breit-Wigners. The channels
containing two pions proceed through either initial
K_{1}
resonances
modelled with fixed width Breit-Wigners or
K^{*}
resonances modelled
with
p
-wave Breit-Wigners.

All the channels with a single pion can proceed through secondary

K^{*}
or
\rho
resonances, except the
K_{S}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{S}^{0}
and
K_{L}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{L}^{0}
channels which can only proceed through secondary
K^{*}

resonances. Additionally, the
K^{-}\pi^{-}K^{+}
,
K^{0}\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}
,
and
K_{S}^{0}\pi^{-}K_{L}^{0}
channels can proceed via secondary
\omega

resonances. The channels with two pions can proceed through secondary

K^{*}
resonances, and the
K^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
and
\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}\pi^{0}
can proceed through additional secondary
\rho
resonances.

m_{23}
distributions for the five channels modelled with this
current containing a single pion are given in

Fig.
24

while the distributions for the
three channels containing two pions are given in

Fig.
25

. For all single pion channels,
the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
and
Herwig
++
distributions match well. However, he

Tauola
distributions, particularly in the
K^{0}\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}
and

K^{-}\pi^{0}K^{0}
channels, do not match. This is expected, as the

Tauola
implementation for these channels uses an older model, which
among other differences, does not include the
K^{*}(1410)

resonance. This older model is used for general three meson decays and
is introduced in the following section. A similar level of agreement
between the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
and
Herwig
++
distributions for the two pion
channels can also be seen, with the same expected discrepancies with
the
Tauola
distributions.

4.3.3

General Three Mesons

The older and more general model of Decker, et al. of

Ref.
[
decker.93.1
]

is also implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
and can be used to
perform the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{+}
,
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
,
K^{-}\pi^{-}K^{+}
,

K^{0}\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}
,
K^{-}\pi^{0}K^{0}
,
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}K^{-}
,
K^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
,
\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}\pi^{0}
, and
\pi^{-}\pi^{0}\eta
decays of the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
. However, by default, only the
\pi^{-}\pi^{0}\eta
decay of the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is performed using this model. The same summation of fixed width
and
p
-wave Breit-Wigners using
t
and
t_{p}
of

Eq.
148

is used, as well as the same
a_{1}

Breit-Wigner of
Eq.
149

. However, the
masses, widths, and real weights of the resonances differ from those
of
Table
4.3.2

and are given in

Table
4.3.3

. Note that no
\omega

resonances are introduced.

The form factors for this model are provided in

Table
4.3.3

for
F_{1}
,

Table
4.3.3

for
F_{2}
, and

Table
4.3.3

for
F_{4}
. Here
F_{3}
and
F_{5}
are
zero for all channels. The three pion channels decay through an
initial
a_{1}
resonance followed by a decay through secondary
\rho

resonances. The
K^{-}\pi^{-}K^{+}
and
K^{-}\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}
channels can
decay through an initial
a_{1}
or
\rho
resonance and through
secondary
K^{*}
and
\rho
resonances. The
K^{-}\pi^{0}K^{0}
channel can
proceed through an initial
a_{1}
resonance followed by secondary
K^{*}

resonances. The
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}K^{-}
channel proceeds through initial

K_{1}
resonances and secondary
K^{*}
resonances, while the
K^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
and
\pi^{-}\bar{K}^{0}\pi^{0}
channels proceed through initial

K_{1}
or
K^{*}
resonances and secondary
K^{*}
or
\rho
resonances.

In
Fig.
26

the
m_{23}
distribution from

\textsc{Pythia}~8
,
Herwig
++
, and
Tauola
for the
\pi^{-}\pi^{0}\eta
decay
channel is given. There is good agreement between all three
generators. Distributions for the additional decays that can be
performed with the general three meson model but are not used by
default in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
are provided in
App.

for brevity, where

Fig.

are the
m_{23}
distributions
for decays with only one pion and

Fig.

are the distributions for decays
with two or more pions.

4.3.4

Two Pions with a Photon

The
\omega
meson can decay into
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
or
\gamma\pi^{0}
final states. Consequently, the decay
{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\omega\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
can result in a four-body or five-body final state. The
five-body final state is included in the intermediate resonances of
the five-body decays in
Sect.
4.4

, but the four-body final
state decay
{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\gamma\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
needs to be accounted for with
an independent model. In
\textsc{Pythia}~8
the model of Jadach et al.
from
Ref.
[
jadach.93.1
]

is implemented for this decay, and is given by
the hadronic current,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}\propto~

\displaystyle F(s_{1},\vec{m}_{\rho},\vec{G}_{\rho},\vec{w}_{\rho})F(0,\vec{m}_{\rho},\vec{G}_{\rho},\vec{w}_{\rho})F(s_{4},\vec{m}_{\omega},\vec{G}_{\omega},\vec{w}_{\omega})

(151)

\displaystyle\bigg(\varepsilon_{2}^{\mu}\left(m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}{q_{4}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu}-{q_{3}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu}({q_{4}}_{\nu}q_{3}^{\nu}-{q_{4}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu})\right)

\displaystyle-{q_{3}}^{\mu}\left(({q_{3}}_{\nu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu})({q_{4}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu})-({q_{4}}_{\nu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu})({q_{3}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu})\right)

\displaystyle-{q_{2}}^{\mu}\left(({q_{3}}_{\nu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu})({q_{4}}_{\nu}q_{3}^{\nu})-({q_{4}}_{\nu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu})(m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}+{q_{3}}_{\nu}q_{2}^{\nu})\right)\bigg)

where
\varepsilon_{2}
is the polarisation vector of the photon given
by
Eq.
38

. Unlike the other hadronic currents of

Sect.
4

, this current takes on two spin states due to the photon
in the final state. The form factor
F
is given by,

F(s,\vec{m},\vec{\Gamma},\vec{w})=\sum_{j}\frac{w_{j}}{m_{j}^{2}-s-im_{j}\Gamma_{j}}

(152)

where
s
is the centre-of-mass energy,
\vec{m}
is the vector of
resonance masses,
\vec{\Gamma}
the vector of widths, and
\vec{w}

the vector of real weights. These vectors are given in

Table
4.3.4

, where the channel proceeds through
initial
\rho
resonances followed by an
\omega
resonance.

\captionabove

for the

channel.

.

.

the
m_{23}
distributions of the photon
and neutral pion are given for
\textsc{Pythia}~8
,

, and

Tauola
. All three distributions match well, although the
Tauola
distribution deviates slightly from the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
and

distributions due to a difference in the parameters used in
implementing the channel.

4.4

Five-Body Decays

Five-body decays of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
into four pions and a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
neutrino have
been observed experimentally, but due to the low branching ratio and
complexity of the decay, experimental observations do not strongly
constrain the model
[
weinstein.01.1
]
. Consequently, the hadronic
current for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
and
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decays in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
is based on the Novosibirsk model of

Refs.
[
bondar.02.1
]

and
[
golonka.03.1
]
, a phenomenological model
fitted to four pion production from electron-positron annihilation
within the energy range of
1.0
to
2.5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. Within this model the
hadronic decay of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
occurs through an initial excited
\rho

resonance and secondary
a_{1}
,
\rho
, and
\sigma
resonances. For
the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay an additional secondary
\omega

resonance is also included. The four pion hadronic currents are the
summation of the resonance subcurrents,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}}

\displaystyle\propto J^{\mu}_{0,a_{1}\rightarrow\rho\pi}+J^{\mu}_{0,a_{1}\rightarrow\sigma\pi}

(153)

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}}

\displaystyle\propto J^{\mu}_{-,a_{1}\rightarrow\rho\pi}+J^{\mu}_{-,a_{1}\rightarrow\sigma\pi}+J^{\mu}_{-,\omega\rightarrow\rho\pi}

where
J_{0,X}
are the subcurrents for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}

decay and
J_{-,X}
are the subcurrents for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay.

The hadronic subcurrent for the decay of the
a_{1}
to a
\rho\pi
pair
for the three neutral pion current is given by the combinatorics of
the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
final state,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{0,a_{1}\rightarrow\rho\pi}=~

\displaystyle G_{1}(s)\bigg(t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{4},q_{5},q_{2})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{2},q_{5},q_{4})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{3},q_{5},q_{2})

(154)

\displaystyle+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{2},q_{5},q_{3})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{5},q_{4})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{5},q_{3})\bigg)

where
t_{1}
is a four-vector based on the
a_{1}
and
\rho
resonance
propagators, and
G_{1}
is a phenomenological fit of the four pion
invariant mass distribution given later in ﬁTau:FourPions.GFit
through
4.4
. The Mandelstam
variable
s
as used within this context is given by,
q_{\mu}q^{\mu}

where,

q^{\mu}=(q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4}+q_{5})^{\mu}

(155)

is the four-momentum of the four pion system.

The hadronic subcurrent for the decay of the
a_{1}
into a
\sigma\pi

pair for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
pion final state is given by,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{0,a_{1}\rightarrow\sigma\pi}=~

\displaystyle G_{1}(s)\bigg(t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{5},q_{4},q_{2})+t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{3},q_{2})+t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{5},q_{4},q_{3})

(156)

\displaystyle-t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{5},q_{3},q_{4},q_{2})-t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{5},q_{4},q_{3},q_{2})-t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{5},q_{2},q_{4},q_{3})\bigg)

where
t_{2}
is a four-vector based on the
a_{1}
and
\sigma
resonance
propagators.

For the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay,
the hadronic subcurrent for the decay of the
a_{1}
into
\rho\pi
is
given by,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{-,a_{1}\rightarrow\rho\pi}=~

\displaystyle G_{2}(s)\bigg(t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{5},q_{4},q_{2})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{3},q_{2})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{4},q_{5},q_{2})

(157)

\displaystyle+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{3},q_{5},q_{2})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{3},q_{5})+t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},q_{5})\bigg)

where
G_{2}
is another phenomenological fit of the four pion invariant
mass also given in ﬁTau:FourPions.GFit through

4.4
. The
a_{1}
to
\sigma\pi

subcurrent is similar,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{-,a_{1}\rightarrow\sigma\pi}=~

\displaystyle G_{2}(s)\bigg(t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{3},q_{5})+t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},q_{5})

(158)

\displaystyle-t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{2},q_{4},q_{5})-t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{2},q_{3},q_{5})\bigg)

but the
\sigma
propagator is accounted for by
t_{2}
. Finally, the
additional
\omega
to
\rho\pi
subcurrent for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel is given by,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}_{-,\omega\rightarrow\rho\pi}=~

\displaystyle G_{3}(s)\bigg(t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{5},q_{4},q_{2})+t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{3},q_{2})-t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{4},q_{5},q_{2})

(159)

\displaystyle-t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{3},q_{5},q_{2})-t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{2},q_{4},q_{5})-t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{2},q_{3},q_{5})\bigg)

where
t_{3}
is a four-vector based on the
\omega
and
\rho

propagators, and
G_{3}
is a phenomenological fit of the four pion
invariant mass given in ﬁTau:FourPions.GFit through

4.4
.

The the four-vector
t_{1}
is given by,

\displaystyle t_{1}^{\mu}(q_{i},q_{j},q_{k},q_{l})=~

\displaystyle-F_{a_{1}}(s_{a_{1}})\frac{m_{a_{1}}^{2}(m_{\rho}^{2}+m_{\rho}\Gamma_{\rho}dm(0))}{D_{a_{1}}(s_{a_{1}})D_{\rho}(s_{\rho})}

(160)

\displaystyle\bigg((q_{\nu}q_{a_{1}}^{\nu})\big(({q_{k}}_{\nu}q_{a_{1}}^{\nu})q_{l}^{\mu}-({q_{l}}_{\nu}q_{a_{1}}^{\nu})q_{k}^{\mu}\big)

\displaystyle+\big((q_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})-(q_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})\big)q_{a_{1}}^{\mu}\bigg)

where
D_{a_{1}}
and
D_{\rho}
are the denominators for the
a_{1}
and

\rho
propagators respectively, and are given later in

Eqs.
165

and
166
. These
propagators differ from those used by Bondar et al. in

Ref.
[
bondar.02.1
]

in the numerator where the
a_{1}
and corrected

\rho
masses have been added such that the forms of the propagators
are that of Breit-Wigners, as is done in
Herwig
++
. The
\rho
mass
is corrected by a running mass correction,
dm(s)
, which is defined
later in
Eq.
167

. The Mandelstam variables and
propagator four-momenta are,

\displaystyle q_{a_{1}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(q_{j}+q_{k}+q_{l})^{\mu}

\displaystyle q_{\rho}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(q_{k}+q_{l})^{\mu}

\displaystyle q_{\sigma}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(q_{k}+q_{l})^{\mu}

\displaystyle q_{\omega}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(q_{j}+q_{k}+q_{l})^{\mu}

(161)

\displaystyle s_{a_{1}}

\displaystyle={q_{a_{1}}}_{\mu}q_{a_{1}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle s_{\rho}

\displaystyle={q_{\rho}}_{\mu}q_{\rho}^{\mu}

\displaystyle s_{\sigma}

\displaystyle={q_{\sigma}}_{\mu}q_{\sigma}^{\mu}

\displaystyle s_{\omega}

\displaystyle={q_{\omega}}_{\mu}q_{\omega}^{\mu}

for the
a_{1}
,
\rho
,
\sigma
and
\omega
. The form factor for the

a_{1}
,
F_{a_{1}}
, is given by,

F_{a_{1}}(s)=\left(\frac{\Lambda^{2}+m_{a_{1}}^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}+s}\right)^{2}

(162)

where the cutoff value
\Lambda
is taken as
1.2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
from

Ref.
[
bondar.99.1
]

.

the

,

, and
\omega
resonances.

The four-vector
t_{2}
is similar to
t_{1}
but with different
combinatorics and propagators,

\displaystyle t_{2}^{\mu}(q_{i},q_{j},q_{k},q_{l})=~

\displaystyle w_{\sigma}F_{a_{1}}(s_{a_{1}})\frac{m_{a_{1}}^{2}m_{\sigma}^{2}}{D_{a_{1}}(s_{a_{1}})D_{\sigma}(s_{\sigma})}

(163)

\displaystyle\bigg((q^{\nu}{q_{a_{1}}}_{\nu})s_{a_{1}}q_{j}^{\mu}-(q^{\nu}{q_{j}}_{\nu})s_{a_{1}}q_{a_{1}}^{\mu}\bigg)

where
w_{\sigma}
is a complex weight for the
\sigma
resonance
calculated from an amplitude and phase given in

Table
4.4

, and
D_{\sigma}
is the denominator of
the propagator for the
\sigma
given later in

Eq.
170

. Again, the form of
t_{2}
used in

\textsc{Pythia}~8
differs from Bondar et al. in the propagator with the
addition of
m_{a_{1}}^{2}
and
m_{\sigma}^{2}
in the numerator.

The final four-vector,
t_{3}
, is used only in the
\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
decay, where the second resonance can occur through either an

\omega
or
a_{1}
,

\displaystyle t_{3}^{\mu}(q_{i},q_{j},q_{k},q_{l})=~

\displaystyle w_{\omega}F_{\omega}(s_{\omega})\frac{m_{\omega}^{2}(m_{\rho}^{2}+m_{\rho}\Gamma_{\rho}dm(0))}{D_{\omega}(s_{\omega})D_{\rho}(s_{\rho})}

(164)

\displaystyle\bigg(\big((q_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})-(q_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})\big)q_{j}^{\mu}

\displaystyle+\big((q_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{j}^{\nu})-(q_{\nu}q_{j}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{l}^{\nu})\big)q_{k}^{\mu}

\displaystyle+\big((q_{\nu}q_{j}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})-(q_{\nu}q_{k}^{\nu})({q_{i}}_{\nu}q_{j}^{\nu})\big)q_{l}^{\mu}\bigg)

where
w_{\omega}
is a complex weight calculated from a phase and
amplitude given in
Table
4.4

using

Eq.
136

,
F_{\omega}
is the
\omega
form factor, and

D_{\omega}
is the denominator of the propagator for the
\omega
. Note
that the
\omega
mass and corrected
\rho
mass have been added to
the numerator of the propagator. Currently, the
\omega
form factor
is taken as
F_{\omega}=1
.

The energy dependent denominator of the propagator for the
a_{1}
is
given by,

D_{a_{1}}(s)=s-m_{a_{1}}^{2}+i\sqrt{s}\Gamma_{a_{1}}(s)

(165)

where the running width for the
a_{1}
,
\Gamma_{a_{1}}
, is calculated
from integrating over phase-space for the
a_{1}\rightarrow\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
and
a_{1}\rightarrow\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}
decays taking into
account the combinatorics of the
\rho
and
\sigma
propagators. In

Tauola
this phase-space integration is performed using Monte Carlo
integration and an interpolation table is built at initialisation. In

\textsc{Pythia}~8
, a fit of the
Tauola
interpolation table, given in

Table
4.4

with parameters in

Table
4.4

and plotted in

Fig.
28

, is used for
\Gamma_{a_{1}}
.

width of

where

and

.

current.

The denominator for the
\rho
resonance propagator includes a running
mass correction,
dm(s)
, similar to that of Gounaris and Sakurai from

Ref.
[
gounaris.68.1
]

which is also applied to
m_{\rho}
in the
numerators of the propagators from
Eqs.
160

to

164
. Consequently, the denominator to the

\rho
propagator is,

D_{\rho}(s)=s-m_{\rho}^{2}-m_{\rho}\Gamma_{\rho}dm(s)+im_{\rho}\Gamma_{\rho}\left(\frac{m_{\rho}^{2}(s-4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})^{3}}{s(m_{\rho}^{2}-4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})^{3}}\right)^{1/2}

(166)

where the running mass correction is given as,

dm(s)=\frac{m_{\rho}\big(h(s)-h(m_{\rho}^{2})-(s-m_{\rho}^{2})dh(m_{\rho}^{2})\big)}{(m_{\rho}^{2}-4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})^{3/2}}

(167)

and
h(s)
is defined by Golonka et al. in
Ref.
[
golonka.03.1
]

as,

h(s)=\begin{cases}\frac{x}{\pi}\left(\frac{\ln(1+x)}{\ln(1-x)}\right)(s-4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2})&\mbox{if $s>4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}$}\\
-\frac{8m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}}{\pi}&\mbox{if $s=0$}\\
0&\mbox{else}\\
\end{cases}

(168)

where
x=\sqrt{1-4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}/s}
. The derivative of
h(s)

with respect to
s
is given by,

dh(s)=\begin{cases}\frac{x}{\pi s}(sx+(2m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}+s)\ln\left(\frac{1+x}{1-x}\right)&\mbox{if $s>4m_{\pi^{-}}^{2}$}\\
0&\mbox{else}\\
\end{cases}

(169)

which is used in the calculation of
dm(s)
in

Eq.
167

.

The denominator of the
\sigma
propagator,
D_{\sigma}(s)
, is given by
the denominator of the
s
-wave Breit-Wigner of
Eq.
127

,

D_{\sigma}(s)=s-m_{\sigma}^{2}-\left(\frac{i\Gamma_{\sigma}m_{\sigma}^{2}}{\sqrt{s}}\right)\left(\frac{g(m_{\pi^{-}},m_{\pi^{-}},s)}{g(m_{\pi^{-}},m_{\pi^{-}},m_{\sigma}^{2})}\right)

(170)

where
g(m_{\pi^{-}},m_{\pi^{-}},s)
is given by
Eq.
130

. For the

\omega
resonance,

D_{\omega}(s)=s-m_{\omega}^{2}+im_{\omega}\Gamma_{\omega}(s)

(171)

where the running width of the
\omega
is taken from Golonka et
al. of
Ref.
[
golonka.03.1
]

and is given by the fit of

Table
4.4

with the parameters of

Table
4.4

.

The phenomenological
G
factors of the hadronic currents used in

Eqs.
154

through

159
are fitted piece-wise with
the functions of
Table
4.4

, where the limits

s_{i}
are given in
Table
4.4

and the fit
parameters
P_{i}
are given in

Table
4.4

. The fits of the three
G

factors are plotted in
Fig.
29

. Note that the
G

factors given in
Ref.
[
bondar.02.1
]

must be corrected using the
functions described in
Ref.
[
golonka.03.1
]

.

in

30(b)

\omega
resonance of the
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel. The
Tauola

\textsc{Pythia}~8
and
Herwig
++
due to differences in the implementations
and the parameters used in the hadronic current.

4.5

Six-Body Decays

Three six-body decays are implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
with the hadronic
final states
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
,
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
, and
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}\pi^{+}
. These decays are modelled
with the five pion current by Kühn and Wa̧s of

Ref.
[
kuhn.08.1
]

. The decays can occur through
a_{1}
,
\rho
,
\omega
,
and
\sigma
resonances. The hadronic currents for the three decay
channels are given by,

\displaystyle J_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}}

\displaystyle\propto~

\displaystyle J_{a}^{\mu}(q_{6},q_{4},q_{2},q_{5},q_{3})+J_{a}^{\mu}(q_{6},q_{5},q_{2},q_{4},q_{3})

(172)

\displaystyle+J_{a}^{\mu}(q_{6},q_{4},q_{3},q_{5},q_{2})+J_{a}^{\mu}(q_{6},q_{5},q_{3},q_{4},q_{2})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{6},q_{2},q_{3})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},q_{6},q_{5})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{5},q_{6},q_{4})

\displaystyle J_{\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\propto~

\displaystyle J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{6},q_{4},q_{5})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{5},q_{3},q_{6},q_{4},q_{2})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{3},q_{4},q_{6},q_{2},q_{5})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{6},q_{3},q_{5})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{5},q_{6},q_{4},q_{3})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{6},q_{2},q_{3})

\displaystyle J_{\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}\pi^{+}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\propto~

\displaystyle J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{5},q_{6},q_{4})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{3},q_{5},q_{6},q_{2})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{5},q_{6},q_{3})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{3},q_{6},q_{5},q_{4})

\displaystyle+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{4},q_{3},q_{6},q_{5},q_{2})+J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{2},q_{4},q_{6},q_{5},q_{3})

where
J_{a}^{\mu}
and
J_{b}^{\mu}
are hadronic subcurrents corresponding
to the decay type. A schematic of the
a
-type decay is given in

Fig.
31(a)

where the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
can decay through an
a_{1}

resonance. This resonance then decays into secondary
\rho
and

\omega
resonances. While the
\omega
resonance of the
a
-type
decay could be modelled further with a tertiary
\rho
resonance
decay, its decay is modelled as a contact interaction. A schematic of
the
b
-type decay is shown in
Fig.
31(b)

. Here, an
a_{1}

resonance decays into secondary
a_{1}
and
\sigma
resonances. This is
followed with the secondary
a_{1}
decaying into a tertiary
\rho

resonance.

((a))

((b))

Figure 31
:
Schematics of the resonance structure for

a
-type and

b
-type
decays of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
into a final state with five
pions.

The hadronic subcurrent for the
a
-decay resonance structure is given by,

\displaystyle J_{a}^{\mu}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},q_{5})=~

\displaystyle w_{\omega}BW(q_{\nu}q^{\nu},m_{a_{1}},\Gamma_{a_{1}})

(173)

\displaystyle BW((q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{\omega},\Gamma_{\omega})

\displaystyle BW((q_{4}+q_{5})_{\nu}(q_{4}+q_{5})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu}\left(q_{4}-q_{5},\epsilon(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}),q\right)\big(BW((q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})

\displaystyle+BW((q_{1}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})

\displaystyle+BW((q_{1}+q_{2})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{2})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})\big)

where
w_{\omega}
is a real weight,
BW
is the Breit-Wigner defined
by,

BW(s,m,\Gamma)=\frac{m^{2}}{m^{2}-s^{2}-im\Gamma}

(174)

and
\epsilon
is the permutation operator also used in

Eq.
138

for the three meson channel. Here,
q
is the
sum of all five momenta,
q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4}+q_{5}
. The parameters of the
resonances used in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
are given in

Table
3

.

resonance

m~[\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}]

\Gamma~[\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}]

w

0.4

0.776

0.15

11.5

0.0085

,

,

, and

resonances.

the

,
\rho
,
\sigma
, and
\omega
resonances.

The hadronic subcurrent for the
b
-decay resonance structure is given by,

\displaystyle J_{b}^{\mu}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4},q_{5})=~

\displaystyle w_{\sigma}BW(q_{\nu}q^{\nu},m_{a_{1}},\Gamma_{a_{1}})

(175)

\displaystyle BW((q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{a_{1}},\Gamma_{a_{1}})

\displaystyle BW((q_{4}+q_{5})_{\nu}(q_{4}+q_{5})^{\nu},m_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\sigma})

\displaystyle\left(\left(\frac{{J_{c}}_{\nu}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})q^{\nu}}{q_{\nu}q^{\nu}}\right)q^{\mu}-J_{c}^{\mu}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\right)

where the subcurrent
J_{c}^{\mu}
is,

\displaystyle J_{c}^{\mu}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=~

\displaystyle BW((q_{1}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})

(176)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{{q_{2}}_{\nu}(q_{1}-q_{3})^{\nu}}{(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu}}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{2})^{\mu}-q_{1}^{\mu}+q_{3}^{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle+BW((q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu},m_{\rho},\Gamma_{\rho})

\displaystyle\left(\frac{{q_{1}}_{\nu}(q_{2}-q_{3})^{\nu}}{(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})_{\nu}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{\nu}}(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{2})^{\mu}-q_{2}^{\mu}+q_{3}^{\mu}\right)

and the parameters are again given in

Table
3

. The definitions of
q
and
BW
are
the same as for
J_{a}^{\mu}
.

((b))

((c))

:

for

\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
,

\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{+}
, and

\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}\pi^{+}
decay channels of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
using the five pion
model.

:

for

,

, and

decay channels of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
using the five pion
model.

In
Fig.

, the
m_{234}
invariant mass distributions are
given for
\textsc{Pythia}~8
,
Herwig
++
, and
Tauola
. There is good
agreement between
\textsc{Pythia}~8
and
Herwig
++
for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
and
\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}\pi^{+}
channels, with
a slight disagreement for the
\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}

channel. However, the
Tauola
distributions do not agree well with the

\textsc{Pythia}~8
and
Herwig
++
distributions.

5

Implementation

One of the goals of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay implementation in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
is
that the production processes and decay channels of the
\tau
leptons are
easily extensible for new physics. Additionally, the machinery used to
generate helicity correlations for
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays may also be used for
helicity correlation in other processes such as
t
-quark decays in the
future. This section outlines the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay machinery architecture
used in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
as well as the additional software developed for
validation. The implementation of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays with full helicity
correlations is summarised in
Sect.
5.1

, while the
implementation of the helicity matrix elements used in the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays
is introduced in
Sect.
5.2

and the validation procedure is
outlined in
Sect.
5.3

. A summary of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
production
mechanisms presented in
Sect.
3

and implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8

is given in
Table
5

, while a summary of the default
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays introduced in
Sect.
4

and implemented in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
is
given in
Table
5

.

\captionabove

\textsc{Pythia}~8

decays are
calculated.

5.1

Tau Decays

The code for
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays is provided in the four files (headers and
source),
ParticleDecays
,
TauDecays
,
HelicityBasics
, and
HelicityMatrixElements
of the

\textsc{Pythia}~8
source code. When a particle decay is requested by

\textsc{Pythia}~8
the following program flow occurs, where the first step
occurs within
ParticleDecays::decay
and all remaining steps
occur within
TauDecays::decay
.

1.

The particle is passed to
ParticleDecays::decay
.

2.

If the particle is a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
, the decay is passed to
TauDecays::decay
.

3.

The hard process is determined.

(a)

The correlated
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is found (if it exists).

(b)

The incoming and outgoing particles are set.

(c)

The helicity matrix element,
\mathcal{M}
, from

HelicityMatrixElements
is set.

4.

The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is selected (if correlated, randomly selected from the two

\tau
leptons).

5.

The helicity density matrix,
\rho
, is calculated by
HelicityMatrixElement::calculateRho
using

Eq.
108

.

6.

The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
children are created by
TauDecays::createChildren
.

(a)

The decay channel is selected.

(b)

The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
children are created.

(c)

The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay matrix element is set from
HelicityMatrixElements
.

7.

The children momenta,
q_{i}
, are assigned isotropically until
the condition
x\in\mathcal{U}(0,1)\geq\frac{\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{max}}}
is met.

(a)

The variable
x
is a random number from a uniform distribution,

\mathcal{U}(0,1)
.

(b)

The decay weight
\mathcal{W}
is calculated by
HelicityMatrixElements::decayWeight
using

Eq.
109

.

(c)

The maximum weight
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{max}}
is empirically or
analytically known.

8.

TauDecays::writeEvent
writes the decay to the event
record.

(a)

If there is no correlated
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
,
TauDecays::decay
returns to

ParticleDecays::decay
.

9.

If the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is correlated, the decay matrix,
D
, of the decayed

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is
calculated by
HelicityMatrixElements::calculateD
using

Eq.
111

.

10.

The second correlated
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is selected, and
steps
5
through

8
are repeated for the second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
.

11.

TauDecays::decay
returns to
ParticleDecays::decay

which returns to the main algorithm of
\textsc{Pythia}~8
.

\captionabove

A list of the methods implemented in the
TauDecays
class.

method

description

decay

this is the main method called by
ParticleDecays

and performs the correlated decays of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}

createChildren

selects the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay channel, assigns the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
helicity matrix element, and returns a
vector of
HelicityParticle
children with
q_{i}=0

isotropicDecay

takes the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
children and reassigns their
momenta using isotropic phase-space

writeEvent

writes the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
children to the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
event
record

Table
5.1

outlines the methods
implemented within the
TauDecays
class and provides a brief
description of each method. The process of decaying a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is very
similar to the decay of a standard particle except for the use of more
sophisticated helicity matrix elements and helicity correlations. As
such, the
TauDecays::createChildren
method is very similar to
inline code within the
ParticleDecays
class and
TauDecays::isotropicDecay
reimplements
ParticleDecays::mGenerator
, the
m
-generator algorithm
introduced in
Sect.
2.3

.

5.2

Matrix Elements

Within the
HelicityBasics
source files, three major classes
are defined:
Wave4
,
GammaMatrix
, and
HelicityParticle
. The class
Wave4
is intended to store
four-momenta and spinors and is just a complex four-vector with
standard vector operations defined: vector addition/subtraction,
vector multiplication, and scalar
multiplication/division. Additionally, the operator
Wave4::(i)

is defined, where
i
must be in the range
0\leq{\tt i}\leq 3
, and allows access to the corresponding element of the four-vector,
i.e.
q(0)
returns the energy of the momentum
q
.

The
GammaMatrix
class is intended to be used in conjunction with
the
Wave4
class such that helicity matrix elements can be easily
translated from analytic expressions to code. For example, the
helicity matrix element for
{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\pi^{-}
,

\mathcal{M}=\bar{u}_{1}\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})u_{0}q_{2}^{\mu}

(177)

can be written in pseudo-code as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\sum_{\mu}~

\displaystyle{\tt Wave4(}\bar{u}_{1}{\tt)*GammaMatrix(}\mu{\tt)*(1-GammaMatrix(5))}

(178)

\displaystyle{\tt*Wave4(}u_{0}{\tt)}{\tt*GammaMatrix(4)(}\mu{\tt,}\mu{\tt)*Wave4(}q_{2}{\tt)(}\mu{\tt)}

in the
HelicityMatrixElements
source code. Again, the operator

GammaMatrix::(i,j)
can be used to access the Dirac matrix
element of row
i
and column
j
where
0\leq{\tt i,j}\leq 3
. The constructor
GammaMatrix(i)
returns
\gamma^{\tt i}
.

The Dirac matrices are defined by
Eq.
32

, using the
representation of
Sect.
1.2

, and
\gamma^{4}
is defined as

g_{\mu\nu}
. Because carrying out the full matrix multiplication of

Eq.
178

is very time consuming, the special sparse
properties of the Dirac matrices are exploited, as well as left to
right order of operations. Accordingly, the
GammaMatrix
class is
represented by four ordered
values
where the first value is the
non-zero element of the first row, the second value is the non-zero
element of the second row, the third is the non-zero element of the
third row, and the fourth is the non-zero element of the fourth
row. Corresponding to each of the
values
is an
index
which
provides the column index for the non-zero value. The left to right
multiplication
w={\tt Wave4*GammaMatrix}
is then given by,

w^{\mu}={\tt Wave4(GammaMatrix.index[}\mu{\tt])*GammaMatrix.values[}\mu{\tt]}

(179)

which is four multiplication and four assignment operations, rather
than the sixteen multiplication, sixteen addition, and four assignment
operations required for full matrix multiplication.

In
Eq.
178

, the term
1 - GammaMatrix(5)
is included,
where the nonsensical subtraction of a matrix from a scalar is
performed. This is because the addition and subtraction of scalars
with the
GammaMatrix
class is defined as the addition or
subtraction of the scalar applied to the non-zero elements of the
Dirac matrix. Because
\gamma^{5}
is on-diagonal in the representation
used,
1 - GammaMatrix(5)
is just the subtraction of
\gamma^{5}

from the identity matrix.

The final class defined in
HelicityBasics
,
HelicityParticle
, takes the standard
Particle
class of

\textsc{Pythia}~8
and extends the class to include a helicity density matrix,

\rho
, and a decay matrix,
D
. Additionally, the method
HelicityParticle::wave(h)
is defined which returns the
Wave4
spinor or polarisation vector for the particle with helicity

h
.

\captionabove

A list of the public methods implemented in the
HelicityMatrixElement

class.

method

description

initPointers

initialise the pointers to the
\textsc{Pythia}~8

\mathrm{SM}
and

\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings database and particle properties database

initChannel

takes as an argument a vector of
HelicityParticle
which are used to initialise any constants
used in the matrix element

decayWeight

takes a vector of
HelicityParticle
and
calculates the decay weight
\mathcal{W}
for the matrix element

calculateME

calculates the helicity matrix element

calculateRho

calculates the helicity density matrix
\mathcal{M}
for one of
the
HelicityParticles
being used in the matrix element

calculateD

calculates the decay matrix
D
for one of the
HelicityParticles
being used in the matrix element

setFermionLine

determines the order to assign a fermion line
in the matrix element based on direction of
particle or anti-particle

xBreitWigner

the Breit-Wigners of
Eqs.
126

through

129
where
x
is fixed,
s
,
p
, or

d

The actual helicity matrix elements for both the hard processes and

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays are defined in the
HelicityMatrixElements
files
which use the classes outlined above from
HelicityBasics
. The
important methods of the
HelicityMatrixElement
class are
outlined in
Table
5.2

with brief descriptions
provided for each method. The hard processes are written as classes
that derive directly from the
HelicityMatrixElement
class and
utilise pointer polymorphism within C
++
.

The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay matrix elements are written as classes that derive from
the
HMETauDecay
class which itself derives from the
HelicityMatrixElement
class. The
HMETauDecay
class is very
similar to the
HelicityMatrixElement
class except that the
additional method
HMETauDecay::initHadronicCurrent

has been implemented which allows the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay matrix element to be
calculated using the general form of Equation
Eq.
124

, so that
for a new
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay only the hadronic current needs to be defined when
implementing the matrix element.

5.3

Validation

Because the matrix elements defined in
Sects.
3

and

4
can be complex, it is important to validate

\textsc{Pythia}~8
against the other event generators which provide similar
features for
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays. In order to facilitate validation, a series
of tools were written in addition to the code implemented in

\textsc{Pythia}~8
and are available by request.

Within the validation package are three sets of tools: a generation
tool, an analysis tool, and a plotting tool. The generation tool
provides a common
Root

n
-tuple output for events generated
with
\textsc{Pythia}~8
,
Herwig
++
, and
\textsc{Pythia}~6
with
Tauola
. The
analysis package runs over the generated
n
-tuples and applies a
common analysis to the events from the three generators. The
histograms from the analysis tool can then be plotted using the
plotting tool. The tools are written in C
++
and interfaced with
Bash
scripts. For event generation, the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
,
Herwig
++
,

ThePEG
,
\textsc{Pythia}~6
, and
Tauola
libraries are required as well
as
Root
libraries. The analysis and plotting tools require only

Root
libraries.

A timing tool is provided within the validation package as a method to
compare the average time per event required by the various generators
to ensure that no significant timing issues arise. Runs of various
sizes, i.e.
2000
,
6000
, and
12000
events, are performed
for each decay channel with each generator. The results are aggregated
by the timing tool and a linear fit is performed for each decay type
with each generator. The slope from the linear fit is taken as the
average time per event with associated uncertainty, while the
intercept of the fit is taken as the average initialisation time of
the generator.

Figure 33
:
A comparison of the average time per
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
between the
\textsc{Pythia}~8
,
Herwig
++
, and
Tauola
event generators
for a selection of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays of

Sect.
4

.

In
Fig.
33

the average time per event for a selection of the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay channels of
Sect.
4

is given. In these tests

\textsc{Pythia}~8
outperforms
Herwig
++
and
Tauola
for all of the
implemented
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay channels by a factor of
\approx 5
except for
the
{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}\pi^{-}\pi^{+}
channel. The decrease in speed
for this channel is due to the sharp resonance from the
\omega
which
is not present in the
\tau^{-}\rightarrow\nu_{\tau}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{0}\pi^{-}
channel. This sharp resonance is not easily sampled using the

m
-generator phase-space algorithm and leads to inefficiencies for
this channel. The timing information given in
Fig.
33

is
dependent upon a variety of factors other than just the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
algorithms used, including compile time options, machine architecture,
and most importantly, the configuration used for event generation with
each generator. Consequently, the results of
Fig.
33

provide a
general sense for the timing of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay implementation in

\textsc{Pythia}~8
, but will vary from system to system.

Chapter 4

Experimental Setup

The data used in the analyses of
Chap.
5

and
Chap.
6

were
collected using the Large Hadron Collider Beauty detector (LHCb) on
the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) at the European Organisation for
Nuclear Research (CERN). CERN was founded in 1954 and is situated on
the French-Swiss border near the city of Geneva,
Switzerland. Currently, CERN is run by
20
European member states
with
7
observer states and organisations, and
37
participating
non-member states. Nearly
10,000
visiting scientists from over
600

universities and institutes and
113
countries utilise the research
facilities at CERN. Fundamental advances in particle physics have been
made throughout the years at CERN including the first observation of
the
W

[
ua1.83.1
,
ua2.83.1
]
and
Z
bosons
[
ua1.83.2
,
ua2.83.2
]
by
the UA
1
and UA
2
detectors on the Super Proton Synchrotron (SPS),
precision electroweak measurements
[
lep.06.1
]
by the detectors on
the Large Electron-Positron collider (LEP), the creation of
anti-hydrogen
[
ps210.96.1
]
, and most recently the discovery of a
Higgs-like boson
[
atlas.12.2
,
cms.13.1
]
with the ATLAS and CMS detectors on the LHC. Within this chapter the collider is introduced
in
Sect.
6

, the LHCb detector is described in
Sect.
7

,
and the the methods used for reconstructing events observed within the
LHCb detector are outlined in
Sect.
8

.

6

Large Hadron Collider

The LHC accelerates protons in opposite directions around a

27~\mathrm{km}
ring, colliding them at four interaction points
around which four detectors are built. Schematics in the vertical
plane, defined by the beamline and a vector perpendicular to the LHC ring, for the general purpose ATLAS and CMS detectors are given in

Figs.
34(a)

and
34(b)
. These detectors are fully
instrumented with tracking systems, calorimeters, and muon
chambers. The LHCb detector, with a schematic shown in

Fig.
34(c)

, is designed specifically for forward physics, in
particular the physics of
B
-hadrons, and is a forward arm
spectrometer, extending outwards on only one side of the interaction
point. The data used in the analyses of
Chaps.
5

and
6

were taken with this detector. Further details on the LHCb detector
and LHCb event reconstruction are given in
Sects.
7

and

8
. The ALICE detector is a heavy ion detector, with
its schematic shown in
Fig.
34(d)

. During nominal LHC operations
the LHCb and ALICE detectors receive reduced luminosities with
respect to the ATLAS and CMS detectors.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

Figure 34
:
Rough schematics of the
ATLAS,

CMS,
LHCb, and
ALICE detectors of the LHC. Here,
\mathrm{ECAL}
are electromagnetic
calorimeters,
\mathrm{HCAL}
are hadronic calorimeters, TRD are transition
radiation detectors, TOF are time of flight detectors, and TPC are
time projection chambers. These schematics were modified from

Refs.
[
atlas.08.1
]

,
[
cms.08.1
]
,
[
lhcb.08.1
]
,
and
[
alice.08.1
]
.

The LHC began operation in September of
2008
, but nine days after
the inaugural start-up, a fault of the busbars in the interconnects
between a dipole and quadrupole magnet system caused a magnet quench
and delayed operations of the LHC until November of

2009

[
lhc.09.1
]
. Full-scale data-taking began in March of

2010
at a centre-of-mass energy of
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
,
3.5~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
per beam,
and continued until November of
2010
when lead ion beams were
circulated for a month. In March of
2011
proton-proton collisions
were again begun at a centre-of-mass energy of
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
and continued
until November of
2011
. A plot of the integrated luminosity over
time for the ATLAS, CMS, and LHCb detectors during
2011

data-taking is given in
Fig.
35(a)

. In April of
2012
,
proton-proton data-taking at a centre-of-mass energy of
8~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}

began, and continued until February of
2013
. The
2012
integrated
luminosity is plotted in
Fig.
35(b)

. Currently, the LHC has entered a long shutdown until sometime in
2014
after which
operation will recommence at higher luminosities and with a
centre-of-mass energy of
14~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}

[
heuer.12.1
]
. The data used in

Chaps.
5

and
6
were collected during the
2011

data-taking operations.

((a))

((b))

Figure 35
:
Integrated luminosity as a function of time
provided by the LHC to the LHCb, ATLAS, and CMS detectors for
the

2011
data-taking at
{\sqrt{s}=7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and

2012
data-taking at

{\sqrt{s}=8~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The integrated luminosity data are taken
from the
LHC statistics
web-page
.

The technical design reports for the LHC can be found in the three
volumes of
Refs.
[
lhc.04.1
]

,
[
lhc.04.2
]
, and
[
lhc.04.3
]
,
corresponding to the main ring, infrastructure and general services,
and injector chain for the LHC. An abridged and updated version of
the technical design reports can be found in
Ref.
[
lhc.08.2
]

. In the
remainder of this section the layout of the machine is outlined in

Sect.
6.1

and the injector chain is described in
Sect.
6.2

.

6.1

Layout

The layout of the main LHC ring is summarised in the schematic of

Fig.
36

. The ring consists of eight long straight sections (LSS)
each with a length of approximately
{528~\mathrm{m}}
alternating
with eight arcs (ARC) each with a distance of approximately

{2.8~\mathrm{km}}
. The ring is also divided into octants, with each
octant centred about an LSS, and each ARC divided between two
octants. The collider ring is located between
{45~\mathrm{m}}
to

{170~\mathrm{m}}
below the surface, with an access point provided at
every LSS. Each LSS is used for beam utilities or experiments, and the
ARC segments contain the dipoles needed to bend the beam and
quadrupoles used to focus the beam. Between each ARC and LSS is
located a dispersion suppressor (DS) which is used to adapt the LHC reference orbit to the tunnel geometry. Additionally, the dispersion
suppressors are used to match the ARC optics with the insertion optics
for each LSS, as well as cancel horizontal dispersion from the dipole
magnets.

Figure 36
:
Schematic of the main LHC ring and the injector
chain as seen from above. The first proton beam (red) rotates
clockwise while the second proton beam (blue) rotates
anti-clockwise.

The beams cross in the LSSs of the first, second, fifth, and eighth
octants. The high luminosity ATLAS and CMS experiments are located
at points
1
and
5
respectively. The plane of the beam crossing
angle at point
1
is vertical while it is horizontal at point

5
. Point
2
houses the lower luminosity ALICE experiment and point

8
contains the LHCb experiment. Beam
1
, rotating clockwise from
above, is injected at point
2
while beam
2
, rotating
anti-clockwise from above, is injected at point
8
.

In the LSS of the third octant, momentum cleaning of the beams is
performed while in the LSS of the seventh octant, optical cleaning of
the beam is performed. The radio frequency (RF) cavities for the main
ring are installed in the LSS of the fourth octant and contained in
the old cavern used to house the ALEPH detector on the LEP collider. The LSS of the sixth octant contains the two independent
beam dump systems for both beams. These dumps abort the beams by using
kicker magnets to horizontally bump the beams into septum magnets
which then deflect the beams vertically into absorbers within
dedicated tunnels.

6.2

Injection Chain

The injection chain for the LHC is also shown in
Fig.
36

, and
begins with LINAC
2
, an Alvarez linear accelerator, where a plasma
is created from an ionised gas within a duoplasmatron running at

{90~\mathrm{kV}}
. The plasma is formed into an ion beam within a

{750~\mathrm{keV}}
RF quadrupole which then passes the beam into
three Alvarez tanks, consisting of in-phase drift tubes, which
accelerate the beam up to an energy of
{50~\mathrm{Me\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. For nominal LHC operation, the LINAC
2
must output a proton beam with a current of

{180~\mathrm{mA}}
and pulse lengths of
{150~\mu\mathrm{s}}
at a
rate of
{0.8~\mathrm{Hz}}
into the Proton Synchrotron Booster
(PSB)
[
hill.00.1
]
. The PSB consists of four rings each with a
radius of
{25~\mathrm{m}}

[
reich.69.1
]
. One proton bunch is
injected into each of the rings and the four bunches are then
accelerated to an energy of
{1.4~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. These bunches are then
extracted into the Proton Synchrotron (PS). The PS has a radius of

100~\mathrm{m}
and holds
84
proton bunches with a bunch spacing of

{25~\mu\mathrm{s}}
. These bunches are accelerated to an energy of

{26~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
after which their bunch length is reduced to

{4~\mathrm{ns}}
for insertion into the
{200~\mathrm{MHz}}
RF
cavities of the Super Proton Synchrotron
[
benedikt.00.1
]
. The
SPS, with a radius of
{1.1~\mathrm{km}}
accelerates the beam to

{250~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
using two
{200~\mathrm{MHz}}
RF cavities and injects
the beams into the main LHC ring at either point
2
or

8

[
schindl.99.1
]
.

7

LHC Beauty Detector

The Large Hadron Collider Beauty experiment (LHCb) is a forward arm
spectrometer located in the experimental cavern of point
8
on the
LHC ring. The detector is primarily designed for the identification
of
B
-hadrons in order to further explore their decays and make
precise measurements of
\delta
, the
\mathcal{CP}
-violating phase angle of the
CKM matrix from the unified electroweak Lagrangian of
Sect.
1.2

,
as well as to search for new physics through the deviation of rare

B
-hadron decays from their
\mathrm{SM}
predictions, e.g.
{B_{s}\to\mu\mu}

[
lhcb.13.4
]
. The production of
b
-quark pairs within the
LHC environment, which then hadronise into jets of
B
-hadrons, is
typically close to the direction of either beam, with both
b
-quarks being
produced in the same direction. Consequently, LHCb is designed as a
forward arm spectrometer about the beamline in order to maximise the
acceptance of
B
-hadrons while minimising construction and material
costs. The reconstruction and identification of
B
-hadrons within the
hadronic environment of the LHC requires excellent secondary vertex
reconstruction, good momentum resolution, a fast and robust trigger,
and hadron identification capabilities.

Figure 37
:
Schematic of the LHCb detector in the
longitudinal
yz
-plane.

A detailed schematic of the LHCb detector and its sub-components in
the longitudinal
yz
-plane is given in
Fig.
37

. The origin
of the LHCb coordinate system is defined as the nominal interaction
point within LHCb. The
x
-axis points outward from the centre of the
LHC ring and is parallel to the plane of the LHC ring. The
z
-axis
points along the beam in the direction of the detector, resulting in
an angle of
{\approx 3.6~\mathrm{mrad}}
with the floor of the LHCb cavern. The
y
-axis points upward and is perpendicular with both the

x
-axis and
z
-axis to form a right-handed Cartesian coordinate
system. The azimuthal angle
\phi
is defined in the transverse

xy
-plane with a
\phi
of zero along the direction of the
x
-axis
and ranges between values of
-\pi
and
\pi
. The polar angle

\theta
is defined as the opening angle with the
z
-axis such that a
particle along the beamline has a
\theta
of zero. The
pseudo-rapidity
\eta
and rapidity
y
are oftentimes used rather
than
\theta
and are defined as,

\eta\equiv\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\left|\vec{p}\right|+p_{z}}{\left|\vec{p}\right|-p_{z}}\right)=-\ln\left(\tan\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right),\quad\quad y=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}\right)

(180)

where
p_{z}
is the
z
-component of the particle momentum. For
massless particles the pseudo-rapidity and rapidity are
equivalent. The transverse momentum of a particle,
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, is also
commonly used and is defined as
\sqrt{p_{x}^{2}+p_{y}^{2}}
.

Surrounding the interaction point in
Fig.
37

is a vertex
locator (
\mathrm{VELO}
) which is followed, in the positive
z
-direction, by a
ring imaging Cherenkov detector (
\mathrm{RICH}1
) and a tracker turicensis
(
\mathrm{TT}
). After the
\mathrm{TT}
is a large dipole magnet which provides a
bending field for the tracking systems. The remainder of the tracking
system follows the magnet, consisting of an inner tracker (
\mathrm{IT}
) about
the beamline and an outer tracker (
\mathrm{OT}
). This part of the tracking
system is separated into three tracking stations, T
1
, T
2
, and
T
3
. Another ring imaging Cherenkov detector (
\mathrm{RICH}2
) is located
after the
\mathrm{IT}
and
\mathrm{OT}
, which is larger than
\mathrm{RICH}1
. After
\mathrm{RICH}2

is the first station M
1
of the muon system, followed by a
scintillating pad detector (
SPD
) and pre-shower calorimeter (
\mathrm{PRS}
),
an electromagnetic calorimeter (
\mathrm{ECAL}
), and a hadronic calorimeter
(
\mathrm{HCAL}
). The remainder of the detector consists of four muon tracking
stations M
2
through M
5
. In the remainder of this section a
description of the tracking system is given in
Sect.
7.1

followed
by an overview of the particle identification systems in

Sect.
7.2

. A full description of the detector can be found in

Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

on which this section is based.

7.1

Tracking

The tracking system for LHCb consists of the
\mathrm{VELO}
,
\mathrm{TT}
, and
\mathrm{IT}
which are silicon microstrip detectors, and the
\mathrm{OT}
which is a
straw-tube detector. The
\mathrm{TT}
and
\mathrm{IT}
were developed under the
combined silicon tracker (
\mathrm{ST}
) project and utilise similar hardware
designs. Both the silicon microstrip and straw-tube technologies
record the passage of charged particles, and when combined with a
magnetic field, the trajectories of the charged particles built from
their hits in the detectors are used to determine the momenta of the
particles. The passage of a charged particle through a silicon
detector ionises atoms, creating a current which is detected, while in
straw-tube detectors the particle ionises the gas within the tube
which then avalanches and creates a detected current. Both silicon
microstrips and straw-tubes are combined in layers to provide a
three-dimensional hit coordinate. During
2011
data taking the
combined tracking system provided a momentum resolution,
\delta_{p}/p
,
for charged particles between
Phys. Rev. C
0.4 - 0.6
[
tobin.12.1
]
.

Figure 38
:
Magnet-down
y
-component of the magnetic
field within the LHCb detector along the
z
-axis, beginning at
the
\mathrm{VELO}
and moving outwards to the
\mathrm{RICH}2
detector. The
uncertainty on the precision of the measurements is less than

{10^{-4}~\mathrm{T}}
. The approximate
z
-axis locations of the
subdetectors are indicated by the shaded areas. The magnetic field
data is taken from
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

.

Figure 38
:
Magnet-down
y
-component of the magnetic
field within the LHCb detector along the
z
-axis, beginning at
the
\mathrm{VELO}
and moving outwards to the
\mathrm{RICH}2
detector. The
uncertainty on the precision of the measurements is less than

{10^{-4}~\mathrm{T}}
. The approximate
z
-axis locations of the
subdetectors are indicated by the shaded areas. The magnetic field
data is taken from
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

.

A large dipole magnet located between the
\mathrm{TT}
and
\mathrm{OT}
provides the
bending field for the tracking system. The dipole is a warm magnet
consisting of two saddle-shaped coils, and is designed to meet the
requirements of the tracking system while fitting within the space of
the experimental cavern and minimising costs. The bending plane of the
magnet is in the
xz
-plane of the detector, and is designed to
provide a magnetic field of less than
{50~\mathrm{mT}}
within the

\mathrm{RICH}
systems and a maximum magnetic field between the
\mathrm{TT}
and
\mathrm{OT}
with an integrated magnetic field of
{4~\mathrm{Tm}}
for a

{10~\mathrm{m}}
track. The dipole can be run with a magnet-down or
magnet-up configuration, corresponding to the direction of the

y
-component of the magnetic field. Both field configurations have
been mapped between the
\mathrm{VELO}
and
\mathrm{RICH}2
using an array of Hall
probes within a precision of better than
{10^{-4}~\mathrm{T}}
. A
report on the field map for the magnet taken during
2011
is
available in
Ref.
[
lhcb.12.4
]

, with the
y
-component of the magnetic
field along the
z
-axis from
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

given in

Fig.

. The initial technical design report for the magnet
system can be found in
Ref.
[
lhcb.07.1
]

.

7.1.1

Vertex Locator

The vertex locator is a microstrip detector located about the LHCb interaction point and is designed for high resolution reconstruction
of secondary vertices from long-lived particles such as

B
-hadrons. The
\mathrm{VELO}
is designed to have a pseudo-rapidity coverage
of
{1.6\leq\eta\leq 4.9}
for particles produced from vertices
within
{10.7~\mathrm{cm}}
of the interaction point. A schematic of
the
\mathrm{VELO}
in the
xz
-plane is given in
Fig.
39

, consisting of

42
semi-circular detector modules and
4
pile-up veto sensors. The
modules have a diameter of
{9~\mathrm{cm}}
and during stable beam
conditions the active areas of the modules are located

{8~\mathrm{mm}}
from the centre of the beamline. Because the
transverse beam width during injection of the LHC beams is larger
than this separation, the
\mathrm{VELO}
modules are retracted during beam
injection so that the active areas of the module sensors are

{29~\mathrm{mm}}
from the centre of the beamline.

Figure 39
:
Schematic of the
\mathrm{VELO}
detector in the

xz
-plane. Each of the
42
modules consists of an
r
-sensor
(red) and a
\phi
-sensor (blue). The leftmost
r
-sensors are
pile-up veto sensors. Figure adapted from

Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

.

Each module consists of a back-to-back radial
r
-sensor and azimuthal

\phi
-sensor, where the
z
-coordinate of each module in conjunction
with the
r
and
\phi
-measurements provide a full three-dimensional
location for each hit in the module. The
r
-sensors are made up of
quadrants, with each quadrant containing
512
strips, while the

\phi
-sensors consist of
683
inner strips and
1365
outer strips;
the total number of strips per sensor for both sensor types is

2048
. The strip pitch of the
r
-sensors increases linearly from

{40~\mu\mathrm{m}}
to
{102~\mu\mathrm{m}}
and the strip pitch for
the
\phi
-sensors range between
{38~\mu\mathrm{m}}
and

{97~\mu\mathrm{m}}
. The use of a cylindrical geometry for the
\mathrm{VELO}
sensors, rather than a more typical rectilinear geometry, allows fast
reconstruction of track impact parameters with sufficient resolution
using information from the
r
-sensors, as is done by the high level
trigger.

The pitches of the
r
and
\phi
-sensors were chosen to provide a
single hit resolution of approximately
4~\mathrm{\mu}m
for a track
with a pseudo-rapidity of
3
passing through the inner

\phi
-strips. The impact parameter significance used in
Sect.
9.2

to separate
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays from prompt backgrounds primarily depends upon
the precision which the
\mathrm{VELO}
can reconstruct track positions close to
the interaction region (impact parameter
resolution). The impact parameter resolution is
given by,

\delta_{\mathrm{IP}}=\delta_{\mathrm{HIT}}\oplus\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{MSE}}}{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}

(181)

where
\delta_{\mathrm{HIT}}
is the uncertainty due to the intrinsic
precision of the track hits,
\delta_{\mathrm{MSE}}
is the uncertainty
due to multiple scattering effects, and
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
is the transverse momentum
of the particle. From
2010
data,
\delta_{\mathrm{HIT}}
was found to
be
{13.1~\mathrm{mm}}
for the
x
-component and
{12.1~\mathrm{mm}}

for the
y
-component, while
\delta_{\mathrm{MSE}}
was found to be

{23.9~\mathrm{mm}\,\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and
{23.7~\mathrm{mm}\,\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for the
x

and
y
-components of the impact parameter
[
farry.12.1
]
.

7.1.2

Tracker Turicensis

The tracker turicensis is a microstrip silicon detector designed to be
used in conjunction with the
\mathrm{VELO}
,
\mathrm{IT}
, and
\mathrm{OT}
to measure the
momenta of charged particles. This sub-detector begins approximately

{2.3~\mathrm{m}}
down the
z
-axis, ends at approximately

{2.8~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis, and covers a pseudo-rapidity
range of
{2.0\leq\eta\leq 5.0}
in the
yz
-plane and
{1.9\leq\eta\leq 4.9}
in the
xz
-plane for particles produced from the
interaction point. The
\mathrm{TT}
consists of four layers, with the first
two layers grouped into a single station and separated from the second
station of the final two layers by a distance of
{0.3~\mathrm{m}}
. A
schematic of the first layer in the
xy
-plane is given in

Fig.
40

, which includes
16
strip modules with
8
modules above
the
xz
-plane and
8
modules below. The second
\mathrm{TT}
layer is similar
to the first, but the modules are rotated by an angle of
+5^{\circ}

with respect to the
yz
-plane. The third layer has an additional
4

modules,
2
above and
2
below the
xz
-plane, and is rotated by an
angle of
-5^{\circ}
, while the fourth and final layer also has an
additional
4
modules, but is not rotated.

Figure 40
:
Schematic of the first layer of the
\mathrm{TT}
detector in the
xy
-plane. The layer consists of
16
strip
modules, each with
7
sensors and
2
or
3
read out
hybrids. Adapted from
Ref.
[
lhcb.03.1
]

.

Every strip module contains
7
silicon sensors, each with
512

strips aligned vertically along the length of the module, where the
pitch for each strip is
183~\mu\mathrm{m}
. This vertical alignment
provides maximum resolution in the bending plane of the dipole
magnet. The sensors are divided into three read out sectors,
L
,
M
,
and
K
. The
4
outermost silicon sensors for each module read out to
the
L
-sector, while the
3
innermost sensors read out to the

M
-sector. Modules with high occupancy sensors adjacent to the
beamline contain an
M
-sector subdivided into an additional

K
-sector, with the two outermost sensors reading out to the

M
-sector, and the innermost sensor adjacent to the beamline reading
out to the
K
-sector.

This design of the
\mathrm{TT}
system provides a minimum single hit
resolution of
{59~\mu\mathrm{m}}
as found from
data
[
tobin.12.1
]
. The
\mathrm{TT}
in combination with the muon system
is used to determine muon track finding efficiencies from data in

Sect.
10.1

. Further details on the
\mathrm{TT}
can be found in the
technical design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.03.2
]

.

7.1.3

Inner Tracker

The inner tracker uses the same silicon microstrip sensors as the
\mathrm{TT}
and provides high pseudo-rapidity tracking coverage along the beam
line complimentary to the
\mathrm{OT}
. There are three
\mathrm{IT}
stations located
at approximately
{7.7~\mathrm{m}}
,
{8.4~\mathrm{m}}
, and

{9.0~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis corresponding to the combined

\mathrm{IT}
and
\mathrm{OT}
tracking stations T
1
, T
2
, and T
3
of

Fig.
37

. The three stations cover a common pseudo-rapidity
range for a track produced at the interaction point of
{4.5\leq\eta\leq 4.9}
in the
yz
-plane and
{3.4\leq\eta\leq 5.0}
in the

xz
-plane. Despite the small acceptance of the
\mathrm{IT}
, nearly
20\%
of
all tracks produced within LHCb pass through the
\mathrm{IT}
. Every
\mathrm{IT}
station contains four layers, similar to the four layers of the

\mathrm{TT}
. Each layer consists of
28
modules in a configuration similar
to the schematic of
Fig.
41

. The strip modules of the first and
fourth layers are vertical like that of
Fig.
41

, while the second
layer is rotated by
+5^{\circ}
with respect to the
yz
-plane and the
third layer is rotated by
-5^{\circ}
. This rotation configuration is
the same as for the
\mathrm{TT}
layers and is designed to maximise the
\mathrm{IT}
resolution in the bending plane of the dipole.

Figure 41
:
Schematic of the first and fourth layer of
the
\mathrm{IT}
detector in the
xy
-plane. The layer consists of
28

strip modules, each with
1
or
2
sensors and
1
or
2
read
out hybrids. Adapted from
Ref.
[
lhcb.02.1
]

.

The
14
outermost strip modules contain two silicon sensors each,
with a dedicated read out hybrid for every sensor, while the
14

innermost strip modules only contain one sensor each. These sensors
use the same technology as the
\mathrm{TT}
sensors and contain
384

vertically aligned strips, each with a pitch of

{198~\mu\mathrm{m}}
. The
\mathrm{IT}
provides a single hit resolution of

{50~\mu\mathrm{m}}
as found from data
[
tobin.12.1
]
, and is
critical for the reconstruction of the charged particle tracks used in
the analysis of
Chap.
5

. Further details on the
\mathrm{IT}
can be found in
the technical design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.02.1
]

.

7.1.4

Outer Tracker

The outer tracker is a large straw-tube detector which provides lower
pseudo-rapidity coverage about the higher pseudo-rapidity
\mathrm{IT}
stations. There are three
\mathrm{OT}
stations which are contained in the
combined
\mathrm{IT}
and
\mathrm{OT}
stations T
1
, T
2
, and T
3
. The three
\mathrm{OT}
stations are located at
{7.8~\mathrm{m}}
,
{8.5~\mathrm{m}}
, and

{9.2~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis and cover a common
pseudo-rapidity range of
{2.0\leq\eta\leq 4.5}
in the
yz
-plane
and
{1.8\leq\eta\leq 3.4}
in the
xz
-plane. Each
\mathrm{OT}
station
consists of four layers, where the same rotation scheme for the
\mathrm{TT}
and
\mathrm{IT}
is used; the first and fourth layers are vertical, the second
layer is rotated by
+5^{\circ}
with respect to the
yz
-plane, and the
third layer is rotated by
-5^{\circ}
. A schematic of a vertical
\mathrm{OT}
layer is given in
Fig.
42

, where each layer contains
14
long

F
-modules and
8
short
S
-modules.

Figure 42
:
Schematic of the first and fourth layer of
the
\mathrm{OT}
detector in the
xy
-plane. The layer consists of
14

F
-modules and
8

S
-modules.

Every
F
-module contains
256
straw-tubes, with the tubes divided
into two monolayers in the
xy
-plane, each containing
128

tubes. The monolayers are divided horizontally in half, with each half
containing
64
straw-tubes. Each layer half reads out at the
outermost edge of the module. The
S
-modules also consist of two
straw-tube monolayers, but these monolayers are not divided in
half. Each layer contains
128
tubes, and so every
S
-modules
contains
256
straw-tubes. Again, each layer reads out to the
outermost edge of the module. The straw-tubes contain a mixture of

30\%
argon and
20\%
carbon dioxide, and with an inner diameter of

4.9~\mathrm{mm}
have a drift time of less than

{50~\mathrm{ns}}
. The single hit resolution of the
\mathrm{OT}
is

{220~\mathrm{\mu}\mathrm{m}}

[
storaci.11.1
]
. Hits from the
\mathrm{OT}
are used in the reconstruction of the tracks used in the analysis of

Chap.
5

. Further details on the
\mathrm{OT}
can be found in the technical
design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.01.2
]

.

7.2

Particle Identification

The particle identification subdetectors of LHCb consist of two ring
imaging Cherenkov radiation (
\mathrm{RICH}
) detectors, four calorimeters,
and a muon system. The
\mathrm{RICH}
detectors use Cherenkov radiation from
charged particles to determine the velocity of particles, and in
conjunction with momentum information from the tracking system, are
used to calculate the mass of charged particles. Information from the

\mathrm{RICH}
detectors is used to differentiate between charged pions and
kaons from
B
-hadron decays. The energy of charged or neutral
particles are determined with the four calorimeters. Here, the
particles interact with scintillating material, producing particle
showers resulting in photons that are measured by photodetectors; the
magnitude and intensity of the photons from the particle shower
correspond to the energy of the particle passing through the
calorimeter. The calorimeters are used to differentiate between
hadrons and leptons, and in combination with the tracking system,
between neutral and charged particles. The muon systems are designed
specifically to identify muons and measure both their momentum and
energy through the use of multi-wire proportional chambers.

7.2.1

Ring Imaging Cherenkov Detectors

Charged particles passing through a medium at a velocity, larger than
the velocity of light propagating through that medium, radiate photons
in a cone about the direction of travel of the particle. The polar
angle
\theta
between the velocity vector of the particle and the
radiated light is given by,

\theta=\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n\beta}\right)

(182)

where
n
is the index of refraction for the medium and
{\beta=v/c}
, where
v
is the velocity of the particle.
\mathrm{RICH}
detectors
consist of a radiating medium through which the particle passes, and a
photodetector which measures the rings produced from the radiating
medium. Because
\mathrm{RICH}
detectors can only resolve rings with radii
within a given range, the index of refraction for the radiating
material dictates the velocity range of particles observable by the
detector.

The
\mathrm{RICH}1
, located approximately
{1.0~\mathrm{m}}
along the

z
-axis directly after the
\mathrm{VELO}
, is designed to differentiate
between charged pions and kaons with low momenta within the range
1

to
{60~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. Typically, particles produced with low momenta from

B
-hadron decays provide a broad spread in pseudo-rapidities, and so
the
\mathrm{RICH}1
is designed to cover a pseudo-rapidity range of
{2.1\leq\eta\leq 4.4}
in the
yz
-plane and
{1.9\leq\eta\leq 4.4}
in the
xz
-plane for a particle originating from the
interaction point.
\mathrm{RICH}1
utilises two radiating materials to
achieve this momentum range, aerogel and
\mathrm{C_{4}F_{10}}
. The
photodetectors used for measuring the Cherenkov radiation are hybrid
pixel photon detectors which are housed in magnetic shielding,
allowing the photodetectors to operate in magnetic fields with a field
strength below
{50~\mathrm{mT}}
. The photodetectors can detect
photons with wavelengths between
200
and
{600~\mathrm{nm}}
, and
are made up of
500
by
{500~\mu\mathrm{m}}
pixels.

The
\mathrm{RICH}2
is located after the
\mathrm{OT}
at a distance of approximately

{9.5~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis and is designed to separate
charged pions and kaons with momenta in the range
15
to

100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. Particles from
B
-hadrons with larger momenta are
expected to also have larger pseudo-rapidities, and so
\mathrm{RICH}2
covers
the pseudo-rapidity range
{3.0\leq\eta\leq 4.9}
in the
yz
-plane
and
{2.8\leq\eta\leq 4.9}
in the
xz
-plane. The
\mathrm{RICH}2
uses
the same photodetectors as
\mathrm{RICH}1
, but uses the radiating material

\mathrm{C_{4}F}
, with an index of refraction approximately
1.7
times
smaller than
\mathrm{C_{4}F_{10}}
, to achieve a larger momentum upper limit
than
\mathrm{RICH}1
. While the
\mathrm{RICH}
detectors are not directly used in
the analysis of
Chap.
5

, they provide an important component in the
detection of
B
-hadron decays. Further details on the
\mathrm{RICH}
systems
can be found in the technical design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.00.1
]

.

7.2.2

Scintillating Pad Detector and Preshower Calorimeter

The
SPD
,
\mathrm{PRS}
, and
\mathrm{ECAL}
are aligned along lines of pseudo-rapidity
from the interaction point to provide a one-to-one mapping between
calorimeter cells. The scintillating pad detector is the first
calorimeter along the
z
-axis and is located at

{12.3~\mathrm{m}}
. No absorber material is placed in front of the

SPD
and so charged particles shower within it, providing a fast
method to determine the track multiplicity of the event and
differentiate between charged particles and photons. The
pseudo-rapidity coverage of the
SPD
is designed to match the coverage
of the tracking system and ranges from
{2.1\leq\eta\leq 4.4}
in
the
yz
-plane and from
{1.9\leq\eta\leq 4.4}
in the
xz
-plane
for particles produced at the interaction point. A schematic for the
layout of a single quadrant of the
SPD
,
\mathrm{PRS}
, and
\mathrm{ECAL}
is given in

Fig.
43

, with the
\mathrm{ECAL}
dimensions provided. The dimensions for
the
SPD
are similar but reduced by a factor of approximately

1.0\%
. The
SPD
contains
3312
modules, with
828
modules per
quadrant. The modules all have the same dimensions and are divided
into outer-modules, middle-modules, and inner-modules.

Figure 43
:
Schematic of the lower left quadrant of the

SPD
,
\mathrm{PRS}
, and
\mathrm{ECAL}
