in the
xy
-plane, along the direction of
the
z
-axis. The quadrant consists of
672
outer-modules with

1
square cell each,
112
middle-modules with
4
square cells
each, and
44
inner modules with
9
square cells each. All
modules have the same dimensions, with the dimensions given here
for the
\mathrm{ECAL}
.

Each outer-module consists of a single square scintillating tile made
from polystyrene, while every middle-module houses
4
square tiles,
and every inner-module contains
9
square tiles. The outer-module
tiles are approximately
{12~\mathrm{cm}}
to a side in the

xy
-plane, the middle-module tiles
{6~\mathrm{cm}}
, and the
inner-module tiles
{4~\mathrm{cm}}
. Each tile, which is

{1.5~\mathrm{cm}}
deep and
0.28
radiation lengths, is connected
via wavelength-shifting fibres to a multi-anode photomultiplier to
create an
SPD
calorimeter cell. The segmentation of the
SPD
in the

xy
-plane ensures a roughly similar particle occupancy between all
calorimeter cells. The
SPD
is not used in the reconstruction of
electrons or hadrons in the analysis of
Chap.
5

, but is used to
perform a fast global event cut based on the
SPD
multiplicity of the
event which must be accounted for in
Sect.
10.1

.

Behind each
SPD
calorimeter cell, along the
z
-axis, is placed a

{1.5~\mathrm{cm}}
deep lead absorber of
2.5
radiation lengths,
followed by another
{1.5~\mathrm{cm}}
deep scintillating tile of

0.28
radiation lengths. These tiles are not part of the
SPD
, but
rather the
\mathrm{PRS}
, and are slightly smaller than the
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells by

0.5\%
in order to maintain the one-to-one correspondence between

SPD
,
\mathrm{PRS}
, and
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells along lines of pseudo-rapidity. Every
\mathrm{PRS}
tile, like
SPD
tiles, is connected via wavelength-shifting fibres to
a multi-anode photomultiplier tube to create a
\mathrm{PRS}
calorimeter
cell. The cells for the pre-shower calorimeter provide a segmentation
in the
yz
-plane for the
\mathrm{ECAL}
, and have the same layout and
pseudo-rapidity coverage as both the
SPD
and
\mathrm{ECAL}
. The
\mathrm{PRS}
is used
to identify neutral and charged particles which interact through the
electromagnetic force. In the analysis of
Chap.
5

, the
\mathrm{PRS}
is used
to differentiate between the electrons and charged hadrons of

Sect.
9.1

.

7.2.3

Electromagnetic Calorimeter

The electromagnetic calorimeter is used to identify photons and
electrons and measure their energy. It is located
{12.5~\mathrm{m}}

along the
z
-axis, just after the
SPD
and
\mathrm{PRS}
calorimeters. The

\mathrm{ECAL}
has the same pseudo-rapidity coverage as the
SPD
and
\mathrm{PRS}
, and
is given by the schematic of
Fig.
43

, with the same module
configuration and cell layout in the
xy
-plane as the
SPD
and

\mathrm{PRS}
. Each module, however, contains a stack of
132
layers,
alternating between lead absorbers and calorimeter tiles along the
direction of the
z
-axis; this corresponds to
66
absorbers and
66

scintillating tiles. Each absorber is
{0.2~\mathrm{cm}}
deep and

0.33
radiation lengths, and each scintillator tile is

{0.4~\mathrm{cm}}
deep and
0.08
radiation lengths. The dimensions
for each absorber and tile from a stack in the
xy
-plane are
approximately the same as those for the
SPD
and
\mathrm{PRS}
. The photons
from all
66
tiles in a stack are collected via wavelength-shifting
fibres into a phototube to produce a single
\mathrm{ECAL}
cell. The entire

\mathrm{ECAL}
covers
25
radiation lengths along the direction of the

z
-axis.

The energy resolution for a calorimeter can be written as,

\delta_{E}=\delta_{\mathrm{clb}}E\oplus\delta_{\mathrm{smp}}\sqrt{E}\oplus\delta_{\mathrm{noi}}

(183)

where
E
is the measured energy of the particle,

\delta_{\mathrm{clb}}
is the calibration uncertainty,

\delta_{\mathrm{smp}}
is the uncertainty due to sampling fluctuations,
and
\delta_{\mathrm{noi}}
is the noise uncertainty. For high energy
particles, oftentimes only the first two uncertainties are considered
as the noise uncertainty is negligible. In
Ref.
[
lhcb.07.2
]

these
uncertainties were measured to be
{\delta_{\mathrm{clb}}=8.3\times 10^{-2}}
and
{\delta_{\mathrm{smp}}=9.5\times 10^{-1}~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}^{1/2}}
for the LHCb
\mathrm{ECAL}
. The energy of
particles as measured by the
\mathrm{ECAL}
is used to separate electrons from
hadrons in the analysis of
Chap.
5

.

7.2.4

Hadronic Calorimeter

The hadronic calorimeter is directly after the
\mathrm{ECAL}
,

{13.3~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis, and is used to identify
hadrons and measure their energy. The pseudo-rapidity coverage of the

\mathrm{HCAL}
is
{1.8\leq\eta\leq 4.2}
in the
yz
-plane and
{2.1\leq\eta\leq 4.2}
in the
xz
-plane for a particle originating from
the interaction point. A schematic of the calorimeter cell layout for
the
\mathrm{HCAL}
is given in
Fig.
44

for the lower left quadrant in
the
xy
-plane along the direction of the
z
-axis. Each
\mathrm{HCAL}
quadrant contains
152
square outer-cells with sides of

{26.26~\mathrm{cm}}
in the
xy
-plane, and
215
inner-cells with
sides of
{13.13~\mathrm{cm}}
. The segmentation of the
\mathrm{HCAL}
cells in
the
xy
-plane is larger than the
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells due to the larger size
of hadronic showers.

Figure 44
:
Schematic of the lower left quadrant of the

\mathrm{HCAL}
in the
xy
-plane, along the direction of the
z
-axis. The
quadrant consists of
152
outer-cells and
215

inner-cells.

Each outer-cell is made from a stack of
26
layers along the
direction of the
x
-axis, opposed to the
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells which are
stacked along the
z
-axis. The width of each layer is

{1.01~\mathrm{cm}}
, with a height of
{26.26~\mathrm{cm}}
and a
depth of
{128.3~\mathrm{cm}}
. Every layer contains two sublayers, a

{0.6~\mathrm{cm}}
wide structural plate, followed by a sublayer of

3
polystyrene scintillator tiles alternating with iron absorbers
along the direction of the
z
-axis. The scintillator tiles are

{0.3~\mathrm{cm}}
wide and
{19.7~\mathrm{cm}}
deep, while the iron
absorbers are
{0.4~\mathrm{cm}}
wide. Each scintillator tile is
separated by
{20.2~\mathrm{cm}}
of iron absorber along the direction
of the
z
-axis, which is
1.0
interaction length of material. The
scintillator-absorber sublayer layout is staggered between the layers
so that the scintillator of one layer is followed by an absorber in
the next layer of the stack. The scintillator tiles are connected to
photomultiplier tubes via wavelength-shifting fibres, with one read
out channel per cell.

The inner-cells have the same stack structure as the outer-cells, but
each stack contains
13
layers. Additionally, each scintillator tile
is split in two with the same width and depth but a height of

{13.13~\mathrm{cm}}
. Consequently, each inner-cell stack contains
two inner-cells along the direction of the
y
-axis. From this
configuration, each outer-cell houses
78
scintillator tiles and each
inner-cell contains
39
scintillator tiles. The entire
\mathrm{HCAL}
,
including structure and absorbers, is
5.6
interaction lengths along
the
z
-axis. The energy resolution of the
\mathrm{HCAL}
, using the same
parametrisation of
Eq.
183

, is
{\delta_{\mathrm{clb}}=0.9}
and
{\delta_{\mathrm{smp}}=6.9~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}^{1/2}}
from

Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

. The energy of particles measured by the
\mathrm{HCAL}
is used
to separate hadrons from electrons in the analysis of

Chap.
5

. Further information on all of the calorimeter systems can
be found in the technical design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.00.2
]

.

7.2.5

Muon System

Because muons are two orders of magnitude more massive than electrons,
the energy lost through bremsstrahlung radiation for muons is ten
orders of magnitude smaller than electrons, and so muons do not shower
in the
\mathrm{ECAL}
. Additionally, muons interact minimally with the
\mathrm{HCAL}
,
as they are leptons and do not interact through the strong
force. Consequently, the majority of the muon system is placed after
the
\mathrm{ECAL}
and
\mathrm{HCAL}
. The muon system is divided into five stations,
M
1
through M
5
, located at distances of
{12.1~\mathrm{m}}
,

{15.3~\mathrm{m}}
,
{16.5~\mathrm{m}}
,
{17.7~\mathrm{m}}
, and

{18.9~\mathrm{m}}
along the
z
-axis. The five stations cover a
pseudo-rapidity range of
{2.0\leq\eta\leq 4.8}
in the
yz
-plane
and
{1.9\leq\eta\leq 4.6}
in the
xz
-plane for particles
produced from the interaction point. The first three muon stations are
built for high resolution transverse momentum measurements, while the
final two stations are designed for primarily particle identification.
To ensure only muons reach the final two stations, three iron
absorbers, each with a depth of
{80~\mathrm{cm}}
, are placed between
the final four muon stations. To reach the final station, a muon will
have traversed over
20
interaction lengths of material, including
the calorimeters.

Figure 45
:
Schematic of the lower left quadrant of a
muon station in the
xy
-plane, along the direction of the

z
-axis. The width and height of the quadrant for each muon
station is given. Adapted from
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

.

The five muon stations are segmented into chambers, with the chamber
layout and size designed to provide a one-to-one mapping between the
chambers of each station along lines of pseudo-rapidity. A schematic
of the chamber layout for a quadrant of a muon station in the

xy
-plane is given in
Fig.
45

. The quadrant is divided into
four regions, R
1
through R
4
, where the chamber resolution is lower
for chambers in regions farther from the
z
-axis. Each chamber is a
multi-wire proportional chamber except for the R
1
-chambers of the
first muon station, which are triple-gas electron multiplier
chambers. The chambers are divided into rectangular pads, where each
pad has a read out. For all muon stations the R
1
-chambers are
segmented into
8
pads along the
y
-axis, while the R
2
-chambers
are segmented into
4
pads, the R
3
-chambers into
2
pads, and the
R
1
-chambers into only a single pad. Along the
x
-axis, the R
1

through R
3
-chambers of the M
1
station are divided into
24
pads,
while the R
4
-chambers are divided into
12
pads. For M
1
and M
2
,
the number of pads along the
x
-axis for each chamber type are
doubled with respect to the M
1
segmentation. For M
4
and M
5
, the
pad segmentation of the chambers along the
x
-axis is halved.

The transverse momentum resolution,
\delta_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}/{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, for muon tracks
using only hits from the muon stations was measured to be
approximately
20\%
in
Ref.
[
lhcb.10.2
]

. The use of the muon system in
the analysis of
Chap.
5

is critical. Every decay category of
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events analysed, except one, requires at least a single
muon, which is identified using M
2
through M
5
. Additionally, the
track finding efficiency for muons passing through the
\mathrm{VELO}
,
\mathrm{IT}
,
and
\mathrm{OT}
is determined using combined hits from the
\mathrm{TT}
and muon
system in
Sect.
10.1

. Further information on the LHCb muon
system can be found in the technical design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.01.3
]

.

8

LHCb Event Reconstruction

To interpret the raw output from the LHCb detector described in

Sect.
7

, the signals from the subdetectors are combined using
reconstruction to produce high level physics objects that can be used
in analyses. Hits from the tracking system are combined to produce
tracks, particle trajectories of charged particles, while cells from
the calorimeters are clustered to find deposits of energy from either
charged or neutral particles. Matching algorithms between tracks and
calorimeter clusters, as well as
\mathrm{RICH}
information, is then run to
identify particles.

Reconstruction of LHCb data is performed twice, once at the online
level of the trigger system and once at the offline level. The online
triggers perform a fast reconstruction that is not complete, to
quickly filter events from the detector and write them to disk for
later offline reconstruction. Offline reconstruction uses the
information from the entire event to create a more precise
detector-wide reconstruction of the event. The offline LHCb reconstruction software is
Brunel

[
brunel.13.1
]
which produces
data files that can be processed by the
DaVinci

[
davinci.13.1
]

physics analysis software.

Full simulations of events in the detector are performed with the

Gauss

[
gauss.13.1
]
simulation software which utilises

\textsc{Pythia}~6

[
sjostrand.06.1
,
pythia.13.1
]
as a general purpose Monte Carlo
event generator,
EvtGen

[
evtgen.13.1
]
as a
B
-hadron particle
decayer, and
Geant4
as a detector simulator. The simulated events
produced from
Gauss
are digitised by the
Boole

[
boole.13.1
]

software, with trigger emulation applied by the

Moore

[
moore.13.1
]
software.

All LHCb software is based on the
Gaudi

[
gaudi.13.1
]

framework. Large distributed processing jobs for any of the LHCb software is typically submitted through the
Ganga

[
ganga.13.1
]

software and can be distributed to the CERN and LHCb computing grids
via the
Dirac

[
dirac.13.1
]
software. Further information on
LHCb computing and software can be found in the technical design
report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.05.1
]

.

The track reconstruction implemented in
Brunel
is described in

Sect.
8.1

, and the use of calorimeter information in the

Brunel
reconstruction process is outlined in
Sect.
8.2

. The
trigger system, which uses similar algorithms to the tracking
algorithms implemented in
Brunel
, is introduced in

Sect.
8.3

. Finally, the methods used for determining the
integrated luminosity for a reconstructed data sample are described in

Sect.
8.4

.

8.1

Tracking Information

Information from the tracking system of
Sect.
7.1

provides the
foundation for LHCb event reconstruction. Hits from the
\mathrm{VELO}
,
\mathrm{TT}
,

\mathrm{IT}
, and
\mathrm{OT}
are combined into tracks which represent possible
trajectories of charged particles passing through the detector. These
trajectories, if passing through the dipole magnetic field of

Fig.

, can be used to determine the momentum of the
particle. Tracks from a common origin can be used to produce vertices,
i.e. locations in the detector where two or more particles were
produced. In LHCb reconstruction, five types of tracks are
considered
[
lhcb.07.3
]
.

\mathrm{VELO}
:

Contain hits only in the

\mathrm{VELO}
and consequently have no associated momenta. Because the
\mathrm{VELO}
surrounds the interaction point, these tracks can have either a
forward or backward direction along the
z
-axis. These tracks are
particularly useful for vertex reconstruction.

T (
\mathrm{IT}
/
\mathrm{OT}
):

Have hits from either
the
\mathrm{IT}
or
\mathrm{OT}
, or both, but do not have any associated hits
from the
\mathrm{VELO}
or
\mathrm{TT}
. T-tracks are combined with information from
the
\mathrm{RICH}2
and used for the reconstruction of
charged pions and kaons.

upstream:

Are built from both
\mathrm{VELO}
and
\mathrm{TT}
hits, but do not contain
\mathrm{VELO}
,
\mathrm{IT}
, or
\mathrm{OT}
hits. Oftentimes, these are low momentum tracks which are deflected
from within the detector by the magnetic field of the dipole. If the
tracks are within
\mathrm{RICH}1
acceptance the mass of the particle
producing the track can typically be determined.

downstream:

Consist of hits from
the
\mathrm{TT}
and the
\mathrm{IT}
,
\mathrm{OT}
, or both. These tracks can be produced
from long lived particles that may not decay within the
\mathrm{VELO}
, such
as the
K_{S}
.

long:

Contain hits from the
\mathrm{VELO}
and the
\mathrm{IT}
,
\mathrm{OT}
, or both. These tracks may also contain hits from
the
\mathrm{TT}
, but are not required to contain
\mathrm{TT}
hits. This track type
is the most commonly used type in LHCb analyses.

A graphical summary of the LHCb track types is given in

Fig.

. The tracks used in the analysis of
Chap.
5

are
long-tracks, and the efficiency of long-track reconstruction for muons
is measured in
Sect.
10.1

.

Figure 46
:
Schematic of the LHCb tracking system in the
bending
xz
-plane depicting the different types of reconstructed
LHCb tracks.

Figure 46
:
Schematic of the LHCb tracking system in the
bending
xz
-plane depicting the different types of reconstructed
LHCb tracks.

Tracks are reconstructed by first track-seeding in the individual
tracking system detectors, then track-finding where a search is made
outside the seeding detector, and finally track-fitting where the
trajectories from track-finding are refined. After this, reconstructed
long-tracks are extrapolated to the muon system, and if compatible
hits are found, a combined muon track is formed. The tracks produced
from the reconstruction process are then used to seed possible
vertices, which are then further refined through vertex
fitting. Further information on LHCb track reconstruction can be
found in
Ref.
[
lhcb.04.1
,
*lhcb.06.1
,
*lhcb.07.4
,
*lhcb.07.5
,
*lhcb.07.6
,
*lhcb.07.7
,
*lhcb.07.8
,
*lhcb.07.9
,
*lhcb.07.10
]

.

8.2

Calorimeter Information

After the reconstruction of tracks from
Sect.
8.1

is performed,
calorimeter and
\mathrm{RICH}
information is used to identify the type of
particle from which the track was produced. In
Chap.
5

only muon
system and calorimeter information is used to identify muons,
electrons, and charged hadrons, with no
\mathrm{RICH}
information
used. Consequently,
\mathrm{RICH}
reconstruction is not introduced in this
thesis, but an overview can be found in
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.2
]

.
\mathrm{ECAL}
information is used to create neutral pion and photon candidates, as
well as identify tracks from electrons and correct their
energy. Additionally, the energies from
\mathrm{PRS}
,
\mathrm{ECAL}
, and
\mathrm{HCAL}
cells
associated with tracks extrapolated to the calorimeters are used to
differentiate between muons, electrons, and charged hadrons.

Electrons within LHCb interact with the detector material, losing
energy through the emission of bremsstrahlung photons, and so the
recovery of these photons is important in reconstructing the full
momentum of electrons. Within LHCb reconstruction, all long-tracks
are considered as electron candidates and bremsstrahlung recovery is
performed by searching for compatible photon candidates and adding
their momentum to the track momentum. Photon candidates are
reconstructed by grouping
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells into
\mathrm{ECAL}
clusters, matching

\mathrm{ECAL}
clusters with tracks, and generating candidates from clusters
without matching tracks. Details on the LHCb calorimeter
reconstruction can be found in
Refs.
[
lhcb.01.4
]

,
[
lhcb.03.3
]
, and

[
lhcb.03.4
]
.

The calorimeter energy associated with a long-track is found by
extrapolating the track from its last state vector to the start of the
calorimeter. The track is then linearly extrapolated from the start of
the calorimeter through the depth of the calorimeter and points are
sampled along the line of the trajectory. A list of the calorimeter
cells which contain the points is made, with any duplicate cells
removed. The energies from the corresponding digits of the cells from
the list are then summed to produce the associated calorimeter energy
for the track.

((a))

((b))

Figure 47
:
Distribution of transverse

\mathrm{ECAL}
energy associated with electrons tracks taken from
{Z\to ee}
data events, illustrating
\mathrm{ECAL}
cell
saturation.
Invariant mass distributions for

{Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{Z\to ee}
events from data,
demonstrating incomplete bremsstrahlung recovery.

The associated
\mathrm{PRS}
,
\mathrm{ECAL}
, and
\mathrm{HCAL}
energies of tracks are used to
identify high
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
electrons and charged hadrons in
Chap.
5

. However,
the LHCb calorimeter systems were designed for lower
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}

B
-hadron
decay products, and the individual
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells saturate at transverse
energies,
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
, greater than
10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. Consequently, many of the
energies from the
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells associated with an electron track are
fully saturated, and so the
\mathrm{ECAL}

{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
for an electron is degraded. In

Fig.
47(a)

the distribution for the
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
of electrons produced
from
{Z\to ee}
events in data is plotted, where clear peaks can
be seen at multiples of
10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, the
\mathrm{ECAL}
saturation

{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
. Additionally, the energies for recovered bremsstrahlung photons
are also degraded, and so the bremsstrahlung correction of the
momentum for electron tracks is incomplete, resulting in a reduced
momentum resolution. This can be seen in the broadened and shifted

{Z\to ee}
invariant mass distribution taken from data with
respect to the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
distribution plotted in

Fig.
47(b)

.

8.3

Triggers

The rate of detectable collisions produced within the LHCb detector
is approximately
{10~\mathrm{MHz}}
and must be reduced to a rate of

{2~\mathrm{kHz}}
for storage. Most of the events produced within the
detector are not of interest for LHCb physics analyses, and
consequently can be discarded. The LHCb trigger system provides a
fast decision on whether an event should be kept or discarded and is
used to reduce the rate of events from the detector to a rate that can
be written to disk. The LHCb trigger system consists of both hardware
and software components and is divided into a level
0
trigger (L
0
)
and a high level trigger (HLT). A full description of the LHCb trigger system can be found in
Ref.
[
lhcb.08.1
]

and the technical
design report of
Ref.
[
lhcb.03.5
]

.

8.3.1

Level 0 Trigger

The level
0
trigger is a hardware trigger which must process each
event within
2~\mu\mathrm{s}
and reduce the event rate from

{10~\mathrm{MHz}}
to
{1~\mathrm{MHz}}
using partial detector
information. At this lower event rate the entire detector can be read
out for each event and passed on to the HLT for further
refinement. The L
0
is divided into a calorimeter trigger and a muon
trigger. With these two components, the L
0
attempts to reconstruct
the highest
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
photon, electron, neutral pion, hadron, and the two
highest
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
muons in the event. The information from each component is
passed to a decision unit which then produces a combined decision on
whether the event should be passed to the HLT.

The calorimeter trigger attempts to reconstruct the highest transverse
energy photon, electron, and hadron in the event using
19420
read
out channels from the
SPD
,
\mathrm{PRS}
,
\mathrm{ECAL}
, and
\mathrm{HCAL}
. Portions of the

\mathrm{ECAL}
and
\mathrm{HCAL}
are read out, and the sum of the transverse energy for
every two-by-two group of cells is calculated. The group of cells with
the highest
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
in the
\mathrm{HCAL}
is passed on as a hadron candidate. The
two-by-two groups of
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells are merged with the
SPD
and
\mathrm{PRS}
cells to produce photon, electron, and neutral pion candidates. The
candidate with the largest
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
of each type is passed on to the
decision unit along with the total
\mathrm{HCAL}

{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
and
SPD
multiplicity.

The muon trigger uses
25920
read out channels and searches for muon
tracks in the event. The tracks are seeded from hits in the third muon
station, M
3
, which are linearly extrapolated to the interaction
point. A search for hits in a field of interest along the
x
-axis is
made in M
2
and along both the
x
and
y
-axis in M
4
and M
5
. If
hits are found in all three of these stations the line from the M
3

hit to the M
2
hit is extrapolated to M
1
and the closest hit within
a field of interest is added to the track. The momentum of the track
is estimated and the two highest
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
tracks are passed on to the
decision unit.

The decision unit combines the information from the two L
0
triggers,
and either accepts the event and passes on the information to the HLT,
or discards the event. The L
0
will accept an event if a hadron
candidate with
{{E_{\mathrm{T}}}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
is found. The L
0
will also accept an
event if an electron, photon, or neutral pion candidate with
{{E_{\mathrm{T}}}>2.5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
is found or if
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>1.2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for any muon
track. Additionally, an event will be accepted if
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}+{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}>1~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for the two muon tracks. All calorimeter candidates and
tracks that pass the requirements above are sent to the
\mathrm{HLT}
trigger as L
0

objects.

8.3.2

High Level Trigger

The high level trigger is a software trigger which runs on an event
filter farm consisting of
2000
computing nodes and is divided into
an HLT
1
and HLT
2
level. The HLT
1
must reduce the

{1~\mathrm{MHz}}
event rate from the L
0
to the
{30~\mathrm{kHz}}

event rate required by HLT
2
, and begins by confirming the L
0

objects. L
0
calorimeter objects are confirmed by accessing tracking
system information and attempting to reconstruct a track associated
with the object; the L
0
decision is confirmed if no track is found
for neutral objects and if a track is found for charged objects.

The HLT
2
takes the
{30~\mathrm{kHz}}
event rate from HLT
1
, and
reduces this to a rate of
{2~\mathrm{kHz}}
where the raw data from
the event can be written to disk for later full reconstruction and
analysis. The HLT
2
performs full track fitting. Because the HLT
1

and HLT
2
are both software based triggers, the algorithms and
selections applied can be modified to accommodate improved techniques
or changing physics interests. To ensure consistency between the
algorithms and requirements used between data taking periods, a
trigger configuration key (TCK) is assigned to each unique trigger
setup which can be later accessed during analysis.

8.4

Luminosity Determination

Many of the physics analyses performed using LHCb data require a
precise measurement of the integrated beam luminosity for the dataset
being analysed. The luminosity can be determined with,

\frac{\textrm{d}{\mathscr{L}}}{\textrm{d}{t}}=\frac{\mu N_{b}f}{\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}}

(184)

where
\mu
is the average number of visible proton-proton
interactions per bunch crossing,
\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}
is the
proton-proton inelastic cross-section,
N_{b}
is the number of proton
bunches which is well known, and
f
is the revolution frequency which
is also well known. Only the values of
\mu
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}

are unknown;
\mu
must be measured per bunch crossing while

\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}
remains constant and need only be measured once.

The value of
\mu
can be measured per bunch crossing by recording
observables, or luminosity counters, that are proportional to

\mu
. For LHCb luminosity determination, five luminosity counters
are recorded: the number of
\mathrm{VELO}
vertices, the number of
\mathrm{VELO}

rz
-tracks, the number of hits in the
\mathrm{VELO}
pile-up sensors, the
number of
SPD
hits, and the
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
deposition within the
calorimeters. Of these counters, the number of
\mathrm{VELO}

rz
-tracks is
found to be the most reliable measure of
\mu
. The inelastic
cross-section is measured using two different methods, a Van de Meer
scan and a beam-gas imaging technique. Both are described in the
remainder of this section. The combination of the uncertainties from
the measurements on
\mu
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}
result in a
3.5\%

uncertainty on the integrated luminosity measurement used for any
LHCb analysis with
2011
data. Full details on the LHCb luminosity
determination can be found in
Ref.
[
lhcb.11.1
]

.

8.4.1

Van de Meer Scan

The Van de Meer scan was first proposed by Van de Meer in

Ref.
[
meer.68.1
]

, and is performed by scanning the two colliding beams
across each other in the transverse plane. The visible inelastic
cross-section is given by,

\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}=\frac{\int\mu(\Delta_{x},{\Delta_{y}}_{0})\textrm{\,d}{\Delta_{x}}\int\mu({\Delta_{x}}_{0},\Delta_{y})\textrm{\,d}{\Delta_{y}}}{{N_{p}}_{1}{N_{p}}_{2}\mu({\Delta_{x}}_{0},{\Delta_{y}}_{0})\cos\alpha_{1}}

(185)

where
{N_{p}}_{1}
is the number of protons per bunch in the first beam,

{N_{p}}_{2}
is the number of protons per bunch in the second beam, and

\alpha_{1}
is the polar angle of the first beam velocity with respect
to the
z
-axis
[
balagura.11.1
]
. Here
\Delta_{x}
is the

x
-coordinate offset between the two beams with a nominal offset of

{\Delta_{x}}_{0}
, and
\Delta_{y}
is the
y
-coordinate offset with a
nominal value of
{\Delta_{y}}_{0}
.

The beams are scanned over a series of approximately
15
to
30

x
-offset steps with a constant
y
-offset of
{\Delta_{y}}_{0}
, and the
average
\mu
per step is measured. These measurements are used to
calculate the first integral of
Eq.
185

. The same process is
repeated, but now with
y
-offsets and a constant
x
-offset of

{\Delta_{x}}_{0}
. These measurements of
\mu
are then used to calculate
the second integral. The systematic uncertainty on
\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}

is determined from the uncertainty from the
\mu
measurements of the
scans, the offsets of the scans, and the uncertainty on the number of
protons per bunch for both beams. The primary source of uncertainty is
from the number of protons per bunch, and is on the order of
2.7\%
.

8.4.2

Beam-Gas Imaging

The beam-gas imaging method, first proposed in
Ref.
[
luzzi.05.1
]

, uses
the vertices reconstructed within the
\mathrm{VELO}
from the interactions of
the beams with residual gas in the beam pipes to measure the profile
of the beams and determine
\sigma_{\mathrm{inelastic}}
. The distribution of
vertices provide a transverse image of the beams from which their
angles, profiles, and positions can be extracted. While the rate of
interactions is much smaller than for a Van de Meer scan, the beams do
not need to be moved, and so many uncertainties of the Van de Meer
scan method are mitigated. However, the beam-gas imaging method
requires a vertex resolution smaller than the transverse beam width
and a well understood uncertainty, as this contributes to the overall
systematic uncertainty.

Beam-gas interaction measurements are made both when the bunches
collide and do not collide. Only the measurements from colliding
bunches can be used to make luminosity measurements, but the
measurements from non-colliding bunches can be used to further
understand the beams and perform cross-checks with the measurements
from the colliding bunches. The transverse beams widths are determined
with beam-gas interactions from colliding bunches, but must be
measured away from the interaction point so beam-gas interactions can
be separated from proton-proton interactions. The same beam width
measurement can be made from non-colliding bunches, but the
measurement can also be made at the interaction point. These two
widths from non-colliding bunches are compared to ensure the offset
width does not differ within uncertainty from the interaction point
width. The beam crossing angles are also measured using beam-gas
interactions from non-colliding beams.

The systematic uncertainty on the luminosity determination using the
beam-gas imaging method depends upon the vertex resolution, time
stability, beam sizes and offsets, gas pressure gradient, and
crossing-angle effects as well as the number of protons per bunch. The
luminosity measurements made with the beam-gas imaging method for

2011
data are consistent with the Van de Meer measurements, and also
have a systematic uncertainty dominated by the number of protons per
bunch.

Chapter 5

Z Boson Cross-Section

Within this chapter, a measurement of the cross-section for
Z
bosons decaying into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair using data from LHCb with
{\sqrt{s}=7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}}
is presented. The analysis is described in
Sect.
9

,
while the cross-section measurement is performed in
Sect.
10

and
the results are presented in
Sect.
11

. In this introduction, the

{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section measurement is motivated by
describing how it can be used to test lepton universality, search for
new physics, and further constrain the proton
\mathrm{PDF}
.

In the standard model of particle physics (
\mathrm{SM}
), the
Z
boson couples to
fermions with a vertex given by
Fig.
4(b)

which is a factor of

{\frac{-ig_{w}}{2{\cos\theta_{w}}}\gamma^{\mu}(v_{f}-a_{f}\gamma^{5})}
where the
vector and axial couplings,
v_{f}
and
a_{f}
, are for neutral leptons,
charged leptons,
u
-type quarks, or
d
-type quarks, and are given in

Table
1

. The charged leptons,
e
,
\mu
, and
\tau
,
all have the same factors, and so the
Z
boson couples with an identical
strength to each charged lepton. This property of the SM, lepton
universality, can be experimentally tested by comparing the ratios of

{Z\to ee}
,
{Z\to\mu\mu}
, and
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

production. Previous measurements by LEP
[
lep.06.1
]
have been
made,

\frac{\sigma_{ee\to Z\to\mu\mu}}{\sigma_{ee\to Z\to ee}}=1.0009\pm 0.0028,\quad\quad\frac{\sigma_{ee\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}}{\sigma_{ee\to Z\to ee}}=1.0019\pm 0.0032

(186)

which verify lepton universality in
Z
boson decays to a precision of
better than
1\%
. The measurement of
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}

within this chapter, when compared to the LHCb measurements of

\sigma_{pp\to Z\to ee}

[
lhcb.13.2
]
and
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}

[
lhcb.12.1
]
, can test lepton universality to the
level of
4\%
at best, assuming the precision of
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
is similar to the precisions of the
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to ee}
and
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}

measurements. However, the LHCb tests of lepton universality are
unique; the
Z
bosons are produced from
proton-proton collisions, unlike the electron-positron collisions at
LEP, and the leptons produced from the
Z
bosons are observed in the
forward pseudo-rapidity range of
2.0\leq\eta\leq 4.5
.

Measuring a ratio different from unity might be indicative of new
physics, rather than a failure of lepton universality. In most models
with Higgs bosons, described in
Sect.
1.2

for the
\mathrm{SM}
and
Sect.
1.4

for the minimal supersymmetric model (
\mathrm{MSSM}
), the Higgs bosons are expected
to couple to fermions with a strength proportional to the mass of the
fermion. This can be seen by the factor of the fermion mass,
m_{f}
, in
the vertices of
Fig.
6(g)

for the
\mathrm{SM}
and
Figs.
10(a)

through
10(f)
for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
. The Higgs bosons can also couple
to the electroweak vector bosons with strengths proportional to the
mass of the electroweak bosons. The relevant vertices are given in

Figs.
6(b)

and
6(c)
for the
\mathrm{SM}
and

Figs.
10(i)

through
10(l)
for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
.

Consequently, for a neutral Higgs boson with a mass less than
2m_{W}
, the
dominant decays of the Higgs boson at leading order are into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and
b
-quark pairs. For the case of neutral Higgs bosons decaying into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs, this
process will be similar to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal, except for
the invariant mass and spin correlations of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair, and will
provide an unaccounted background to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal,
resulting in an excess of observed events. Using the measured
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section, Higgs boson searches are performed in

Chap.
6

by looking for an unexpected excess of events.

((a))

((b))

Figure 48
:
An example
\mathrm{SUSY}
decay
chain from a stau LSP, producing a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
in the final state. Dashed
lines indicate bosons and solid line
fermions.
Rough outline in momentum
fraction and momentum transfer space of the measurements currently
used to constrain the proton
\mathrm{PDF}
. The
x
and
Q^{2}
limits on the
data available from Tevatron (blue), HERA (green), and fixed
target experiment (yellow) are taken from
Ref.
[
ball.11.1
]

.

In addition to excesses from Higgs boson decays, many
\mathrm{SUSY}
models, described
in
Sect.
1.4

, predict a lightest supersymmetric partner (LSP) such
as the stau. The decay of the stau depends upon the
\mathrm{SUSY}
parameter
space, but oftentimes the stau will decay via chains containing
\tau
leptons,
such as the decay of
Fig.
48(a)

[
dreiner.09.1
]
. Here,
the stau decays into a virtual lightest neutralino,
\tilde{N}_{1}
, and
a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
. The neutralino then decays into a muon and left-handed smuon,

\tilde{\mu}_{L}
, which further decays into a
u
-quark and
d
-quark pair. The
dashed lines in this diagram indicate bosons, while the solid lines
indicate fermions and the arrows indicate the flow of electromagnetic
charge. These types of events can also produce unaccounted backgrounds
to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal, resulting in an excess in the ratio
of the measured
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
to
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}
and
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to ee}
.

Unlike at LEP,
Z
bosons at LHCb are produced from composite particles;
typically a valence quark and sea anti-quark from two protons
annihilate, producing a
Z
boson. Consequently, the measurement of the
Z
cross-section at LHC can be used to refine the structure of the
proton
\mathrm{PDF}
, described in
Sect.
1.3

. The proton
\mathrm{PDF}
is
constrained by previous results from Tevatron, HERA, and fixed
target experiments at values of the parton momentum fraction,
x
, and
momentum transfer,
Q^{2}
, in the regions of parameters space shown in

Fig.
48(b)

. The
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
centre-of-mass energy of the
LHC combined with the forward coverage of LHCb provides
Z
boson data,
which can be used to further constrain the proton
\mathrm{PDF}
. The region in
which LHCb data can be used is also shown in

Fig.
48(b)

. This estimated coverage for LHCb
Z
boson data
is bounded by,

Q^{2}(x)=\begin{cases}xs&\mbox{upper bound}\\
e^{\pm 2\left|\eta\right|}x^{2}\sqrt{s}&\mbox{left-right bounds}\\
m_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}&\mbox{lower bound}\\
\end{cases}

(187)

where
\sqrt{s}
is the centre-of-mass energy of the two colliding
beams,
\eta
is defined in
Eq.
180

and is the minimum or
maximum pseudo-rapidity of the detector, and
m_{\mathrm{min}}
is the
minimum measured invariant mass of the two leptons produced from the

Z
boson. The relations of the upper and left-right bounds from

Eq.
187

are determined by setting the momentum of one
colliding parton as
x\sqrt{s}/2
, the momentum of the other parton as

Q^{2}/(2x/\sqrt{s})
, and assuming both partons have only longitudinal
momentum.

9

Analysis

Within this section the methods used for selecting the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events used in the cross-section calculation of

Sect.
10

are described. First the final states of the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events and possible backgrounds are outlined. In

Sect.
9.1

the details of the particle identification criteria used
to obtain
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are given. The event selection
which uses these candidates is then described in

Sect.
9.2

. Finally, in
Sect.
9.3

the methods used to estimate
the backgrounds to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal are introduced.

The cross-section for producing a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair from the partons of two
protons can be perturbatively expanded into terms represented by
diagrams mediated by an excited photon or
Z
boson with their leading
order matrix elements given by
Eqs.
112

and

113
respectively. Experimentally, these terms and their
interference, given by
Eq.
114

, cannot be
separated. Consequently, for the remainder of this chapter, the symbol

Z
is used to indicate contributions from an excited photon or
Z
boson with an invariant mass about the on-shell mass of the

Z
boson. Additionally, the symbol
\ell
for a charged lepton is used to
indicate only an electron or muon, and not a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
.

Most
\tau
leptons produced within LHCb decay before reaching the
\mathrm{VELO}
which
is described in
Sect.
7.1

, and so the experimental selection of

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events must use
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products rather than the

\tau
leptons themselves, contrary to
Z\to\mu\mu
and
Z\to ee

analyses where lepton pairs can be directly selected. Because the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is heavier than the lightest mesons and all other leptons, it can
decay into a variety of final states with multiplicities of up to
seven particles, as detailed in
Sect.
4

and summarised in

Table
5

. However, the experimental reconstruction of high
multiplicity
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
final states can have large backgrounds from
\mathrm{QCD}
induced processes at hadron colliders which introduce undesirable
systematic uncertainties. Consequently, this analysis only utilises
leptonic
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
neutrino with a lepton and lepton
neutrino, or semi-leptonic
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
neutrino and single
charged hadron; only a single muon, electron, or charged hadron is
selected as a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product, as neutrinos are not
reconstructed. The matrix elements for these decays are given by

Eqs.
131

and
133
of
Sect.
4

.

((a))

jet

((b))

Figure 49
:
The signal
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
final states
considered with
two leptonic
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays and

one leptonic decay and one semi-leptonic
decay. The experimentally selected final state is highlighted in
red.

((a))

jet

((b))

jet

((c))

jet
jet

((d))

((e))

((f))

Figure 50
:
Examples of the

{Z\to\ell\ell}
,
–

\mathrm{EWK}
,

\mathrm{QCD}
,

{WW}
, and

{t\bar{t}}
backgrounds are
given, with the experimentally selected final state highlighted in
red.

A lepton is required to experimentally trigger
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

events, and so only final states where both
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays are leptonic,
or where one
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay is leptonic and one
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay is
semi-leptonic, are selected. Feynman diagrams for these two final
states are given in
Fig.
49

. A variety of processes, with
examples diagrammed in
Fig.
50

, contribute experimental
backgrounds to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
final states of

Fig.
49

. For this analysis the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson provides a
negligible contribution, as demonstrated in
Chap.
6

, and
consequently is not considered as a background. In both

Fig.
49

and
Fig.
50

the possibly selected
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are highlighted in red.

A
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background is shown in
Fig.
50(a)

where an
electron or muon pair is produced from a
Z
boson. This acts as a
background to
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events consisting of an
electron or muon pair where both
\tau
leptons decay leptonically. If one of
the leptons from the event type of
Fig.
50(a)

is mis-identified as
a hadron, then these events can also act as a background to the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events of
Fig.
49(b)

where only one
of the
\tau
leptons decays leptonically and the other decays
semi-leptonically. For signal events where one
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays
leptonically into an electron and the other into a muon, the
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background is not considered, as the probability of
mis-identifying a muon as an electron, or conversely an electron as a
muon, is negligible.

In
Figs.
50(c)

and
50(b)
a single charged lepton
from a
Z
or
W
boson decay falls within LHCb acceptance and an
associated jet provides either an additional charged lepton or
hadron. Alternatively, this jet could be produced from jet activity
not associated with the vector boson. These two backgrounds are
grouped together as an electroweak background,
\mathrm{EWK}
, and are
considered for both
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal event types. Backgrounds
consisting of particles produced from jet activity,

Fig.
50(d)

, are grouped into a general
\mathrm{QCD}
background where
a hard lepton is typically produced from the decay of a heavy flavour
meson and either an additional lepton or a charged hadron is produced
within the event. This background, like the
\mathrm{EWK}
background, is
considered for both
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal event types.

Pairs of
W
bosons can be produced within LHCb, resulting in the
background shown in
Fig.
50(e)

. Here, both the
W
bosons decay
leptonically, providing a background to signal events where both
\tau
leptons decay leptonically. One of the
W
bosons can also decay into quarks,
providing a background to signal events with a leptonic and
semi-leptonic
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay. In
Fig.
50(f)

a background to signal
events where both
\tau
leptons decay leptonically is produced from a pair of

t
-quarks decaying leptonically. One of the
t
-quarks can also decay
hadronically, producing a background to signal events with a leptonic
and semi-leptonic
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay.

9.1

Particle Identification

In order to select
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events, the muons,
electrons, and charged hadrons within an event must first be
reconstructed and identified. All reconstructed particles considered
as
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are long tracks, described in

Sect.
8.1

, which are required to have a
\chi^{2}
probability
greater than
0.1\%
and a pseudo-rapidity within the range
2.0\leq\eta\leq 4.5
. Hits associated with the long tracks from the muon
system are used to identify muons, while associated calorimeter
energy, described in
Sect.
8.2

, from the
\mathrm{PRS}
,
\mathrm{ECAL}
, and
\mathrm{HCAL}
is used to differentiate between electrons and charged
hadrons. Additionally, the momenta for electron candidates is
corrected for bremsstrahlung radiation losses using the method of

Sect.
8.2

.

Four variables associated with long tracks are used for particle
identification: the number of muon stations with hits, the
\mathrm{PRS}
energy, the
\mathrm{ECAL}
energy over the momentum of the track, and the
\mathrm{HCAL}
energy over the momentum of the track. Distributions of these
variables, normalised to an integral of one, for muons, electrons, and
hadrons are shown in
Fig.
51

. The muon distributions are taken
from data events consistent with a
{Z\to\mu\mu}
signal, which are
selected by requiring an isolated muon and an isolated opposite-sign
track. Both the muon and track must have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, a combined
invariant mass within the range
{60\leq m\leq 120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, and an
azimuthal angle separation of greater than
2.7
radians. The
distributions are then produced from the variables of the isolated
track. The same method is used to obtain the electron distributions,
but a
{Z\to ee}
signal is selected by requiring isolated
electrons rather than muons. The charged hadron distributions are
obtained from long tracks in minimum bias data with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
.

The distributions of
Fig.
51

are intended to illustrate the
particle identification requirements and will be similar to, but not
the same, as the muon, electron, and charged hadron distributions from

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events which are unavailable directly from
data. Primarily, the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
spectrum of the electrons and muons used to
produce these distributions will be harder than the spectrum for
electrons and muons from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events. The vertical black
lines of
Fig.
51

indicate the particle identification
requirements used for selecting
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates and are
summarised in
Table
9.1

. A more detailed motivation of the
selection criteria for each particle type is given in the remainder of
this section.

9.1.1

Muon Identification

Muons are reconstructed as described in
Sect.
8.1

, where tracks
are extrapolated to the four muon stations downstream of the
calorimeters and matched to compatible hits. Muon candidates must have
an associated hit in each of the four muon stations, requiring the
candidate to traverse approximately twenty hadronic interaction
lengths of material. The high efficiency of this requirement can be
seen in the distribution of muon stations with associated hits for
muons in
Fig.
51(a)

, where nearly all muons have associated
hits in all four muon stations. Charged hadrons and electrons
typically have less than three stations with associated hits. Because
the muons from
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays have a lower
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
spectrum than the muons
from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events of
Fig.
51(a)

, the muon
identification efficiency for
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events is slightly
reduced.

9.1.2

Electron Identification

Electrons are reconstructed by extrapolating tracks into the
\mathrm{ECAL}
,
matching
\mathrm{ECAL}
clusters with the track, and then performing
bremsstrahlung recovery, as described in
Sect.
8.2

. The
\mathrm{ECAL}
cells saturate at transverse energies above
10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, also described
in
Sect.
8.2

, and so bremsstrahlung recovery for high
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
electrons from
Z
bosons is incomplete. The standard LHCb electron
identification requirements were designed for low
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
electrons from

B
-hadron decays, relying upon
\mathrm{RICH}
information, and so different
requirements suited for high momentum electrons are used instead.

High momentum electrons begin showering within the
\mathrm{PRS}
, but deposit
most of their energy within the
\mathrm{ECAL}
. Conversely, most hadrons do not
shower until the
\mathrm{HCAL}
, and muons typically do not shower. The

E_{\mathrm{PRS}}
distributions for muons and electrons with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}

and charged hadrons with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
are shown in

Fig.
51(b)

. An associated
\mathrm{PRS}
energy,
E_{\mathrm{PRS}}
, greater than

0.05~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
is required for electron identification, as most electrons
from
Z
boson decays will have energies well above this requirement.

The
\mathrm{ECAL}
and
\mathrm{HCAL}
energies are dependent upon the energy and
momentum of the electron and so the fractional
\mathrm{ECAL}
and
\mathrm{HCAL}
energy
with respect to the electron momentum are used as electron
identification variables. The
\mathrm{ECAL}
energy over momentum distribution
is shown in
Fig.
51(c)

and the
\mathrm{HCAL}
energy over momentum
distribution is shown in
Fig.
51(d)

for muons, electrons, and
pions. Electrons are expected to deposit a large fraction of their
momentum within the
\mathrm{ECAL}
, as can be seen in
Fig.
51(c)

, and
so electron candidates must satisfy
E_{\mathrm{ECAL}}/p>0.1
. Conversely,
electrons are not expected to reach the
\mathrm{HCAL}
and are required to have

E_{\mathrm{HCAL}}/p<0.05
, as shown in
Fig.
51(d)

. By requiring less
than three muon stations with associated hits, electron candidates by
definition are mutually exclusive to muon candidates.

9.1.3

Charged Hadron Identification

Charged hadrons are identified using a mutually exclusive selection to
both muons and electrons. They are expected to shower within the

\mathrm{HCAL}
, as described in
Sect.
7.2

, and so all charged hadron
candidates must satisfy
E_{\mathrm{HCAL}}/p>0.05
, in addition to the
requirement of less than three muon stations with associated hits. To
ensure coverage of the
\mathrm{HCAL}
, charged hadron candidates must also fall
within the reduced pseudo-rapidity range of
2.25\leq\eta\leq 3.75
.

9.2

Event Selection

Once reconstructed particles are identified using the criteria of

Sect.
9.1

,
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events from data can be selected. The
data used have been collected via a single muon trigger requiring
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and a single electron trigger requiring
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>15~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.
Further details on the trigger can be found in
Sect.
8.3

. The
selection of
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events is divided into five mutually
exclusive categories:
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
,
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
. The first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate is labelled by the first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
subscript, while
the second candidate is labelled by the second subscript; here
\mu
,

e
, and
h
indicate muons, electrons, and charged hadrons
respectively. The first three categories select signal events where
both
\tau
leptons decay leptonically, while the last two categories select
signal events where the first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays leptonically and the second

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays semi-leptonically into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
neutrino and single charged
hadron. No di-electron category is considered due to the
\mathrm{ECAL}
saturation described in
Sect.
8

which results in poor separation
of this signal from large backgrounds.

To eliminate large
\mathrm{QCD}
backgrounds with soft
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
spectra, the first

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate is required to have
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
while
the second candidate must have
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The following
additional trigger and particle identification requirements are
applied for each category.

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
:

Requires two
oppositely-charged muons, where the muon with the larger
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
is
considered the first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate. Either or both
muons can trigger the event.

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
:

Requires a muon and an
oppositely-charged electron. Only the muon can trigger the event.

{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
:

Requires an electron and an
oppositely-charged muon with
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}<20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. Either or both the
electron and muon can trigger the event.

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
:

Requires a muon and an
oppositely-charged hadron. Only the muon can trigger the event.

{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
:

Requires an electron and an
oppositely-charged hadron. Only the electron can trigger the event.

Additional selection requirements, dependent upon the category, are
also applied to further separate the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal from its
backgrounds. These variables, as well as the requirements placed on
them, are described in the remainder of this section. The selection
requirements are not optimised using multivariate techniques, but are
manually selected to adequately separate signal and background without
severely limiting signal statistics. For each variable, its
distributions for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events from simulation and data are
compared to validate simulation. The
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data events are
selected by requiring two opposite-sign muons with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}

and a combined invariant mass within the range
{80\leq m\leq 100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. If the simulation does not match well with data, it is
corrected to data and the distribution for this calibrated simulation
is also provided.

Distributions for the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal and its backgrounds,
described in the introduction of
Sect.
9

, are also given for
each variable. The
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
,
\mathrm{EWK}
,
{WW}
, and
{t\bar{t}}

distributions are taken from calibrated simulation, while the
\mathrm{QCD}
and

{Z\to\ell\ell}
background distributions are estimated from data
using the methods described in
Sect.
9.3

. All distributions are
normalised to an integral of one for comparison purposes and are
provided for only the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
event category.

9.2.1

Invariant Mass

The invariant mass of the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates is defined
as,

m\equiv\sqrt{(E_{1}+E_{2})^{2}-(\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{p}_{2})^{2}}

(188)

where
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are the energies of the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates and
\vec{p}_{1}
and
\vec{p}_{2}
are their
three-momenta. The momentum resolution observed in data is
underestimated in simulation, resulting in narrower mass distributions
from simulation. This effect can be seen by comparing the invariant
mass distribution from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data to the distribution from
simulation, as shown in
Fig.
52(a)

. The momentum
components,
p_{i}
, for a reconstructed particle in simulation are
calibrated using,

p_{i}={f_{\mathrm{shift}}}\left({p_{i}}_{\mathrm{gen}}+{f_{\mathrm{width}}}({p_{i}}_{\mathrm{rec}}-{p_{i}}_{\mathrm{gen}})\right)

(189)

where
p_{\mathrm{gen}}
is the generated momentum of the particle and
p_{\mathrm{rec}}

is the reconstructed momentum of the particle. The parameter
{f_{\mathrm{shift}}}
shifts the distribution, while
{f_{\mathrm{width}}}
adjusts the width of the
distribution. Values of
{{f_{\mathrm{shift}}}=0.998\pm 0.001}
and
{{f_{\mathrm{width}}}=2.0\pm 0.1}
were obtained by fitting the simulated invariant mass
distribution to the data distribution, resulting in the calibrated
distribution of
Fig.
52(a)

. This calibration is used
for all mass distributions taken from simulation and is assumed to
remain constant for the lower momentum range of the
Z\to{\tau\tau}

signals and backgrounds.

((a))

Figure 52
:
A comparison of the
invariant mass distributions between data (points), simulation
(red), and calibrated simulation (blue) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}

events. Invariant mass distributions for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The distributions from simulation are
corrected with the momentum calibration of
Eq.
189

and the
mass ranges excluded by the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
selection requirement are shaded
in grey.

((b))

((c))

Figure 52
:
A comparison of the
invariant mass distributions between data (points), simulation
(red), and calibrated simulation (blue) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}

events. Invariant mass distributions for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The distributions from simulation are
corrected with the momentum calibration of
Eq.
189

and the
mass ranges excluded by the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
selection requirement are shaded
in grey.

The invariant mass for a
Z
boson decay into a back-to-back
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair in
the transverse plane is,

m^{2}\geq\left(\sqrt{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}}+\sqrt{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}-({p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}-{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2})^{2}\geq 4{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}

(190)

where
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}
and
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}
are the transverse momenta of the two
\tau
leptons,
and
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are their masses. As
m_{Z}\gg m_{\tau}
, the
single charged visible decay products of the
\tau
leptons are approximately
collinear with their parent
\tau
leptons. Consequently, the inequality of

Eq.
190

also holds for the invariant mass of the decay products
from the
\tau
leptons.

Since
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs produced from
Z
bosons within LHCb fulfil the
assumptions of
Eq.
190

and
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
are required for the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates, the
invariant mass of the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products must be greater than

20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.
Figure
52(b)

shows the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
invariant
mass distribution for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category of events.

Of the five backgrounds, only the
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background fulfils
the assumptions of
Eq.
190

and consequently has no events below

20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The
{Z\to\mu\mu}
background distribution is shown in

Fig.
52(c)

and has a sharp peak at
90~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, corresponding
to the on-shell mass of the
Z
boson. The
{Z\to\mu\mu}
background
dominates the signal for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category, and so the mass window of

{80<m<100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
is excluded for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category only.

The invariant mass distribution for the
\mathrm{QCD}
background is given in

Fig.
52(b)

and shows that
\mathrm{QCD}
background events have a
significantly lower invariant mass than signal
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

events. For
\mathrm{EWK}
events, a single lepton with hard
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
from a
W
or

Z
boson is combined with a candidate from the underlying event to produce
an invariant mass distribution harder than the
\mathrm{QCD}
background, yet
softer than the signal, see
Fig.
52(b)

. In

Fig.
52(c)

the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
invariant mass distributions are given
for the
{t\bar{t}}
and
{WW}
backgrounds. The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates
from these events are produced from massive parents and produce
distributions with higher mass tails than either the
\mathrm{QCD}
or
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds.

9.2.2

Track Isolation

The track isolation associated to a candidate track,
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
, is defined as,

{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}\equiv\sum_{i}^{\mathrm{tracks}}{p_{x}}_{i}\oplus\sum_{i}^{\mathrm{tracks}}{p_{y}}_{i}

(191)

where the
x
and
y
-momenta of all tracks within a cone of
\Delta R\equiv\Delta\phi\oplus\Delta\eta<0.5
around the candidate
track are summed and then added in quadrature. Here,
\Delta\phi
and

\Delta\eta
are the differences in
\phi
and
\eta
, defined in

Sect.
7

, between the candidate and the track. The track of the
candidate itself is excluded from the sum. Physically,
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
is the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
of the vectorial sum of all tracks in a cone surrounding the candidate
and quantifies the charged isolation of the candidate. Note that a
large
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
corresponds to a poorly isolated track.

In
Fig.
53(a)

, the distributions of the maximum
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
of
the two muons from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data and simulation are
compared. The isolation of the two muon candidates is dependent
primarily upon the underlying event activity, which is underestimated
in simulation. Consequently, the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
distribution produced from
simulation is softer than that from data. The soft underlying event
cannot be calculated perturbatively, and must be modelled
phenomenologically in simulation, as described in
Chap.
2

. As there
is no simple relation between data and simulation, calibration of the

{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
distribution from simulation is not possible. However, the
efficiency of the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection is calculated from data, as described
in
Sect.
10.2

, and so the cross-section determination of

Sect.
10

does not depend upon the accuracy of the simulated
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
distribution.

((a))

Figure 53
:
A comparison of the
maximum track isolation distributions between data (points) and
simulation (red) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the
maximum track isolation between the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The
isolation range excluded by the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
selection requirement is
shaded in grey.

((b))

((c))

Figure 53
:
A comparison of the
maximum track isolation distributions between data (points) and
simulation (red) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the
maximum track isolation between the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The
isolation range excluded by the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
selection requirement is
shaded in grey.

Figures
53(b)

and
53(c)
show the distribution
of the maximum
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
of the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates for
signal and background events. The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates
produced from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
,
{WW}
, and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
events are
expected to be relatively isolated, as the candidates are produced
from the decays of massive electroweak bosons, and the jets from the
underlying event will be uncorrelated with the candidate
direction. For
{t\bar{t}}
events, the associated
b
-jets from the decay
of the
t
-quark slightly contaminate the isolation of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
product candidates.

For the
\mathrm{QCD}
backgrounds the candidates are produced from jet
activity, resulting in tracks that are not isolated, as can be seen

Fig.
53(b)

. Similarly, for the
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds, one of the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates typically is produced from a jet and is
not isolated. The
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds are separated from
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
signal events by requiring the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
for both
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
product candidates to be less than
2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
signals a harsher criteria of
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<1~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
is necessary as the
initial
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds are larger.

9.2.3

Azimuthal Separation

For
Z
bosons produced at the LHC, their
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
is typically small. In the
subsequent decay of the
Z
boson, transverse momentum must be conserved,
and so the two
Z
boson decay products are approximately back-to-back in
the transverse plane. For
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
decays
{m_{\tau}\ll m_{Z}}
, resulting in the decay products of the
\tau
leptons produced in a
collinear direction with their parent
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
, and so the decay products
of the two
\tau
leptons will also be back-to-back.

The azimuthal separation of the observed
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates,
{|\Delta\phi|}
, is defined as,

{|\Delta\phi|}\equiv\begin{cases}\left|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\right|&\mathrm{if}~\left|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\right|\leq\pi\\
2\pi-\left|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\right|&\mathrm{else}\\
\end{cases}

(192)

where values near
\pi~\mathrm{radians}
indicate events where the two

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are back-to-back. Here
\phi_{1}
and

\phi_{2}
are the azimuthal angles of the first and second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
product candidates. Given the definition of
Eq.
192

,
{|\Delta\phi|}
must
range between
0
and
\pi~\mathrm{radians}
.

A comparison between the
{Z\to\mu\mu}

{|\Delta\phi|}
distributions from
data and simulation is given in
Fig.
54(a)

. The
distribution is described by the decay kinematics of the
Z
boson which
are well modelled in simulation, and so there is good agreement
between data and simulation.

((a))

Figure 54
:
A comparison of the
azimuthal separation distributions between data (points) and
simulation (red) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the
azimuthal separation between the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates
for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and

\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The range excluded
by the azimuthal separation selection requirement is shaded in
grey.

((b))

((c))

Figure 54
:
A comparison of the
azimuthal separation distributions between data (points) and
simulation (red) for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the
azimuthal separation between the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates
for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and

\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The range excluded
by the azimuthal separation selection requirement is shaded in
grey.

The distributions of
{|\Delta\phi|}
are shown in
Figs.
54(b)

and

54(c)
for
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
signal and background events. The
back-to-back nature of
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
events
is apparent and these distributions are nearly identical, validating
the collinear approximation of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays. The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates from the
\mathrm{QCD}
background are primarily produced from the
same jet, and travel approximately in the same direction, producing
events with small
{|\Delta\phi|}
. However, the candidates can also be produced
from di-jet events, contributing to the high
{|\Delta\phi|}
tail of the
\mathrm{QCD}
distribution. The
{|\Delta\phi|}
distributions of the
\mathrm{EWK}
,
{t\bar{t}}
, and
{WW}
backgrounds are relatively flat, as expected due to the small
correlations between the production of the two candidates. For all
five event categories,
{|\Delta\phi|}>2.7~\mathrm{radians}
is required.

9.2.4

Impact Parameter Significance

The mean lifetime of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
is experimentally known to be
{0.2906\pm 0.0010~\mathrm{ps}}

[
pdg.12.1
]
and so
\tau
leptons produced from

Z
boson decays are expected to travel on the order of a centimetre within
LHCb before decaying. While the decay vertex position for a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decaying into a single visible track cannot be directly measured
within LHCb, the impact parameter of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate
can be measured. Here, the impact parameter,
\vec{r}
, is defined as
the vector of closest approach between the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidate track and the associated primary vertex. The associated
primary vertex is refitted, as described in
Sect.
8.1

, without
including the candidate track in the fit.

The uncertainty on the impact parameter resolution is dependent upon
the uncertainty of not only the track fit but also the primary vertex
location. However, by summing the signed impact parameters of the two

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates, the associated primary vertex
uncertainties can be effectively cancelled
[
aleph.92.1
,
ferrante.95.1
]
, yielding a variable more sensitive to the lifetime
of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
. The signed impact parameter,
r
, is defined as,

r\equiv\left|\vec{r}\right|\left(\frac{\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{p}\right)\cdot\hat{k}}{\left|\left(\vec{r}\times\vec{p}\right)\cdot\hat{k}\right|}\right)

(193)

where
\vec{p}
is the momentum of the track and
\hat{k}
is the

z
-direction unit vector. A diagram of the signed impact parameter is
shown in
Fig.

.

Figure 55
:
Schematic of the components used to build the
signed impact parameter,
r
, of
Eq.
193

. The diagram on the
left-hand side is in the plane of the particle momentum and impact
parameter. The diagram on the right-hand side is in the plane of

{\vec{r}\times\vec{p}}
and the
z
-axis.

Figure 55
:
Schematic of the components used to build the
signed impact parameter,
r
, of
Eq.
193

. The diagram on the
left-hand side is in the plane of the particle momentum and impact
parameter. The diagram on the right-hand side is in the plane of

{\vec{r}\times\vec{p}}
and the
z
-axis.

The summed impact parameter significance,
{\mathrm{IPS}}
, can then be written
as,

{\mathrm{IPS}}\equiv\frac{\left|r_{1}+r_{2}\right|}{\delta_{1}\oplus\delta_{2}}

(194)

where
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the signed impact parameters for the first
and second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates and
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}

are their associated uncertainties calculated from the track fit
covariance matrices.

For accurate simulation of the
{\mathrm{IPS}}
, correct modelling of the track
fit and its associated uncertainty is critical. The
{\mathrm{IPS}}
is
underestimated in simulation as demonstrated in

Fig.
56(a)

which plots the
{\mathrm{IPS}}
distributions for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events from data and simulation. The
{\mathrm{IPS}}
distribution
from simulation can be corrected to match the distribution from data
by multiplying the
{\mathrm{IPS}}
for each simulated event by a factor

{f_{\mathrm{scale}}}
. An
{f_{\mathrm{scale}}}
of
{1.12\pm 0.01}
is found to minimise the

\chi^{2}
between the data distribution and the corrected simulated
distribution, and is used to calibrate all simulated samples. This
calibration is assumed to propagate from the lower
{\mathrm{IPS}}
values of
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events to the higher
{\mathrm{IPS}}
values of
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

events.

((a))

Figure 56
:
A comparison of the
impact parameter significance distributions between data (points),
uncalibrated simulation (red), and calibrated (blue) simulation
for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the impact parameter
significance for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The
distributions from simulation are corrected with the
{f_{\mathrm{scale}}}
factor described in the text and the range excluded by the impact
parameter significance selection requirement is shaded in
grey.

((b))

((c))

Figure 56
:
A comparison of the
impact parameter significance distributions between data (points),
uncalibrated simulation (red), and calibrated (blue) simulation
for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the impact parameter
significance for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue), and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The
distributions from simulation are corrected with the
{f_{\mathrm{scale}}}
factor described in the text and the range excluded by the impact
parameter significance selection requirement is shaded in
grey.

In
Figure
56(b)

the
{\mathrm{IPS}}
distribution for
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

signal events of the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category is shown, as well as the
distributions for the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds. The signal
distribution has a longer tail than the
\mathrm{QCD}
distribution as most
particles produced within a jet are either stable or have a shorter
lifetime than the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
. However, heavy-flavour mesons can have longer
lifetimes than the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and produce the harder tail of the
\mathrm{QCD}
distribution. The
\mathrm{EWK}
background has a much harder
{\mathrm{IPS}}
spectrum than
either the signal or
\mathrm{QCD}
distributions, which could be a result of
the two candidates being produced from a separate electroweak boson
and jet in the event.

The
{\mathrm{IPS}}
distributions for the
{t\bar{t}}
,
{WW}
, and
{Z\to\mu\mu}

backgrounds are provided in
Fig.
56(c)

. The
{t\bar{t}}
background
distribution has a much harder tail than both the signal distribution
and all other background distributions. This is most probably caused
by one of the candidates being produced by a heavy-flavour meson decay
from one of the
b
-jets. The
{WW}
and
{Z\to\mu\mu}
distributions,
however, are both softer than the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal
distribution. The
{Z\to\mu\mu}
distribution is softer because both

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are produced directly from the same

Z
boson, while the
{WW}
distribution is softer because the lifetime of

W
bosons is approximately twelve orders of magnitude smaller than the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
.

For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories a requirement of
{\mathrm{IPS}}>9
is
applied, as indicated by the shaded grey areas of
Figs.
56(b)

and
56(c)
. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category this reduces the
dominant
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background, while for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories this requirement reduces the
\mathrm{QCD}
backgrounds. The
{\mathrm{IPS}}
requirement is not necessary for the cleaner
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories.

9.2.5

Transverse Momentum Asymmetry

While the selection requirements detailed above are sufficient for
separating signal from background for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
event categories, these requirements are inadequate in eliminating the

{Z\to\mu\mu}
background from
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
signal events. The transverse momentum
asymmetry is defined as,

{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}\equiv\frac{\left|{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}-{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}\right|}{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}+{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}}

(195)

where
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}
and
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}
are the transverse momenta of the first and
second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates respectively. The muons from
background
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events will have balanced
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, resulting
in a low
{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
while
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events have missing
neutrinos, oftentimes resulting in a larger
{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
.

((a))

Figure 57
:
A comparison of the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
asymmetry distributions between data (points) and simulation (red)
for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events. Distributions of the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
asymmetry
between the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates for

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
(red),
\mathrm{EWK}
(blue), and
\mathrm{QCD}
(green) events and

{t\bar{t}}
(orange),
{WW}
(magenta), and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
(cyan) events. The range excluded
by the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
asymmetry selection requirement is shaded in
grey.

9.3

Background Estimation

The selection criteria developed in
Sect.
9.2

are applied to data
to select
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events. In order to calculate the

{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section in
Sect.
10

the number
of background events in the selected data must be estimated. The
methods used to estimate the number of events from the backgrounds
categorised in the beginning of
Sect.
9

and shown in

Fig.
50

are now described.

The invariant mass distributions for
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
candidates from
data for all five event categories, together with the estimated
backgrounds which are described in the remainder of this section, are
given in
Fig.
58

. No events were observed in data above an
invariant mass of
120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The simulated signal is normalised to
the difference between the number of observed and estimated background
events. A summary of these values is given in

Table
9.3

. Further validation plots are provided in

Figs.

through
of
App.

for the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
and

\eta
distributions of the combined
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates, as
well as the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
and
\eta
distributions of the individual candidates
and the number of primary vertices for the event.
