9.3.1

QCD and EWK Backgrounds

The number of
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
background events given in

Table
9.3

are estimated from data. A signal-depleted data
sample is produced by applying the selection criteria of
Sect.
9.2

to data, but requiring the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates have the
same-sign charge. The distributions from the same-sign sample of the

{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
difference between the first and second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates,
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}-{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}}
, are fitted with
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
templates to determine the number of same-sign charge
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
events,
{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
and
{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
. The same-sign
\mathrm{QCD}
template is taken
from data fulfilling the selection requirements of
Sect.
9.2

, but
requiring
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}>10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for both
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product
candidates. The
\mathrm{EWK}
template is taken from simulation without the

{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection applied.

The
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{1}-{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{2}}
distributions from same-sign data, with their
respective
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
template fits, are given in

Fig.
59

for the five event categories. In
\mathrm{QCD}
events both

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are typically produced from the same
jet, resulting in a similar
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
for the two candidates and a small
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
difference. For
\mathrm{EWK}
events the first candidate produced is generally
from an electroweak boson and will have a hard
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, while the second
candidate will have a softer
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
from jet activity, and so the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
difference distribution is shifted upwards to larger
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
differences
than the
\mathrm{QCD}
distribution.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

((e))

Figure 59
:
The distributions of the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
difference between
the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates for opposite-sign data
(points) and fitted
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue) and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green)
templates. These fits are used to determine the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds, as described in the text, and are given for the

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,

{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and

{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories.

Figure 59
:
The distributions of the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
difference between
the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates for opposite-sign data
(points) and fitted
\mathrm{QCD}
(blue) and
\mathrm{EWK}
(green)
templates. These fits are used to determine the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds, as described in the text, and are given for the

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,

{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and

{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories.

The number of same-sign background events
{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
and
{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
are
extrapolated to the number of opposite-sign events,
N_{\mathrm{QCD}}
and

N_{\mathrm{EWK}}
, using,

N_{\mathrm{QCD}}={r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}},\quad\quad N_{\mathrm{EWK}}={r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}

(196)

where
r
is the ratio of opposite-sign to same-sign events evaluated
from data for the
\mathrm{QCD}
background and from simulation for the
\mathrm{EWK}
background. These
r
are calculated from the ratio of opposite-sign
to same-sign events satisfying the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
sample
requirements. The value of
r_{\mathrm{QCD}}
was found to remain consistent,
within statistical uncertainty, when varying the
\mathrm{QCD}
sample
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
requirement between
5
and
20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, and consequently is assumed to
remain valid for the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection requirements. A
cross-check with data was made for
r_{\mathrm{EWK}}
by selecting an
\mathrm{EWK}
sample from data using
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<1~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for the first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
product candidate and
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}>10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
for the second candidate.

The uncertainties on the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds are taken from the
uncertainties of the same-sign template fit,
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
, and
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
. The
uncertainties on
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
and
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
are statistical and uncorrelated
between the two backgrounds. The same-sign template fit requires the
sum of the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
events,
{{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}+{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}}
, to equal the total
number of same-sign data events,
S
, and so the fit uncertainty is
fully correlated between the two backgrounds. The combined number of

\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
background events, using
Eq.
196

is,

N_{\mathrm{QCD}+\mathrm{EWK}}={r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}+{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}={r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}S+{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}(1-{f_{\mathrm{fit}}})S

(197)

where
{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}
is defined as
{S_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}/S
. Assuming the uncertainties on

{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}
,
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
, and
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
are uncorrelated and normally distributed, the
uncertainty on
N_{\mathrm{QCD}+\mathrm{EWK}}
is,

\delta_{N_{\mathrm{QCD}+\mathrm{EWK}}}^{2}=\left({r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}S-{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}S\right)^{2}\delta_{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}^{2}+\left({f_{\mathrm{fit}}}S\right)^{2}\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}^{2}+\left(S-fS\right)^{2}\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}^{2}

(198)

where
\delta_{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}
,
\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
, and
\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
are the
uncertainties on
{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}
,
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}
, and
{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}
respectively.

In
Table
9.3

, the number of
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds, with
associated uncertainty, have been presented separately for
clarity. Here, the uncertainty for the two backgrounds from

Eq.
198

is split between the two backgrounds using the
arbitrary convention,

\displaystyle\delta_{N_{\mathrm{QCD}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}S-{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}S\right)^{2}\delta_{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}^{2}+\left({f_{\mathrm{fit}}}S\right)^{2}\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}^{2}

(199a)

\displaystyle\delta_{N_{\mathrm{EWK}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{QCD}}}}S-{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}S\right)^{2}\delta_{f_{\mathrm{fit}}}^{2}+\left(S-fS\right)^{2}\delta_{r_{\mathrm{\mathrm{EWK}}}}^{2}

(199b)

where the template fit uncertainty term is evenly divided between the

\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
background uncertainties.

9.3.2

\bm{Z\to\ell\ell}
Background

The number of
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background events, given in

Table
9.3

are evaluated only for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories and not for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories, as the background
for these categories is negligible. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
categories
the
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background consists of
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events,
while for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
category the
{Z\to\ell\ell}
background is from

{Z\to ee}
events.

In the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
invariant mass distribution from data of

Fig.
58(a)

, the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events from an
on-shell
Z
boson are clearly visible in the excluded mass range of
{80<m<100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The shape for the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
background is
obtained from data by applying the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
selection of
Sect.
9.2

,
but requiring
{\mathrm{IPS}}<1
to eliminate
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events. This
shape is then normalised to the number of
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
events within the
excluded mass range
{80<m<100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The uncertainty on the
number of
{Z\to\mu\mu}
background events is estimated from the
statistical uncertainty on the normalisation of the background sample
and is the primary systematic uncertainty for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category.

Events from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
process contribute a small background
to the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
category when one of the muons is mis-identified as a
hadron. The data sample for this background is found by applying the

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
requirements of
Sect.
9.2

, but requiring that the second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate fulfil the muon identification criteria of

Sect.
9.1

. The sample is scaled by the probability of
mis-identifying a muon as a hadron. The muon mis-identification
probability is determined from data where
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events are
selected by requiring a single well defined muon and a second isolated
track with a combined invariant mass within the range
{80<m<100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. Only
Phys. Rev. C
0.06
±
0.01 of the isolated tracks pass the
hadron identification requirement. The uncertainty on the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}

{Z\to\ell\ell}
background is estimated from the statistical
uncertainty of the background sample and the muon mis-identification.

For
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
events, a small
{Z\to ee}
background can contribute when
one of the two electrons is mis-identified as a hadron. The background
sample is found by applying the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
selection of
Sect.
9.2

to data,
but requiring the second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate to fulfil the
electron identification criteria of
Sect.
9.1

. The background
sample is scaled by the probability for an electron to be
misidentified as a hadron, which is determined from simulation to be

Phys. Rev. C
0.63
±
0.02. The uncertainty on the background is estimated as
the statistical uncertainty of the background sample and the electron
mis-identification.

9.3.3

\bm{WW}
and
\bm{t\bar{t}}
Backgrounds

Both the
{WW}
and
{t\bar{t}}
samples are estimated to be small for all
event categories as shown in
Table
9.3

. These backgrounds are
estimated from simulation which has been calibrated for the variables
described in
Sect.
9.2

and normalised to theoretical
cross-sections. Additionally, the simulation samples are corrected on
an event-by-event basis for the differences observed in the
reconstruction efficiencies between data and simulation. Details on
the reconstruction efficiencies are provided in
Sect.
10.1

.

10

Cross-Section

For consistency with the
{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{pp\to Z\to ee}
cross-section measurements published by the LHCb collaboration in
Refs.
[
lhcb.12.1
]

and
[
lhcb.13.2
]
, the
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section is evaluated in the kinematic region
{60<m_{\tau\tau}<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
,
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{\tau}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, and
{2.0\leq\eta_{\tau}\leq 4.5}
, where
\tau
indicates the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
before
decaying. The cross-section is calculated with,

\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}=\frac{\displaystyle\sum\limits{{}_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{obs}}}}\left({\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}_{i}\right)-\sum\limits{{}_{j}}\left({N_{\mathrm{bkg}}}_{j}\langle{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}\rangle_{j}\right)}{\displaystyle{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}\mathscr{L}{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}}

(200)

where
N_{\mathrm{obs}}
is the number of candidate events observed in data,

{N_{\mathrm{bkg}}}_{j}
is the number of estimated background events for each
background source
j
, and
\langle{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}_{j}\rangle
is the
average
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}
for each background source
j
. The
reconstruction efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}
, is calculated from data or
simulation and is dependent upon the momentum and pseudo-rapidity of
the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates for each event, while the event
selection efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}
, is an average efficiency for all
events. The integrated luminosity is given by
\mathscr{L}
, while

{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
is an acceptance and final state radiation
factor and
{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
is the branching fraction for the
event category.

The first summation with index
i
over all observed events, corrects
each event by the reconstruction efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}_{i}
,
evaluated for the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates of that event. The
second summation with index
j
over all background sources, is the
addition of the estimated number of events for each background source,
weighted by the average event reconstruction efficiency for the data
or simulation sample used to evaluate that background.

In
Sect.
10.1

, the methods used to calculate the reconstruction
efficiency are described, while in
Sect.
10.2

the selection
efficiency is calculated and in
Sect.
10.3

, the acceptance and
branching fractions are determined. The reconstruction efficiencies
are tabulated in
Table
10.1

of
Sect.
10.1

, while the
selection efficiencies are provided in
Table
10.2

of

Sect.
10.2

. The acceptance and branching fractions are given in

Table
10.3

of
Sect.
10.3

.

The integrated luminosity was determined using the Van de Meer
scan
[
meer.68.1
]
and beam-gas imaging
[
luzzi.05.1
]
methods
described in
Sect.
8.4

. These methods provide similar results
and so the integrated luminosity is taken as the average of the two
with an estimated uncertainty of
3.5\%

[
lhcb.12.4
]
. For the

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
, and
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
categories the integrated luminosity is
{1028\pm 28~\mathrm{pb}^{-1}}
, while the integrated luminosity for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
channels is
{955\pm 33~\mathrm{pb}^{-1}}
. The reduction in integrated
luminosity for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories is due to a change in the
electron triggers during the
2011
data-taking period.

10.1

Reconstruction Efficiency

The reconstruction efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}
, used in the cross-section
determination of
Eq.
200

, is defined as,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}\equiv{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{1}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{2}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{1}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{2}

(201)

where
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
is the global event cut (
\mathrm{GEC}
) efficiency,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}
the trigger efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}
the track finding
efficiency, and
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}
the particle identification efficiency.
The numerical subscripts indicate whether the efficiency is evaluated
for the first or second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate. A summary of
these component reconstruction efficiencies for muons, electrons, and
charged hadrons is given in
Table
10.1

. The component
efficiencies are calculated in the order indicated in

Eq.
201

, e.g. the muon identification efficiency is
determined for muons with reconstructed tracks from events passing the

\mathrm{GEC}
and single-muon trigger requirement.

Sect.

and the trigger
requirements for each category in

Sect.

.

The trigger efficiencies are evaluated individually for muons and
electrons and combined to determine the trigger efficiency for the
given event category. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories either the
first or second
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidate can trigger the event,
and so
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}
is,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}={\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{1}+{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{2}-{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{1}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{2}

(202)

where the numerical subscripts indicate the first or second
candidate. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories the first
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
product is required to trigger the event, and so the corresponding
trigger efficiency for the candidate type is used.

The lepton trigger, track finding, and identification efficiencies are
evaluated using tag-and-probe methods on
{Z\to\ell\ell}
events from
data. The
{Z\to\ell\ell}
events are selected by requiring a tag
lepton passing the full trigger, track, and identification
reconstruction requirements and a probe lepton passing looser
reconstruction requirements, where the requirement being tested is
omitted; oftentimes further event requirements are necessary to ensure
a pure
{Z\to\ell\ell}
sample. The efficiency is then calculated as
the percentage of probes passing the test requirement. The event
topologies for
{Z\to\ell\ell}
and
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
are nearly
identical except for the momenta of the final state candidates, due to
the decays of the
\tau
leptons, and so the lepton reconstruction efficiencies
from
{Z\to\ell\ell}
samples are evaluated only as a function of
lepton momentum, when practicable.

In the remainder of this section the methods used to determine the
reconstruction efficiencies are detailed. Plots of the trigger, track
finding, and identification efficiencies for muons, electrons, and
charged hadrons are also provided. In these plots the efficiency
determined from data is compared to the biased and unbiased
efficiencies from simulation. The biased efficiency is found by
applying the tag-and-probe method to the reconstructed level of
simulated events, while the unbiased efficiency is found directly from
the generator level of simulated events.

10.1.1

Global Event Cut

The global event cut is applied at the L
0
trigger, described in

Sect.
8.3

, to eliminate high multiplicity events which require
significant processing time, and the
\mathrm{GEC}
efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
, is
the probability for an event to pass the
\mathrm{GEC}
requirement. For the
single-muon and single-electron triggers, the
SPD
multiplicity for an event
is required to be less than
600
hits, while for the di-muon
trigger, the
SPD
multiplicity must be less than
900
hits.

In
Fig.
60(a)

the
SPD
distribution for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events from data requiring a di-muon trigger is given. The

{Z\to\mu\mu}
data is selected by requiring two opposite-sign muons
with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and an invariant mass within the range
{60\leq m\leq 120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The distribution for
{Z\to\mu\mu}

events from data requiring a single-muon trigger is also given in

Fig.
60(a)

, but normalised so the integral of the
distribution equals the integral for the di-muon trigger distribution
below
600
hits.

((a))

((b))

Figure 60
:
The
SPD
hit
distributions from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data events passing the
di-muon (points) and single-muon (cyan) triggers, where the
single-muon distribution is normalised to the di-muon distribution
below
600

SPD
hits. Between
600
and
900

SPD
hits the
di-muon distribution is fit with the
\Gamma
-function of

Eq.
203

(line).
The
SPD
hit
distribution from
{Z\to ee}
events passing the
single-electron trigger (cyan) normalised to the
{Z\to\mu\mu}

di-muon distribution (points), where the shift upwards of
20

SPD
hits is apparent in the
{Z\to ee}

distribution.

The tail of the di-muon trigger distribution of

Fig.
60(a)

is fit over the range
600
to
900

hits with a
\Gamma
-function,

\Gamma(x)\equiv P_{0}x^{P_{1}}e^{-\frac{x}{P_{2}}}

(203)

where
P_{0}
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
are free parameters. This function is
chosen as the
SPD
multiplicity should be roughly Poisson in
shape. The single-muon trigger
\mathrm{GEC}
efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
, is then
evaluated as the number of events in the di-muon trigger distribution
with less than
600
hits over the total number of events in the
distribution plus the integrated tail of
Eq.
203

. The
uncertainty on the efficiency is determined from the statistical
uncertainty of the di-muon distribution and the uncertainty on the
integral of the tail from the fit of the
\Gamma
-function.

The
SPD
multiplicity distribution for
{Z\to ee}
events from
data requiring a single-electron trigger is given in

Fig.
60(b)

, where the distribution has been normalised
to the integral below
600
hits of the di-muon trigger distribution
of
Fig.
60(a)

. The
{Z\to ee}
events from data
are selected by requiring opposite-sign electrons with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and an invariant mass within the range
{60\leq m\leq 120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The di-muon trigger distribution from
{Z\to\mu\mu}

events of
Fig.
60(a)

is also plotted in

Fig.
60(b)

to provide a comparison.

As can be seen in
Fig.
60(b)

, the
{Z\to ee}

single-eletron trigger distribution is shifted upwards by
20

SPD
hist with respect to the di-muon trigger distribution, due to
additional
SPD
activity in the event from early showering of the
electrons. The single-electron trigger
\mathrm{GEC}
efficiency is evaluated
with the same method used for the single-muon
\mathrm{GEC}
efficiency, but
with the di-muon distribution shifted upwards by
20
hits. The

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
for both muons and electrons is found to be approximately

95\%
. The muon
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
is used for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
, and
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
categories, while the electron
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GEC}}}
is used for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
and

{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories.

10.1.2

Muon and Electron Trigger

The muon trigger efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{\mu}
, is evaluated using a
tag-and-probe method on
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events from data and is the
probability for a muon to pass the triggers of
Sect.
8.3

. The
tag is a muon passing trigger, track, and identification requirements,
while the probe is a muon passing only the track and identification
requirements. The tag and probe are required to have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, opposite charge, and a combined invariant mass within the
range
{60<m<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The efficiency is calculated as the
number of probes passing the muon trigger requirement over the total
number of probes, and is evaluated as a function of the probe muon
momentum in bins of
50~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
within the range
0
to
500~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
.

In
Fig.
61(a)

the efficiency is plotted for data,
biased simulation, and unbiased simulation, and ranges from
87\%
to

93\%
for the data determined efficiency. The uncertainty on the
efficiencies is determined from the statistical uncertainty on the
number of events used to determine the efficiency for each bin. The
biased and unbiased efficiencies match within uncertainty, indicating
no bias is introduced via the event requirements.

((a))

((b))

Figure 61
:
The muon trigger
efficiency as a function of muon momentum from

{Z\to\mu\mu}
data (points), biased simulation (red), and
unbiased simulation (blue). The
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{\mu}
is taken from
the data-driven
efficiency.
The electron trigger
efficiency as a function of electron momentum from
{Z\to ee}

data (points), biased simulation (red), and unbiased simulation
(blue). The
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{e}
is taken from the data-driven
efficiency.

The electron trigger efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{e}
, is the probability
for an electron to pass the triggers of
Sect.
8.3

and is
evaluated using a tag-and-probe method similar to the method for
muons, but now on
{Z\to ee}
events from data. The
{Z\to ee}
sample, if selected using the same requirements as the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
trigger efficiency sample, is contaminated by a
background on the order of
5\%
. This lowers
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trg}}}_{e}
on the
percent level and so stricter requirements on the the tag and probe of

{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and a combined invariant mass within the range
{70<m<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
are required. Additionally, the tag electrons must
be isolated with
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
.

Again, the efficiency is evaluated as a function of probe momentum in
bins of
50~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
within the range
0
to
500~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and is given in

Fig.
61(b)

. The efficiency is found to vary from

65\%
to
72\%
. No bias is observed between the simulated
samples. The uncertainty for each bin is evaluated from the
statistical uncertainties of the data sample and is the primary
systematic uncertainty for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
category cross-section measurement.

10.1.3

Muon Track Finding

The muon track finding efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{\mu}
, is the
probability for a muon to have a reconstructed track fulfilling the
requirements of
Sect.
9.1

. The efficiency is determined from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data using the tag-and-probe method diagrammed in

Fig.
62(b)

, first proposed in

Ref.
[
lhcb.10.1
]

. The tag is a muon passing the trigger, track
finding, and identification requirements. The probe is a track
reconstructed from hits within the
\mathrm{TT}
and muon system, as neither
set of hits is used in the initial reconstruction of the muon
track. The tag and probe are required to have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and
opposite charge with a separation of
{{|\Delta\phi|}>1~\mathrm{radians}}
to
ensure the tag and probe are not produced from the same muon. The tag
and probe are also required to be produced from the same vertex with a

\chi^{2}
less than
5
.

((a))

((b))

Figure 62
:
The muon track finding
efficiency as a function of muon momentum from
{Z\to\mu\mu}

data (points),
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
data (grey points with
uncertainty given by fill), biased simulation (red), and unbiased
simulation (blue). The first three
J/\psi
bins and the remaining
Z
bins are used for
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{\mu}
.

A schematic, in the bending
xz
-plane of the detector, for the
tag-and-probe method used to determine the
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{\mu}
from
data.

The probe is matched with tracks fulfilling the requirements of

Sect.
9.1

. If more than
40\%
of the
\mathrm{TT}
and muon system hits
from the probe match the hits from the track, the probe is considered
to have an associated reconstructed track. The tracking efficiency is
evaluated as the number of probes with a reconstructed track over the
total number of probes, and is given as a function of probe momentum
in
Fig.
62(a)

with bins of
50~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
from
0
to

500~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The large difference between the efficiencies from data
and simulation is primarily due to poor modeling of the probability

\chi^{2}
variable in simulation. A slight bias between the biased and
unbiased efficiencies from simulation is introduced by the selection
used to obtain the tag-and-probe sample, but is less than the
uncertainty on the efficiency evaluated from data. The uncertainty is
estimated as the statistical uncertainty on the sample used to
calculate each bin.

The
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
requirement eliminates low momentum probes, and no
efficiencies from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data events are available for the
probe momentum range of
0
to
100~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
in

Fig.
62(a)

. To access this low momentum range, the
same tag-and-probe method is applied to low mass
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}

events. Now, the tag is required to have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>1.3~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and the
combined tag and probe are required to have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>1~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
with an
invariant mass within the range
{3.0<m<3.2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and vertex

{\chi^{2}<5}
. The
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
sample is not pure, and so the

J/\psi
mass peak must be fit to determine the number of signal
events. Two different fit methods are used, and the difference between
the tracking efficiencies determined from the two fits is estimated as
the systematic uncertainty. Further details can be found in

App.

.

The pseudo-rapidity distribution of
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
events differs
from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events due to the mass difference between the
on-shell
Z
boson and
J/\psi
masses. Consequently, the muon tracking
efficiency from
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
events is evaluated as a function
of both probe
\eta
and
p
. The efficiency is reduced to only a
function of
p
by taking the weighted average of the
\eta
bins for
a given
p
, where the weight for each
\eta
bin is taken from the

{Z\to\mu\mu}
pseudo-rapidity distribution. The resultant
efficiency from
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
events is given in

Fig.
62(a)

and matches the overlapping
{Z\to\mu\mu}
efficiencies, within uncertainty. The uncertainty on this
efficiency is the combination of the systematic uncertainty from
fitting the
J/\psi
peak and the statistical uncertainty of the
sample. This uncertainty is indicated by the grey error bands of

Fig.
62(a)

.

The
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{\mu}
used in
Eq.
201

and given in

Table
10.1

is evaluated from the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
efficiency
for muon momenta between
0
to
150~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
efficiency for momenta between
150
and
500~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The
efficiency is found to range from
87\%
to
93\%
.

10.1.4

Electron Track Finding

The electron track finding efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{e}
, is the
probability that an electron produces a reconstructable track, and is
determined using the tag-and-probe method diagrammed in

Fig.
63(b)

on
{Z\to ee}
events from
data. The tag is an electron passing the trigger, track finding, and
identification requirements of
Sect.
9.1

with a
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
,
while the probe is an
\mathrm{ECAL}
cluster with an
{{E_{\mathrm{T}}}>5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The tag
is required to be isolated with
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and the
\mathrm{HCAL}
energy
in a cone of
{\Delta R<5}
about the probe must be less than
50\%

of the
\mathrm{ECAL}
energy in the same cone. The tag and probe are required
to be back-to-back in the azimuthal plane with
{{|\Delta\phi|}>3~\mathrm{radians}}
and the difference in
\mathrm{ECAL}

{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
between the
probe and tag must be less than
20\%
of the
\mathrm{ECAL}

{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
of the probe.

((a))

((b))

((c))

Figure 63
:
Template fit of the electron tag
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution from electron tracking tag-and-probe data (points)
with
{Z\to ee}
(cyan) and
\mathrm{QCD}
events (blue) from data

before requiring a test track and

after.
A
schematic, in the bending
xz
-plane of the detector, for the
tag-and-probe method used to determine
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{e}
from
data.

Figure 63
:
Template fit of the electron tag
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution from electron tracking tag-and-probe data (points)
with
{Z\to ee}
(cyan) and
\mathrm{QCD}
events (blue) from data

before requiring a test track and

after.
A
schematic, in the bending
xz
-plane of the detector, for the
tag-and-probe method used to determine
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{e}
from
data.

This selection results in a sample of
{Z\to ee}
events
contaminated with a small
\mathrm{QCD}
background. The purity of the sample is
determined by fitting the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution of the tag with
{Z\to ee}
and
\mathrm{QCD}
templates. For
{Z\to ee}
events a peak is
expected in the tag
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution at half the on-shell mass of the

Z
boson, while a much softer distribution is expected from
\mathrm{QCD}
events. The
{Z\to ee}
template is constructed by selecting
events from data with two identified electrons with opposite charge
and a combined invariant mass in the range
{60<m<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The

\mathrm{QCD}
template is taken from data using the same requirements as the
tag-and-probe sample, but omitting the tag isolation, azimuthal
separation, and
{E_{\mathrm{T}}}
balance requirements. Additionally, the
sub-leading
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
track in the event must have the same charge as the
leading
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
tag.

The fit of the
{Z\to ee}
tag-and-probe
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution with the

{Z\to ee}
and
\mathrm{QCD}
templates, is given in

Fig.
63(a)

, resulting in a signal purity of
approximately
70\%
. The tag-and-probe sample is tested for electron
track finding by requiring a track, fulfilling the requirements of

Sect.
9.1

, with opposite charge and an azimuthal separation of

{{|\Delta\phi|}>2.5~\mathrm{radians}}
with the tag. The fit of this
distribution is given in
Fig.
63(c)

and is estimated to
have a signal purity of approximately
80\%
. As expected, both the
total number of events and the
\mathrm{QCD}
background in this distribution
are reduced.

The electron track finding efficiency is calculated as the number of

{Z\to ee}
signal events from
Fig.
63(c)

, after
requiring a track, over the number of signal events from

Fig.
63(a)

, prior to requiring a track, and is found to
be
Phys. Rev. C
83.0
±
0.3. The uncertainty is estimated from the fit
uncertainty combined in quadrature with the statistical uncertainty of
the efficiency. While no momentum information is available, the
electron track finding efficiencies from simulated
{Z\to ee}
and

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events are found to be in statistical agreement with
values of
Phys. Rev. C
85.2
±
0.2 and
Phys. Rev. C
85.7
±
4.9,
respectively. These two efficiencies demonstrate that while the
electron tracking efficiency is estimated from generator level
simulation to have a momentum dependence, the net effect is small. No
additional systematic uncertainty is included in the
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}_{e}

uncertainty for this effect. The
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}_{e}
uncertainty is the
dominant systematic uncertainty for the cross-section measurements of
the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories, as well as a large systematic
uncertainty for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
category cross-section measurement.

10.1.5

Charged Hadron Track Finding

The charged hadron track finding efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{trk}}}_{h}
, is
the probability for a charged hadron to produce a reconstructable
track. Prior to the final tracking station, particles pass through
approximately
20\%
of a hadronic interaction length of material,
resulting in the early showering of charged hadrons caused by their
nuclear interactions with the detector material. Consequently, the
muon track finding efficiency is used as the charged hadron track
finding efficiency, but with a correction for additional material
interactions which is estimated from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation to
be
Phys. Rev. C
84.3
±
1.5. The uncertainty on this correction factor is
from the uncertainty of the LHCb material budget of

10\%

[
lhcb.12.3
]
.

The grey points in
Fig.

are the muon track
finding efficiency from
Fig.
62(a)

as a function of
momentum, corrected for material interactions, and are used as the
hadron track finding efficiency. The grey bands indicate the
uncertainty on the efficiency which is the combination of the muon
track finding efficiency uncertainty and the correction
uncertainty. The uncorrected muon track finding efficiency from data
is also plotted in
Fig.

, as well as the
unbiased muon and charged hadron track finding efficiencies from
simulation. The hadron track finding efficiency ranges from values of

73\%
to
79\%
.

Figure 64
:
The charged hadron track finding efficiency
(points with fill) determined from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}

data-driven muon track finding efficiency (points) corrected for
hadronic nuclear interactions. The associated uncertainty is the
LHCb material budget uncertainty combined in quadrature with the
muon track finding uncertainty. The unbiased muon (red) and
charged hadron (blue) track finding efficiencies from simulation
are shown for comparison.

Figure 64
:
The charged hadron track finding efficiency
(points with fill) determined from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}

data-driven muon track finding efficiency (points) corrected for
hadronic nuclear interactions. The associated uncertainty is the
LHCb material budget uncertainty combined in quadrature with the
muon track finding uncertainty. The unbiased muon (red) and
charged hadron (blue) track finding efficiencies from simulation
are shown for comparison.

10.1.6

Muon Identification

The muon identification efficiency is the probability for a muon with
a track to pass the muon identification requirements of

Sect.
9.1

. Here, the muon is already required to have a
reconstructed track from hits within the
\mathrm{VELO}
and
\mathrm{TT}
stations and a
search for hits from the muon system is performed, in contrast to the
muon track finding efficiency where a track is not required a
priori and hits from the muon system are not required. The muon
identification efficiency is evaluated from data using a tag-and-probe
method on
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data, diagrammed in

Fig.
65(b)

, where the tag is a muon passing the
trigger, track, and identification requirements and the probe is a
muon passing the track requirements. Additionally, the tag and probe
are required to have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, an isolation of
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, opposite charge, and a combined invariant mass within the
range
{60<m<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The efficiency is then taken as the
number of probes with associated hits in each of the four outermost
muon stations over the total number of probes.

((a))

((b))

Figure 65
:
The muon
identification efficiency as a function of muon momentum from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data (points),
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
data (points with
fill), biased simulation (red), and unbiased
simulation (blue). The first three
J/\psi
bins and the remaining
Z
bins are used for

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{\mu}
.
A schematic,
in the bending
xz
-plane of the detector, for the tag-and-probe
method used to determine
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{\mu}
from
data.

The muon identification efficiency from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events is
determined with respect to the probe momentum, and is plotted in

Fig.
65(a)

. Due to the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
requirements on the tag
and probe, the low momementum bins between
0
and
150~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
must be
suplemented using efficiencies from
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
events. For
the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
sample, the probe must have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>1.5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}

and
{p>6~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
while the tag must have
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>0.8~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and
{p>3~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. The combined invariant mass of the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}

tag and probe is required to be within the range
3.0<m<3.2
and
the vertex
\chi^{2}
for the event must be less than
8
.

Just as for the muon track finding efficiency, the pseudo-rapidity
distributions between the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
and
{Z\to\mu\mu}

samples differ. Consequently, the muon identification efficiency from

{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
events is evaluated as a function of probe
\eta

and
p
. This efficiency is reduced to a function of only
p
using
the same weighted average method as the muon track finding efficiency
and is given in
Fig.
65(a)

. The uncertainty on this
efficiency is the combination of the statistical uncertainty for the
sample and the uncertainty from the fit of the
J/\psi
peak.

The two overlapping bins between the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
and
{Z\to\mu\mu}
tag-and-probe efficiencies match within uncertainty,
although the uncertainty for the final
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
bin is
large due to poor fits from low statistics. Additionally, the decrease
in efficiency at low momentum for the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
tag-and-probe
efficiency arises from minor background contamination in the data
sample. There is good agreemant between the biased and unbiased
efficiencies from simulation, verifying no bias has been introduced by
the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
selection requirements. The biased efficiency is
determined from applying the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
tag-and-probe method to
simulation, while the unbiased efficiency is taken directly from the
generator level information in simulation. The muon identification
efficiency, used in
Eq.
201

and given in
Table
10.1

, is
taken from the
{J/\psi\to\mu\mu}
data for
{0<p<150~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and
from the
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data for
{150<p<500~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, with values
ranging from
93\%
to
99\%
.

10.1.7

Electron Identification

The probability for an electron with a track fulfulling the
requirements of
Sect.
9.1

to pass the electron identification
requirements is given by the electron identification efficiency,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{e}
. This efficiency is determined using the tag-and-probe
method of
Fig.
66(b)

on
{Z\to ee}
events from
data. The tag is an electron passing the trigger, track, and
identification requirements with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, while the probe is
a track, also with
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. Both the tag and probe are
required to be isolated with
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<2~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, have a combined
invariant mass within the range
{60<m<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, and an
azimuthal separation of
{{|\Delta\phi|}>3.0~\mathrm{radians}}
.

((a))

((b))

Figure 66
:
The electron
identification efficiency as a function of electron momentum from

{Z\to ee}
data (points), biased simulation (red), and
unbiased simulation (blue). The
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{e}
is taken as the
unbiased efficiency with the total efficiency difference between
data and biased simulation estimated as the associated uncertainty
(grey fill).
A schematic, in the
bending
xz
-plane of the detector, for the tag-and-probe method
used to determine
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{e}
from data.

The efficiency is then calculated as the number of probes passing the
electron identification requirements over the total number of probes,
and is plotted as a function of probe momemntum in

Fig.
66(a)

. A large bias is introduced by the
tag-and-probe selection requirements, as can be seen by the
disagreemant between the biased and unbiased efficiencies from
simulation. Relaxing the selection requirements reduces the bias, but
introduces background contamination to the
{Z\to ee}
signal.

However, the efficiency from data matches the efficiency from biased
simulation, and so the unbiased efficiency from simulation is taken to
describe the inaccessible unbiased efficiency from data. Consequently,
the electron identification efficiency used in
Eq.
201

and
given in
Table
10.1

is evaluated as the unbiased efficiency from
simulation, and ranges between values of
79\%
to
93\%
. The
uncertainty is estimated as the difference between the total biased
efficiencies from simulation and data, combined in quadrature with the
statistical uncertainty from simulation. This uncertainty is shown in

Fig.
66(a)

as the grey band about the unbiased
efficiency.

Figure 67
:
Hadron identification efficiency as a function
of hadron pseudo-rapidity from minimum bias data (points), biased
simulation (red), and unbiased simulation (blue). The drop-off in
efficiency at low and high pseudo-rapidity is due to
\mathrm{HCAL}
geometrical acceptance.

Figure 67
:
Hadron identification efficiency as a function
of hadron pseudo-rapidity from minimum bias data (points), biased
simulation (red), and unbiased simulation (blue). The drop-off in
efficiency at low and high pseudo-rapidity is due to
\mathrm{HCAL}
geometrical acceptance.

10.1.8

Hadron Identification

The charged hadron identification,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{h}
, is the
probability for a hadron with a reconstructed track fulfilling the
requirements of
Sect.
9.1

to pass the charged hadron particle
identification requirements. Events selected using a minimum bias
trigger from data are assumed to consist primarily of charged hadrons,
validated with simulation, and so this efficiency is taken as the
percentage of events where the highest
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
track in the event, with a
minimum
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
of
{5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
passes the charged hadron identification
requirements. The efficiency as a function of hadron momentum is flat,
but is highly dependent upon
\eta
due to the acceptance of the

\mathrm{HCAL}
, as can be seen in
Fig.
67

. Consequently,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{id}}}_{h}
is evaluated from minimum bias data in
pseudo-rapidity bins of
0.125
and found to range from
92\%
to

96\%
.

10.2

Selection Efficiency

The event selection efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}
, is the probability for an
event with two reconstructed
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates to pass the
selection requirements of
Sect.
9.2

summarised in

Table
9.2.5

. The event selection efficiency is defined as,

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}\equiv{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}~{\varepsilon_{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}~{\varepsilon_{|\Delta\phi|}}~{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{IPS}}}~{\varepsilon_{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}

(204)

where each component is the efficiency for a reconstructed event to
pass the corresponding selection of
Table
9.2.5

. The invariant mass
selection efficiency is excluded, as this efficiency is by definition
one. However, a kinematic efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}
, is included which
is the probability for a true reconstructable event passing the
kinematic requirements of
Sect.
9.2

to have its reconstruction
also fulfil the same kinematic requirements. Each component efficiency
from
Eq.
204

is determined from either data or simulation
calibrated to data, with the component values for each event category
tabulated in
Table
10.2

. More details on the determination for
each component efficiency are provided in the remainder of this
section.

9.2

.

10.2.1

Kinematic

The kinematic efficiency is found by applying the pseudo-rapidity,
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
,
and invariant mass requirements of
Sect.
9.2

to simulated
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events. The efficiency is defined as the number of events
fulfilling these requirements at the reconstructed level of simulation
divided by the number of events fulfilling the requirements at the
generated level of simulation. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
categories

{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}
is found to be consistant with unity, as these variables
are well reconstructed for both muons and hadrons. Because of
\mathrm{ECAL}
saturation, described in
Sect.
8.2

, the brehmsstralung recovery
for high
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
electrons is incomplete, and their reconstructed momenta
is lower than their generated momenta. This leads to low kinematic
efficiencies for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories, on the order of
70\%
,
and an efficiency near one for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
category, as given in

Table
10.2

.

Figure 68
:
The
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution of
{Z\to ee}
events
from data (points), uncalibrated simulation (red), and calibrated
simulation (blue). The calibrated distribution of the electron
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
is scaled by a factor of
{1.02\pm 0.01}
and is applied when
evaluating
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}
for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories.

Figure 68
:
The
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution of
{Z\to ee}
events
from data (points), uncalibrated simulation (red), and calibrated
simulation (blue). The calibrated distribution of the electron
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
is scaled by a factor of
{1.02\pm 0.01}
and is applied when
evaluating
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}
for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
,
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories.

When calculating
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}
for the categories containing an
electron, the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distribution of the electrons is scaled by a factor
of
1.02\pm 0.01
. The scale is determined from a fit between the
electron
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
distributions of
{Z\to ee}
events from simulation
and data, where the procedure for the fit is similar to the invariant
mass fit of
Sect.
9.2

; the result of the fit is given in

Fig.

. The associated uncertainties for the
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{kin}}}

values are estimated as the propogated electron
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
scale uncertainty
combined in quadrature with the statistical uncertainty from
simulation.

10.2.2

Track Isolation

The track isolation variable of
Eq.
191

,
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
, is not well
described by simulation, as previously shown in

Fig.
53(a)

, due to an underestimation of the underlying
event. However, the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
distributions for
{Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{Z\to{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}}
events from simulation match, as the underlying event
topologies are identical. Consequently, the track isolation selection
efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
, for each category is calculated from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation and calibrated to data by the ratio of the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection efficiencies from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data to
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

simulation. The uncertainty for
{\varepsilon_{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
is estimated as the
difference between the
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection efficiencies evaluated using
events from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{Z\to{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}}
simulation.

The
{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}
selection efficiencies for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories are given in
Table
10.2

and range from approximately

Phys. Rev. C
60-70. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
categories, both with an electron
in the final state, the efficiency is slightly lower than the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category due to contamination of the electron candidate isolation by
pair production from brehmstrahlung photons. The harsher isolation
requirement for the semi-leptonic
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories,
{{I_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}<1~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, results in an efficiency of approximately
40\%
which is
slighly lower for the
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
category, again as a result of
brehmstrahlung radiation from the electron.

10.2.3

Azimuthal Angle and Transverse Momentum Asymmetry

Both the variables of azimuthal angle separation and transverse
momentum asymmetry are well described by simulation, as previously
shown in
Figs.
54(a)

and

57(a)
. The two selection efficiencies,

{\varepsilon_{|\Delta\phi|}}
and
{\varepsilon_{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
are determined from simulation and are
given for each category in
Table
9.2.5

. The
{\varepsilon_{|\Delta\phi|}}
is found to
be approximately
85\%
for all categories, while the
{\varepsilon_{A_{p_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
is
found to be approximately
60\%
for
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
events. The associated
uncertainty for each category is estimated as the difference between
these efficiencies evaluated in
{Z\to\mu\mu}
data and simulation,
combined in quadrature with the statistical uncertainty on the
efficiency from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation.

10.2.4

Impact Parameter Significance

The impact parameter signficance requirement efficiency,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{IPS}}}
,
is calculated from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation using the
{\mathrm{IPS}}
calibration of
Sect.
9.2

, with the agreemant between data and
calibrated simulation previously shown in

Fig.
56(a)

. The uncertainty on
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{IPS}}}
is
determined by re-calculating the efficiency in simulated
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events, where the calibration factor has been varied within
uncertainty. This uncertainty is the dominant systematic uncertainty
for the cross-section measurement of the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
category. For the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
,

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
, and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories the
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{IPS}}}
is on the order of
40\%
, as
given in
Table
10.2

.

10.3

Acceptance and Branching Fractions

Without an acceptance factor,
{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
, the cross-section
calculation of
Eq.
200

would yield a measurement dependent upon
the the kinematic requirements of
Sect.
9.2

on the
pseudo-rapidities, transverse momenta, and combined invariant mass of
the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products for each category. Consequently, to allow
comparison between these cross-section measurements and the
{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{pp\to Z\to ee}
cross-sections of

Refs.
[
lhcb.12.1
]

and
[
lhcb.13.2
]
, the acceptance factor corrects
the kinematics for each category to
{60<m_{\tau\tau}<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
,

{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{\tau}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, and
{2.0\leq\eta_{\tau}\leq 4.5}
with no

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
final state radiation. The acceptance factor is taken from
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation and is defined for each category as the
number of events, after electroweak final state radiation, passing the

\eta
,
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, and
m
requirements of
Sect.
9.2

over the number of
events, before electroweak final state radiation, passing the
requirements
{60<m_{\tau\tau}<120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
,
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{\tau}>20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
,
and
{2.0\leq\eta_{\tau}\leq 4.5}
.

Simulation samples are generated for each category using
\textsc{Pythia}~8

[
sjostrand.06.1
,
sjostrand.08.1
,
pythia.13.2
]
at leading order,
Herwig
++

[
bahr.08.1
,
herwig.13.1
]
at leading order, and
Herwig
++
at
next-to-leading order using the
Powheg
method

[
frixione.07.1
]
. The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays in
\textsc{Pythia}~8
are simulated
using the methods of
Chap.
3

with full spin correlations, while the

\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays in
Herwig
++
are also decayed with full spin
correlations and decay models as outlined in

Ref.
[
grellscheid.07.1
]

. The CTEQ
6
L
1
leading-order
\mathrm{PDF}
set

[
lai.99.1
]
was used with
\textsc{Pythia}~8
while the MSTW
08

\mathrm{PDF}
sets

[
martin.09.1
]
were used with
Herwig
++
. A sufficient number of
events were generated to ensure the statistical uncertainties for each
sammple are much less than the associated systematic uncertainties.

\captionabove

The acceptances

{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
, and branching fractions

{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
, as a percent, for each
of the five categories.

The acceptance factors for each category are given in
Table
10.3

and are calculated as the mean of the maximum and minimum acceptances
from the three samples. The uncertainty is taken as half the
difference between the maximum and minimum values. Because the three
samples encompass different
\mathrm{PDF}
sets,
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay and correlation
mechanisms, hard matrix elements, intial state radiation, and final
state radiation, this uncertainty determination is expected to provide
a conservative estimate.

The
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
acceptance of
Table
10.3

is the largest of the five
categories, as both muons can fulfill either of the two
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
requirements. The remaining acceptances are smaller than the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
acceptance as the two
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay product candidates are not the same
particle type. The
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
acceptance is smaller than the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
acceptance
because of the additional
{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{\mu}<20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
requirement. The
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
acceptances are also smaller than the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
acceptance,
primarily from the additional
{2.25\leq\eta_{h}\leq 3.75}

requirement.

The branching fractions for each category, given in
Table
10.3

, are
calculated using the world averaged
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay branching fractions of

Ref.
[
pdg.12.1
]

,

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}}={\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\mu^{-}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}}{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\mu^{-}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}},&{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}},{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}}}=2{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\mu^{-}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}}{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}e^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e}}}\\[5.69046pt]
{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}}}=2{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}\mu^{-}\bar{\nu}_{\mu}}}{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}h^{-}\geq 0h^{0}}},&{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}}}=2{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}e^{-}\bar{\nu}_{e}}}{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}h^{-}\geq 0h^{0}}}\\
\end{array}

(205)

where
{\mathcal{B}_{{\tau^{-}\to\nu_{\tau}}h^{-}\geq 0h^{0}}}
is the branching fraction of
a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
to a single charged hadron with zero or more neutral
hadrons. The uncertainty on the branching fraction for each category
is propogated from the uncertainties on the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
branching fractions,
assuming the uncertainty for each unique channel is uncorrelated and
normally distributed.

11

Results

The cross-sections for each category are determined using
Eq.
200

and the values presented in ﬁZed:Events,
10.1
,

10.2
, and
10.3
. These cross-sections are
calculated for the production of
Z
bosons, with photon interference, from
proton-proton collisions at
{\sqrt{s}=7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}}
where the
Z
boson mass
is between
60
and
120~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and the
Z
boson decays into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair,
both within the pseudo-rapidity range
{2.0\leq\eta\leq 4.5}
and
with transverse momenta greater than
20~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. A summary of the
systematic uncertainties for these values propogated to percentage
uncertainties on the cross-section is given in

Table
11

. The results for each event category are,

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}~({\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}})

\displaystyle=~77.4\pm 10.4\pm 8.6\pm 2.7~\mathrm{pb}

(206)

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}~({\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}})

\displaystyle=~75.2\pm\phantom{1}5.4\pm 4.1\pm 2.6~\mathrm{pb}

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}~({\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}})

\displaystyle=~64.2\pm\phantom{1}8.2\pm 4.9\pm 2.2~\mathrm{pb}

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}~({\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}})

\displaystyle=~68.3\pm\phantom{1}7.0\pm 2.6\pm 2.4~\mathrm{pb}

\displaystyle\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}~({\tau_{e}\tau_{h}})

\displaystyle=~77.9\pm 12.2\pm 6.1\pm 2.7~\mathrm{pb}

where the first uncertainty is statistical, the second is systematic,
and the third is due to the uncertainty on the integrated luminosity.
