A fit of the five cross-sections is performed using the method of the
best linear unbiased estimator
[
lyons.88.1
]
with further details
given in
App.

. A combined result of,

\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}=72.3\pm 3.5\pm 2.9\pm 2.5

(207)

with a
\chi^{2}
per degrees of freedom of
0.40
is obtained. Each
category is a mutually exclusive dataset, and so the statistical
uncertainties were assumed to be uncorrelated, while the luminosity
and shared reconstruction and selection efficiencies are assumed to be
fully correlated. The theoretical cross-section for
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
is
{74.3_{-2.1}^{+1.9}~\mathrm{pb}}
, and was calculated at
next-to-next-to-leading order using
Dynnlo

[
catani.07.1
,
catani.09.1
,
dynnlo.13.1
]
with
the MSTW
08
NNLO
\mathrm{PDF}
set
[
martin.09.1
]
.

Figure 69
:
A comparison between the LHCb
{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}
,
{pp\to Z\to ee}
, and
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section measurements divided by their expected

\mathrm{SM}
theoretical values. The equivalent
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}

measurements from ATLAS and CMS are also provided. The red
points indicate the muon, electron, or combined
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay
channels of the
Z
boson while the black points represent the
individual
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
categories. The blue bar provides the theoretical
uncertainty, centred about unity. The dark inner error bars
correspond to the statistical uncertainty for the individual
measurements, while the light outer error bars are the combined
systematic and luminosity uncertainties.

A graphical comparison between the combined result of

Eq.
207

and the individual results of
Eq.
206

is given in
Fig.
69

where the values are expressed as the
ratio of experiment to theory. The theoretical uncertainty is given by
the blue band centered about unity, while the statistical uncertainty
is given by the dark inner error bars and the systematic and
luminosity uncertainty are given by the light outer error bars for
each point. Each decay channel of the
Z
boson is highlighted in red,
while the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay categories for the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
channels are
given in black. Comparisons to the
{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}

[
lhcb.12.1
]
and
{pp\to Z\to ee}

[
lhcb.13.2
]

cross-sections from LHCb are made, as well as the
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
cross-section measurements from ATLAS
[
atlas.11.1
,
atlas.12.1
]
and CMS
[
cms.11.1
]
.

All measurements, within uncertainty, are consistant with their
corresponding
\mathrm{SM}
theory predictions. Of the three combined
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
measurements, the LHCb measurement of this chapter
is the most precise, primarily due to a much lower systematic
uncertainty than either the ATLAS or CMS measurements, specifically
in the semi-hadronic final states. Both ATLAS and CMS reconstruct
hadronic final states of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay using jet reconstruction
algorithms, resulting in large uncertainties on the jet energy scale
and identification efficiency. The combined cross-section uncertainty
for the LHCb measurement is
7.2\%
while the ATLAS uncertainty is

9.5\%
and the CMS uncertainty is
10.2\%
.

The efficacy of the
\mathrm{VELO}
subdetector in separating
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

events in the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category from
{Z\to\mu\mu}
events is clear, in
comparison to the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
results from ATLAS and CMS. In this
category, LHCb achieves a signal purity of approximately
70\%
while
ATLAS attains a purity of
50\%
and CMS a purity of
60\%
. The
purity of the LHCb
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
category is slightly reduced to the ATLAS and CMS measurements as
{{\not\mathrel{E}}_{\mathrm{T}}}
cannot be reconstructed within LHCb and
used to further reduce the
\mathrm{QCD}
and
\mathrm{EWK}
backgrounds. The purities for
the semi-leptonic
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
and
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
categories are approximately equivalent
between the three experiments.

The lepton universality test of
Eq.
186

can be performed,

\frac{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}}{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to ee}}=1.01\pm 0.08,\quad\quad\frac{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}}{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to ee}}=0.95\pm 0.07

(208)

using the combined result of this chapter and the
{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}
and
{pp\to Z\to ee}
cross-section measurements of
LHCb. Here, the luminosity uncertainty is assumed to be fully
correlated as the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
and
{Z\to ee}
analyses were
both performed using the same luminosity measurement. Additionally the ratio,

\frac{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}}{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to\mu\mu}}=0.94\pm 0.09

(209)

can be calculated where all uncertainties between the two
cross-sections are assumed to be uncorrelated as the
{Z\to\mu\mu}

LHCb analysis uses the
2010
dataset and not the
2011
dataset of
the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
analysis. All three ratios from

Eqs.
208

and
209
verify lepton
universality under the unique conditions observed by LHCb.

Chapter 6

Higgs Boson Limits

The upper limits on the production of neutral Higgs bosons decaying into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs using the data from the analysis of
Chap.
5

are presented
within this chapter. The Higgs boson phenomenology needed to determine the
event model is introduced in
Sect.
12

while the event model
itself is described in
Sect.
13

, the statistical methods used to
calculate the limits are outlined in
Sect.
14

, and the limits are
presented in
Sect.
15

.

Further investigation of the boson with a mass of approximately

125~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
, discovered by ATLAS
[
atlas.12.2
]
and CMS
[
cms.13.1
]
is required to determine whether the properties of the
boson match those of a Higgs boson from the standard model (
\mathrm{SM}
),
supersymmetry (
\mathrm{SUSY}
), or other models beyond the
\mathrm{SM}
. Within both
the
\mathrm{SM}
and the minimal supersymmetric model (
\mathrm{MSSM}
), the Higgs boson is
predicted to couple to fermions with a strength proportional to the
mass of the fermion, as previously shown by the vertices of

Fig.
6(g)

, and
Figs.
10(a)

through

10(h)
. Consequently, the neutral Higgs bosons, whether
\mathrm{SM}
or

\mathrm{MSSM}
, are expected to decay into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs over two orders of
magnitude more often than into muon pairs, and seven orders of
magnitude more often than into electron pairs.

Within this chapter the neutral
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson is denoted by
H
, while for
the
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons the light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson is denoted by
{h^{0}}
, the heavy

\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson by
{H^{0}}
, the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson by
{A^{0}}
, and the charged Higgs bosons by
{H^{\pm}}
. Any neutral Higgs boson, whether
\mathrm{SM}
,
\mathrm{MSSM}
, or otherwise, is
indicated by
{\phi^{0}}
, while any neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs boson is indicated by

{\Phi^{0}}
. Model independent upper limits on the production for a neutral
Higgs boson decaying into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair,
\sigma_{pp\to{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
,
within the LHCb acceptance,
{2.0\leq\eta_{\tau}\leq 4.5}
, as a
function of the mass of the Higgs boson are set using both the individual
event categories of
Chap.
5

and their combination. The combined
limit is compared to the expected cross-section from the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson. Model dependent limits on
{\tan\beta}
are set for the three neutral

\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons decaying into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs as a function of the mass of the

\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson. These limits are also set using the individual
categories and their combination.

The
\mathrm{MSSM}
limits are set using the
{m_{h^{0}}^{\mathrm{max}}}
scenario of

Ref.
[
carena.03.1
]

where the parameter space is selected to maximise
the mass of the light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson. This benchmark provides the most
conservative limits on
{\tan\beta}
for a given mass of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson and
is commonly used amongst experiments when reporting
\mathrm{MSSM}
limits. This
allows the limits of this chapter to be compared to results from
ATLAS, CMS, and LEP. The
\mathrm{SM}
parameters for this scenario are set
at
172.5~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the
t
-quark mass
m_{t}
,
4.213~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the bottom
quark mass
m_{b}
using the
\overline{\textrm{MS}}
scheme, and
0.119
for the strong
coupling
{\alpha_{s}}(m_{Z})
. The
\mathrm{SUSY}
parameters are set at
1~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
for
the soft
\mathrm{SUSY}
-breaking mass
m_{\mathrm{SUSY}}
,
2~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the stop mixing
parameter
X_{t}
,
200~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the
{\mathit{SU}(2)}
gaugino mass parameter

m_{2}
,
200~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the Higgs mixing parameter
\mu
, and

800~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for the gluino mass parameter
m_{3}
.

12

Higgs Phenomenology

To set limits on the production of Higgs bosons, both the production and
decay of the Higgs bosons must be known. Within this section the branching
fractions are given in
Sect.
12.1

and the cross-sections in

Sect.
12.2

for both the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson and the neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons. The
branching fractions and cross-sections depend upon the mass of the
Higgs boson, and for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
, also depend upon
{\tan\beta}
. However, the masses
of the light and heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs bosons of the
\mathrm{MSSM}
can be written in
terms of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass,
m_{A^{0}}
, and
{\tan\beta}
. Consequently,
the branching fractions and cross-sections for the
\mathrm{SM}
are given as a
function of only
m_{H}
, while the
\mathrm{MSSM}
branching fractions and
cross-sections are given as a function of
m_{A^{0}}
and
{\tan\beta}
.

In
Fig.
70

the light and heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson masses are
plotted as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and
{\tan\beta}
. The masses
are calculated using the program
FeynHiggs

[
heinemeyer.98.1
,
heinemeyer.98.2
,
degrassi.03.1
,
frank.07.1
,
feynhiggs.13.1
]

which performs the calculations up to the order
{\alpha_{e}}{\alpha_{s}}
. The features
of these mass functions can be understood at tree-level using the
relations,

\displaystyle m_{h^{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(m_{A^{0}}^{2}+m_{Z}^{2}-\left((m_{A^{0}}^{2}-m_{Z}^{2})^{2}+4m_{Z}^{2}m_{A^{0}}^{2}{\sin^{2}2\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

(210)

\displaystyle m_{H^{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(m_{A^{0}}^{2}+m_{Z}^{2}+\left((m_{A^{0}}^{2}-m_{Z}^{2})^{2}+4m_{Z}^{2}m_{A^{0}}^{2}{\sin^{2}2\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle m_{H^{\pm}}^{2}

\displaystyle=m_{A^{0}}^{2}+m_{W}^{2}

which are determined from the
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs boson mass eigenstate matrix of

Eq.
86

. Here,
\mu
is the Higgs mixing parameter as
described in
Sect.
1.4

.

((a))

((b))

Figure 70
:
Masses of the
light
\mathcal{CP}
-even
and
heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons as a function of
the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and
{\tan\beta}
, calculated using

FeynHiggs

[
heinemeyer.98.1
,
heinemeyer.98.2
,
degrassi.03.1
,
frank.07.1
,
feynhiggs.13.1
]
.

The relations of
Eq.
210

result in a light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson mass that must be less than or equal to
m_{Z}\left|{\cos 2\beta}\right|
, a heavy

\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson mass that must be greater than or equal to
m_{Z}
, and
a charged Higgs boson mass that must be greater than
m_{W}
. The mass of the

\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson is bounded by the light and heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even masses,

m_{h^{0}}\leq m_{A^{0}}\leq m_{H^{0}}
, and for large
m_{A^{0}}
the mass
of the heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson approaches that of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson.

In
Fig.
70

the light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson mass plateaus at

130~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for larger
m_{A^{0}}
rather than
m_{Z}
, as heavy quark
and squark loops provide sizable corrections at the one-loop
level. The heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson mass approaches the maximum
m_{h^{0}}

at low
m_{A^{0}}
as expected, and for
m_{A^{0}}>140~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
is nearly
degenerate with the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson at
{\tan\beta}
values greater than

20
. However, for a given
m_{A^{0}}
, the heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson mass
increases asymptotically as
{\tan\beta}
approaches zero.

12.1

Branching Fractions

The decay width for the
\mathrm{SM}
or
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons is calculated as the
sum of the partial decay widths,

\Gamma_{\phi^{0}}=\sum_{i}\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to f_{i}\bar{f}_{i}}+\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to gg}+\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to\gamma\gamma}+\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to Z\gamma}+\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to ZZ}+\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to WW}

(211)

where the summation is over muon,
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
,
s
-quark,
c
-quark,
b
-quark, and
t
-quark pairs, and the remaining terms are the possible gauge boson
combinations. The branching fraction for a given channel is then,

{\mathcal{B}_{{\phi^{0}}\to X}}=\frac{\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to X}}{\Gamma_{\phi^{0}}}

(212)

where
\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to X}
is the partial decay width for the
channel. First the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson branching fractions are described,
followed by a description of the
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons branching fractions.

12.1.1

SM Branching Fractions

Over the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson mass range considered in this chapter,
{90<m_{\phi^{0}}<250~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
, the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
,
b
-quark, gluon,
W
boson, and
Z
boson pair partial
widths dominate the total width of the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson. The branching
fractions for these channels as a function of the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson mass are
plotted in
Fig.

. These branching fractions, with
uncertainties, are calculated following the prescription of

Ref.
[
denner.11.1
]

which uses the programs
HDecay

[
djouadi.97.1
,
spira.97.1
,
butterworth.10.1
,
hdecay.13.1
]

and
Prophecy4f

[
bredenstein.06.1
,
bredenstein.06.2
,
bredenstein.06.3
,
prophecy.13.1
]
. Further details on these
calculations are given in
App.

, as well as tabulated values
for the
H\to{\tau\tau}
branching fraction. The features of the
branching fractions plotted in
Fig.

can be understood from
the simpler tree-level calculations of the partial widths which can be
determined using the vertices of
Fig.
6

,

Eq.
68

, and the methods outlined in
Chap.
2

.

Figure 71
:
Branching fractions, as a percentage, for the
five leading decay channels of the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson in the relevant mass
range:
\tau\tau
(red),
b\bar{b}
(blue),
gg
(green),
{WW}

(orange), and
ZZ
(magenta). The branching fractions and
uncertainties, indicated by the coloured bands, are calculated using
the results of
Ref.
[
denner.11.1
]

and plotted as a function of the
Higgs boson mass.

Figure 71
:
Branching fractions, as a percentage, for the
five leading decay channels of the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson in the relevant mass
range:
\tau\tau
(red),
b\bar{b}
(blue),
gg
(green),
{WW}

(orange), and
ZZ
(magenta). The branching fractions and
uncertainties, indicated by the coloured bands, are calculated using
the results of
Ref.
[
denner.11.1
]

and plotted as a function of the
Higgs boson mass.

For fermions above the threshold
{m_{H}>2m_{f}}
the relevant
vertex is
Fig.
6(g)

and the tree-level decay width from

Ref.
[
resnick.73.1
]

is,

\Gamma_{H\to f\bar{f}}=\frac{N_{c}{g_{w}^{2}}m_{f}^{2}}{32\pi m_{W}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{4m_{f}^{2}}{m_{H}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}m_{H}

(213)

where
N_{c}
is the number of colours,
1
for leptons and
3
for
quarks, and
{g_{w}}
is the
{\mathit{SU}(2)}
gauge coupling. For the Higgs boson masses
considered, the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and
b
-quark channels dominate the fermion partial
width, and the
b
-quark pair partial width is expected to be a factor of

{3m_{b}^{2}/m_{\tau}^{2}\approx 15}
times larger than the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair width
as can be seen in
Fig.

. Electron,
u
-quark, and
d
-quark pairs are
not considered in the calculation of the total decay width, as their
partial widths are less than six orders of magnitude smaller than the

{H\to b\bar{b}}
width resulting in a maximum branching fraction
of less than
10^{-4}\%
.

For on-shell vector bosons,
W
and
Z
, the corresponding vertices are

Figs.
6(b)

and
6(c)
and the tree-level decay
width from
Ref.
[
lee.77.1
]

is,

\Gamma_{H\to VV}=\frac{N_{m}{g_{w}^{2}}}{64\pi m_{W}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{4m_{V}^{2}}{m_{H}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1-\frac{4m_{V}^{2}}{m_{H}^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{4m_{V}^{2}}{m_{H}^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)m_{H}^{3}

(214)

for a Higgs boson mass above threshold,
{m_{H}>2m_{V}}
, where
N_{m}
is a
multiplicity factor of
1/2
for the
Z
boson and
1
for the
W
boson. This
factor results in a
W
boson pair partial width approximately double that
of the
Z
boson pair width for
{m_{H}>2m_{Z}}
, as can be seen in

Fig.

. Additionally, the
m_{H}^{3}
term ensures that the
partial widths of
Eq.
214

are on the order of
m_{H}^{2}

times larger than the fermionic partial widths for large
m_{H}
.

However, the on-shell decay width of
Eq.
214

is not
sufficient to describe the full
W
and
Z
boson branching fractions. It is
also necessary to include decays where one or both of the vector bosons
are off-shell. From
Ref.
[
keung.84.1
]

, this partial width can be
written as,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{H\to VV^{*}}=\frac{F_{V}{g_{w}^{4}}}{\pi^{3}}\Bigg(

\displaystyle\frac{3\left(1-8x^{2}+20x^{4}\right)}{\sqrt{4x^{2}-1}}\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{3x^{2}-1}{2x^{3}}\right)

(215)

\displaystyle-3\left(1-6x^{2}+4x^{4}\right)\ln\left(x\right)

\displaystyle-\left(1-x^{2}\right)\left(\frac{47}{2}x^{2}-\frac{13}{2}+x^{-2}\right)\Bigg)m_{H}

where
x
is
{m_{V}/m_{H}}
and the pre-factors
F_{V}
are,

F_{W}=\frac{3}{512},\quad\quad F_{Z}=\frac{7-\frac{40}{20}{\sin^{2}\theta_{w}}+\frac{160}{9}{\sin^{4}\theta_{w}}}{2048\,{\cos^{4}\theta_{w}}}

(216)

for the
W
boson and
Z
boson respectively; here,
\theta_{w}
is the weak-mixing
angle. Over the mass range
2m_{W}<m_{H}<2m_{Z}
the partial
width for the
W
boson pair channel is given by
Eq.
214

while
the width for the
Z
boson pair channel is given by
Eq.
215

resulting in a dip in the
H\to ZZ
branching fraction, which is
clearly visible in
Fig.

. Over this range the
W
boson pair
partial width is anywhere between ten to fifty times larger than the

Z
boson pair width. For Higgs boson masses below the mass of the
W
and
Z
bosons,
the double off-shell width is necessary, which is not discussed here,
but can be found in
Ref.
[
spira.97.1
]

.

The decay widths for the additional gauge boson combinations, gluon
pairs, photon pairs, and
Z
bosons with photons, do not have tree-level
diagrams due to the massless gluon and photon. Of these three
channels, only the gluon pair channel provides a considerable
contribution to the total decay width for the Higgs boson masses considered
here. From
Ref.
[
gunion.90.1
]

, the width for Higgs boson decays into gluon
pairs can be written as,

\Gamma_{H\to gg}=\frac{{g_{s}^{4}}{g_{w}^{2}}}{2048\pi^{5}m_{W}^{2}}\left(\sum_{i}\big(x_{i}+(x_{i}-x_{i}^{2})F(x_{i})\big)\right)\left(\sum_{i}\big(x_{i}+(x_{i}-x_{i}^{2})F(x_{i})\big)\right)^{\dagger}m_{H}^{3}

(217)

where
{g_{s}}
is the
{\mathit{SU}(3)}
gauge coupling and
x_{i}
is
4m_{i}^{2}/m_{H}^{2}

for fermion
i
. The summations are over all contributing fermion
loops where,

F(x)=\begin{cases}\left(\sin^{-1}x^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}&\mathrm{if}~x\geq 1\\
\frac{1}{4}\left(\pi+i\ln\left(\frac{2+2\sqrt{1-x}-x}{x}\right)\right)^{2}&\mathrm{else}\\
\end{cases}

(218)

and only massive quarks are considered, with the
t
-quark loop dominating
the decay width. From the ratio of
Eq.
217

to

Eq.
213

for both
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and
b
-quark pairs, one can see that
for the mass range considered in
Fig.

, the gluon pair width
will remain above the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair width, but below the
b
-quark pair width.

12.1.2

MSSM Branching Fractions

The branching fractions for the
{h^{0}}
,
{H^{0}}
, and
{A^{0}}
Higgs bosons decaying
into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass,
m_{A^{0}}
,
and
{\tan\beta}
are plotted in
Fig.
72

. These branching fractions
are calculated using the programs
HDecay
and
Prophecy4f
, and dressed
with
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings from
FeynHiggs
following the recommendations of

Ref.
[
denner.11.1
]

. Details on the calculation, as well as plots with
numerical values of these branching fractions, are provided in

App.

. Just as for the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson, the features of

Fig.
72

can be understood using tree-level calculations of
the partial widths.

((a))

((b))

Figure 72
:
Branching fractions, as a percentage, for

light
\mathcal{CP}
-even,
heavy

\mathcal{CP}
-even, and

\mathcal{CP}
-odd
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons decaying
into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and

{\tan\beta}
. The
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings are calculated with

FeynHiggs
.

((c))

Figure 72
:
Branching fractions, as a percentage, for

light
\mathcal{CP}
-even,
heavy

\mathcal{CP}
-even, and

\mathcal{CP}
-odd
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons decaying
into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and

{\tan\beta}
. The
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings are calculated with

FeynHiggs
.

Comparing the couplings of the neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons with fermions from the vertex factors of
Figs.
10(a)

through

10(f)
with the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson coupling given in

Fig.
6(g)

, the partial decay width for all three neutral Higgs bosons can be written as,

\Gamma_{{\Phi^{0}}\to f\bar{f}}=F_{\Phi^{0}}\Gamma_{H\to f\bar{f}}

(219)

where
\Gamma_{{\Phi^{0}}\to f\bar{f}}
is given by
Eq.
213

. For
the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson the exponent of
3/2
in
Eq.
213

is
reduced to an exponent of
1/2
due to the additional
\gamma^{5}

factor in the couplings of
Figs.
10(c)

and

10(f)
. The pre-factors
F_{\Phi^{0}}
are given by,

\displaystyle F_{h^{0}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{{\cos^{2}\alpha}}{{\sin^{2}\beta}}&\textrm{for}~\nu,u\\
\frac{{\sin^{2}\alpha}}{{\cos^{2}\beta}}&\textrm{for}~\ell,d\\
\end{cases},\quad\quad F_{H^{0}}=\begin{cases}\frac{{\sin^{2}\alpha}}{{\sin^{2}\beta}}&\textrm{for}~\nu,u\\
\frac{{\cos^{2}\alpha}}{{\cos^{2}\beta}}&\textrm{for}~\ell,d\\
\end{cases},

(220)

\displaystyle F_{A^{0}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\tan^{-}2\beta}&\textrm{for}~\nu,u\\
{\tan^{2}\beta}&\textrm{for}~\ell,d\\
\end{cases}

for the three neutral Higgs bosons where
u
is a
u
-type quark,
\nu
is a
neutrino,
d
is a
d
-type quark, and
\ell
is a charged lepton. The
parameter
\alpha
is the Higgs boson mass mixing angle.

A similar procedure for calculating the vector boson pair partial
width is possible by comparing the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson vertices from

Figs.
6(b)

and
6(c)
with the
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons vertices
from
Figs.
10(i)

through
10(l)
. Notice that here,
the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson does not couple with
W
or
Z
bosons. The vector boson
partial decay width can then be written as,

\Gamma_{{\Phi^{0}}\to VV}=F_{\Phi^{0}}\Gamma_{H\to VV}

(221)

where the pre-factors are given by,

F_{h^{0}}={\sin^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)},\quad\quad F_{H^{0}}={\cos^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)},\quad\quad F_{A^{0}}=0

(222)

for the light and heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs bosons and the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson. The
factor
{\cos^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)}
can be rewritten in terms of,

{\cos^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{m_{A^{0}}^{2}-m_{Z}^{2}{\cos 4\beta}}{2\left(m_{A^{0}}^{4}+m_{Z}^{4}-2m_{A^{0}}^{2}m_{Z}^{2}{\cos 4\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

(223)

for a given
m_{A^{0}}
and
{\tan\beta}
. In the limit of large
m_{A^{0}}
the
term
{\cos^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)}
approaches zero and so the vector boson pair partial
widths are suppressed at large
m_{A^{0}}
for the heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson,
while they approach the
\mathrm{SM}
widths for the light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson.

The branching fractions for the light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson, given in

Fig.
72(a)

, range from
8\%
at large
m_{A^{0}}
up to values
of
16\%
for large
{\tan\beta}
and low
m_{A^{0}}
. The upper limit on the
branching fraction is governed by the ratio of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and
b
-quark pair
partial widths from
Eq.
219

, which are enhanced by a
factor of
{1+{\tan^{2}\beta}}
. However, for large
m_{A^{0}}
the vector
boson pair widths increase, just as in the
\mathrm{SM}
, and so the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
branching fraction is reduced.

For the heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson, the vector boson pair partial widths of

Eq.
221

are suppressed by a factor of
{\cos^{2}\left(\beta-\alpha\right)}
, and
so the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and
b
-quark pair partial widths dominate the total width at
large
m_{A^{0}}
, as can be seen in
Fig.
72(b)

, resulting in
the maximum
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair branching fraction of
16\%
. Similar behaviour
can be seen in
Fig.
72(c)

at large
m_{A^{0}}
for the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd
Higgs boson due to the lack of couplings with vector bosons. The
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
and

b
-quark partial widths for the heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson are enhanced by a
factor of
{1+{\tan^{2}\beta}}
, while the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd widths are enhanced by a
factor of
{\tan^{2}\beta}
.

12.2

Cross-Sections

The cross-section for incoming particles
{p_{1}}
and
{p_{2}}
producing a Higgs boson can be related to the decay widths of
Sect.
12.1

using a result of

Ref.
[
bijnens.06.1
]

,

\sigma_{{p_{1}}{p_{2}}\to{\phi^{0}}}(s)\approx\frac{16\pi}{(2N_{s_{p_{1}}}+1)(2N_{s_{p_{2}}}+1)N_{c_{p_{1}}}N_{c_{p_{2}}}}\frac{\Gamma_{{\phi^{0}}\to{p_{1}}{p_{2}}}\Gamma_{\phi^{0}}}{(s-m_{Z}^{2})^{2}+m_{\phi^{0}}^{2}\Gamma_{\phi^{0}}}

(224)

where
s
is the centre-of-mass energy, and
N_{c}
and
N_{s}
are the
colour and spin multiplicities for
{p_{1}}
and
{p_{2}}
. At the LHC,

{p_{1}}
and
{p_{2}}
are two partons from the colliding protons, and so
the observable cross-section
\sigma_{pp\to{\phi^{0}}}
is calculated
using the partonic cross-section
\sigma_{{p_{1}}{p_{2}}\to{\phi^{0}}}
and the
factorisation theorem of
Eq.
78

. Thus, the
cross-section for Higgs boson production at the LHC depends upon the
knowledge of the proton
\mathrm{PDF}
, which introduces an uncertainty within
the range of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~C}{2-14}
.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

Figure 73
:
Example diagrams of
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson production at the
LHC from
gluon-gluon fusion,
vector-boson fusion,

associated vector boson production, and

associated heavy quark production.

Production of Higgs bosons at the LHC in proton-proton collisions occurs
primarily through four partonic processes, outlined in the example
diagrams of
Fig.
73

. Gluon-gluon fusion is shown in
Fig.
73(a)

,
vector-boson fusion in
Fig.
73(b)

, associated vector boson production in
Fig.
73(c)

, and associated heavy quark production in

Fig.
73(d)

. The cross-sections for each of these mechanisms is
dependent upon the mass and type of the Higgs boson, as well as
{\tan\beta}
for the

\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons. The cross-sections for
\mathrm{SM}
and
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons produced in
proton-proton collisions at a centre-of-mass energy of
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}

through these production mechanisms are given in this section.

12.2.1

SM Cross-Sections

The
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson cross-sections, as a function of mass, are shown in

Fig.

. These cross-sections are calculated using the
programs
Higlu

[
graudenz.93.1
,
spira.95.1
,
spira.95.2
,
higlu.13.1
]
,
dFG

[
catani.03.1
,
florian.09.1
,
dfg.13.1
]
, and

vbf@NNLO

[
bolzoni.10.1
,
bolzoni.11.1
,
vbfh.13.1
]
following the methods of

Ref.
[
hxswg.11.1
]

. Further details on these calculations can be found
in
App.

.

Figure 74
:
Cross-sections for
{pp\to H}
production
at the LHC with a centre-of-mass energy of
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The
inclusive cross-section (black) is the sum of gluon-gluon fusion (red), vector-boson fusion (blue), associated
W
boson production (green), associated
Z
boson production (orange), and associated heavy quark production (magenta). The
coloured bands provide the linearly combined
\mathrm{QCD}
scale,
{\alpha_{s}}
, and

\mathrm{PDF}
uncertainties for the cross-sections.

Figure 74
:
Cross-sections for
{pp\to H}
production
at the LHC with a centre-of-mass energy of
7~\mathrm{Te\kern-1.00006ptV}
. The
inclusive cross-section (black) is the sum of gluon-gluon fusion (red), vector-boson fusion (blue), associated
W
boson production (green), associated
Z
boson production (orange), and associated heavy quark production (magenta). The
coloured bands provide the linearly combined
\mathrm{QCD}
scale,
{\alpha_{s}}
, and

\mathrm{PDF}
uncertainties for the cross-sections.

The leading
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson production mechanism is gluon-gluon fusion which, just like the
Higgs boson decay into a gluon pair of
Eq.
217

, must proceed
through a fermion loop, as the massless gluon does not couple directly
with the Higgs boson. At lower masses this loop is dominated by
b
-quarks, but
for
m_{H}>2m_{t}
the
t
-quark loop contributes. While the gluon pair
partial decay width of
Eq.
217

is not the largest width
for the Higgs boson, the gluon contribution to the proton at low momentum
transfer is much larger than the other partons, as shown in

Fig.
9(a)

, resulting in a large cross-section from gluon-gluon fusion.

The vector-boson fusion contribution to the inclusive
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson cross-section is
nearly an order of magnitude smaller than the gluon-gluon fusion contribution, as
the partons for this process are quarks and not gluons. The
cross-section for associated vector boson production is even smaller with respect to gluon-gluon fusion, but at
lower Higgs boson masses is comparable to the vector-boson fusion contribution, as can be
seen in
Fig.

. The associated heavy quark production cross-section is calculated for
associated
t
-quarks and is nearly two orders of magnitude smaller than
the gluon-gluon fusion cross-section.

12.2.2

MSSM Cross-Sections

Two cross-sections are included in the inclusive cross-sections for
the
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons, gluon-gluon fusion and associated
b
-quark production, and are given for the three neutral
Higgs bosons as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and
{\tan\beta}
in

Fig.
75

. These cross-sections are calculated following the
recommendations of
Ref.
[
hxswg.11.1
]

using the programs
Higlu
and

ggH@NNLO

[
harlander.02.1
,
harlander.03.2
,
ggh.13.1
]
for the gluon-gluon fusion cross-section and the program

bbH@NNLO

[
harlander.03.1
,
bbh.13.1
]
for the associated
b
-quark production cross-section. Both of these
calculations are modified with
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings from

FeynHiggs
. Further details on the calculations, including additional
plots, can be found in
App.

.

((a))

((b))

Figure 75
:
Inclusive cross-sections for the production of
the
\mathrm{MSSM}

light
\mathcal{CP}
-even,

heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even, and

\mathcal{CP}
-odd
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons as a function of the

\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and
{\tan\beta}
. The cross-sections are calculated
using
Higlu
,
ggH@NNLO
, and
bbH@NNLO
with the
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings calculated
using
FeynHiggs
.

((c))

Figure 75
:
Inclusive cross-sections for the production of
the
\mathrm{MSSM}

light
\mathcal{CP}
-even,

heavy
\mathcal{CP}
-even, and

\mathcal{CP}
-odd
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons as a function of the

\mathcal{CP}
-odd Higgs boson mass and
{\tan\beta}
. The cross-sections are calculated
using
Higlu
,
ggH@NNLO
, and
bbH@NNLO
with the
\mathrm{MSSM}
couplings calculated
using
FeynHiggs
.

At large
{\tan\beta}
the inclusive
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs boson cross-sections can be enhanced
by nearly two orders of magnitude with respect to the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson cross-section, due to the additional
{\tan\beta}
dependence in the couplings
of
Fig.
10

. The light
\mathcal{CP}
-even Higgs boson approaches a
maximum mass of
130~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
for large
m_{A^{0}}
resulting in an
inclusive cross-section similar to the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson for all
{\tan\beta}
. As can
be seen in
Fig.
75(a)

, this maximum inclusive
cross-section is on the order of
10~\mathrm{pb}
which is comparable to the
inclusive cross-section for the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson given in
Fig.

.

13

Event Model

The decay of a neutral Higgs boson into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair produces an experimental
signature similar to
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events, and so the analysis of

Chap.
5

can also be used to determine the cross-section for neutral
Higgs boson production. However, the
{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}

cross-sections measured in
Chap.
5

match well with the theoretical
prediction, and so if
{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}
events are present within the
data, their contribution is too small to measure a
cross-section. Instead, this data can be used to set upper limits on
neutral Higgs boson production.

To set these limits, the expected number of background and Higgs boson signal
events is required, as well as the observed number of events. Here,
the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
signal of
Chap.
5

is now considered a
background. In
Sect.
13.1

the simulation samples used to determine
the number of expected Higgs boson signal events is described, and in

Sect.
13.2

both the expected number of background and signal events
are estimated. However, using just the number of expected and observed
events to set upper limits does not utilise differences between the
signal
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
events and the dominant
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

background events. The primary difference between these two event
types is the invariant mass of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products, and so the
invariant mass distributions of
Fig.
58

, normalised to the
number of expected events, can be used to increase the information
used to set the limits. In
Sect.
13.3

the signal and background
mass distributions are given, using both the simulation samples of

Sect.
13.1

and the normalisation of
Sect.
13.2

.

13.1

Simulation

The
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation samples are used to determine the
efficiency and acceptance corrections necessary to calculate the
number of expected signal events in
Sect.
13.2

, as well as
determine the invariant mass distributions of
Sect.
13.3

. The
samples are generated, simulated, digitised, and reconstructed
following the process described in
Sect.
8

. All samples are
generated with
\textsc{Pythia}~6

[
sjostrand.06.1
,
pythia.13.1
]
and simulated with

Gauss
using the LHCb simulation configuration
MC11a
. Over the
Higgs boson mass range considered in this chapter the leading order treatment
of
\textsc{Pythia}~6
is sufficient, as effects from higher order corrections,
off-shell effects, and signal and background interference are
small
[
hxswg.12.1
]
.

The Higgs boson signals are generated for seventeen mass steps between

90~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
and
250~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
in steps of
10~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
. For each mass step
five samples are generated, one for each event category of

Sect.
9.2

. Only events with generator level
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products
matching the
\eta
,
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
, and particle type requirements of

Sect.
9.2

are selected for full simulation and reconstruction. A
total of
10^{4}
events are fully reconstructed and simulated for each
mass step and event category.

The primary production mechanism for the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson, as described in

Sect.
12.2

, is gluon-gluon fusion and so the
{H\to{\tau\tau}}
samples are
generated for gluon-gluon fusion production. In the
\mathrm{MSSM}
, the dominant Higgs boson production mechanisms are both gluon-gluon fusion and associated
b
-quark production. However, the expected
number of signal events and invariant mass distribution when
determined from samples produced with either mechanism are found to be
consistent within uncertainty. Additionally, the
\mathcal{CP}
of the Higgs boson affects neither the expected number of signal events nor the mass
distribution, within uncertainty, and so the same simulation samples
used for the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson are also used for all three neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons.

13.2

Event Yields

The expected number of background events for the
\mathrm{QCD}
,
\mathrm{EWK}
,
{t\bar{t}}
,

{WW}
, and
{Z\to\ell\ell}
backgrounds have already been estimated in

Sect.
9.3

, and so only the expected number of
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

background events and
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
signal events need to be
estimated. In this section these expected number of events are
calculated using the results of
Sect.
10

, the theoretical
branching fractions and cross-sections of
Sect.
12

, and the
simulation of
Sect.
13.1

.

13.2.1

Expected
\bm{Z\to\tau\tau}
Background

Given a simulated
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
sample, the expected number of

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
events in data is given by,

N_{Z\to{\tau\tau}}=\frac{\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}\mathscr{L}{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}{\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}}{\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}\displaystyle\sum\limits{{}_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}}\left({\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}_{i}\right)}

(225)

where
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
is
{74.3_{-2.1}^{+1.9}~\mathrm{pb}}
,

N_{\mathrm{sim}}
is the number of events in the simulation sample, and all
remaining variables are the same as for the cross-section formula of

Eq.
200

. In the summation,
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}}_{i}
is the reconstruction
efficiency evaluated for event
i
in the simulated
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

sample. The theoretical cross-section for
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
from
Sect.
11

is used as this cross-section has a
higher precision than either of the experimental
{pp\to Z\to\ell\ell}
cross-sections,
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
and

\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
, measured with LHCb.

In
Table
13.2.1

the number of expected
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

background events in data, calculated using
Eq.
225

,
is given. The remaining backgrounds from
Table
9.3

have been
summed and are also provided, as well as the sum of these backgrounds
and the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}
background. The number of events observed in
data from
Table
9.3

is given for comparison. As can be seen,
the expected number of total background events and the number of
events observed in data are compatible within uncertainty.

,

Table

, as well as the expected number of
\mathrm{SM}

for

13.2.2

Expected Higgs Boson Signal

For the Higgs boson signal, the expected number of events is,

N_{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}(m_{\phi^{0}})=\frac{\sigma_{pp\to{\phi^{0}}}\left(m_{\phi^{0}}\right){\mathcal{B}_{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}}\left(m_{\phi^{0}}\right){\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}(m_{\phi^{0}})\mathscr{L}{\mathcal{A}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}\left(m_{\phi^{0}}\right){\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}}{\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}\displaystyle\sum\limits{{}_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}}\left({\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}_{i}\right)}

(226)

where
m_{\phi^{0}}
indicates the term is dependent upon the Higgs boson mass and

N_{\mathrm{sim}}
is the number of events in the simulated
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}

sample. The term
{\mathcal{B}_{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}}
is the branching fraction of
the Higgs boson into a
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pair and
\sigma_{pp\to{\phi^{0}}}
is the Higgs boson inclusive cross-section. Both terms are mass dependent and are
provided by the calculations of
Sects.
12.1

and
12.2
for

\mathrm{SM}
and
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons. For the
\mathrm{MSSM}
these terms also depend upon

{\tan\beta}
.

The cross-section
\sigma_{pp\to{\phi^{0}}}
is calculated with no
kinematic requirements, unlike
\sigma_{pp\to Z\to{\tau\tau}}
of

Eq.
225

, and so the mass dependent acceptance is
defined as the fraction of generator level events from
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation passing the
{p_{\mathrm{T}}}
and
\eta
requirements of

Sect.
9.2

. Because the same simulation samples are used for all
Higgs boson types and production mechanisms, the acceptance can be plotted as
a function of the Higgs boson mass, as is done for the five event categories
in
Fig.
76(a)

. As
m_{\phi^{0}}
increases, the longitudinal boost of
the Higgs boson is reduced, resulting in less events where both
\tau
leptons from
the Higgs boson fall within LHCb. Consequently, the acceptance decreases
for increasing
m_{\phi^{0}}
. Numerical values for these acceptances are
provided in
Fig.

of
App.

.

((a))

((b))

Figure 76
:
Acceptances and

efficiencies for the Higgs boson signal as a function
of the Higgs boson mass for the five event categories. The uncertainties
for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
(red),
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
(blue),
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
(green),
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
(orange), and

{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
(magenta) event categories are provided by their corresponding
coloured bands.

The selection efficiency
{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}
is mass dependent and is
calculated using the same methods as
Sect.
10.2

but with
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
simulation samples. The combined selection and
reconstruction efficiency correction from
Eq.
226

is,

{\varepsilon_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}(m_{\phi^{0}})\equiv\frac{{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sel}}}(m_{\phi^{0}})}{\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}\displaystyle\sum\limits{{}_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}}\left({\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rec}}^{-1}}_{i}\right)}

(227)

and is plotted in
Fig.
76(b)

for all five event
categories. Again, the same Higgs boson simulation samples are used for all
Higgs boson types, and so
{\varepsilon_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
is only a function of the
Higgs boson mass. As
m_{\phi^{0}}
increases, the boosts of the
\tau
leptons from the Higgs boson increase, producing more collinear
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decays and subsequent decay
products with larger momenta. Consequently, both the
{|\Delta\phi|}
selection
efficiency is expected to increase, as well as the reconstruction
efficiencies, resulting in
{\varepsilon_{\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}}
rising for
increasing
m_{\phi^{0}}
. Numerical values of the efficiency for each event
category are given in
Fig.

of
App.

.

In
Table
13.2.1

, the expected number of
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson events multiplied
by a factor of
100
and assuming
{m_{H}=125~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
is given for
each event category. The number of expected
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson events, without
uncertainty included, is tabulated as a function of
m_{\phi^{0}}
in

Fig.
77

for all five event categories, as well as the sum
of all five categories. The expected number of neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs boson events for each category is tabulated in
Fig.
78

and for
the sum of all categories in
Fig.
79

as a function
of
m_{A^{0}}
and
{\tan\beta}
. As can be seen, the expected number of
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson signal events is less than
1
for all event categories, while
for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
as many as
120
events are expected.

Sect.

\mathrm{EWK}
,

{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

backgrounds as well as the
{{\phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
signal, are taken from
simulation which has been calibrated as described in

Sect.
9.2

. Additionally, the events used to produce the
distributions are weighted on an event-by-event basis for the
differences between the efficiencies evaluated from simulation and
data as determined in
Sects.
10.1

and
10.2
. This
correction is negligible in comparison to the momentum resolution
calibration.

14

Statistical Methods

The tool-set used to set model independent and dependent limits on
Higgs boson production within the forward region of LHCb is now
presented. The primary background can be found in the general
statistics textbooks of
Refs.
[
kendall.45.1
,
*kendall.46.1
]

and

[
brandt.99.1
]
, and the more particle physics oriented textbooks
of
Refs.
[
cowan.98.1
]

and
[
barlow.89.1
]
, as well as the Particle
Data Group review of statistics in
Ref.
[
pdg.12.1
]

. This analysis also
relies heavily upon asymptotic approximations of test statistics, for
which a comprehensive guide is given in
Ref.
[
cowan.10.1
]

.

Two hypothesis are considered: a background only hypothesis,

{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
, and a signal plus background hypothesis,
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
. The
hypotheses can be parametrised as
{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
by a fractional signal
strength factor
\mu
, where
{\mu=0}
is the background only
hypothesis and
{\mu=1}
is the signal plus background
hypothesis. If a hypothesis is fully specified with no unknown
parameters, the hypothesis is simple, while if the hypothesis depends
upon one or more unknown parameters, e.g reconstruction
efficiencies, the hypothesis is complex.

A set of random variables
\vec{x}
observed in data is compared with the
hypotheses. The agreement between the set of random variables and the
proposed hypothesis can be expressed via a test statistic,

\vec{{t}}(\vec{x})
. Ideally the test statistic can be expressed as
a single random variable,
{t}(\vec{x})
, without a loss of
discrimination between the hypotheses.

14.1

Hypothesis Testing

The probability density function for a test statistic is dependent
upon the hypothesis considered,
{\mathit{pdf}({t}|\mu)}
, where
\mu
is
the fractional signal strength parameter of the hypothesis

{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
. In
Fig.

an example of the
\mathit{pdf}
s
for a test statistic is given. The dashed red curve gives the test
statistic probability density function for the background only
hypothesis,
{\mathit{pdf}({t}|0)}
, while the dotted blue curve gives

{\mathit{pdf}({t}|1)}
for the signal plus background hypothesis. An
example experimental measurement is made with a result of
\vec{x}_{\mathrm{obs}}

and the corresponding test statistic is
{t}_{\mathrm{obs}}
, given by the
vertical black line of
Fig.

.

Figure 82
:
Probability density functions for the test
statistic
{t}
, using the test statistic of

Eq.
234

with the likelihood function of

Eq.
247

, assuming the background only
hypothesis (red) and the signal plus background hypothesis
(blue). The
p\textrm{\,-values}
are given by the fills for
p_{0}

(red) and
p_{1}
(blue).

Figure 82
:
Probability density functions for the test
statistic
{t}
, using the test statistic of

Eq.
234

with the likelihood function of

Eq.
247

, assuming the background only
hypothesis (red) and the signal plus background hypothesis
(blue). The
p\textrm{\,-values}
are given by the fills for
p_{0}

(red) and
p_{1}
(blue).

A hypothesis
{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
is always tested via rejection, not
acceptance, by defining a critical region,
{\mathcal{C}}
, within the space of
possible test statistics, such that the probability of observing

\vec{{t}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, assuming
{\mathcal{H}_{\mu}}
, is
p
. An
experimental measurement is then made, and if
\vec{{t}}_{\mathrm{obs}}
falls
within the critical region the hypothesis is rejected with a
significance level
p
. For a test statistic of dimension one like
that of
Fig.

, the critical region can be defined
by,

p\equiv\int_{{c}_{1}}^{{c}_{2}}\mathit{pdf}({t}|\mu)\textrm{\,d}{{t}}

(228)

where
{c}_{1}
and
{c}_{2}
are the limits of the critical
region. The critical region is determined by which hypothesis is being
tested and at what confidence level the test is being performed.

14.1.1

Confidence Levels

If looking for evidence of a new signal, the background only
hypothesis must be rejected. For an observed test statistic

{t}_{\mathrm{obs}}
, the background only hypothesis can be
rejected at a maximum significance of,

p_{0}\equiv\int_{{t_{\mathrm{obs}}}}^{\infty}\mathit{pdf}({t}|0)\textrm{\,d}{{t}}

(229)

where
p_{0}
is the background only
p\textrm{\,-value}
, given by the red fill in
the example of
Fig.

. The background only
p\textrm{\,-value}
is
then the probability of observing a
{t}
larger than
{t_{\mathrm{obs}}}
for
an ensemble of repeated experiments, assuming

{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
. Alternatively, the background only hypothesis confidence
level is oftentimes defined as,

{\mathrm{CL_{b}}}\equiv 1-p_{0}

(230)

for which a large value indicates a low level of confidence in the
hypothesis. Typically to claim the discovery of a new signal in
particle physics, the background only hypothesis must be rejected with
a
p_{0}
of
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~C}{2.9\times 10^{-5}}
or less. Further discussion of
this convention can be found in
Ref.
[
feldman.06.1
]

.

When testing the signal plus background hypothesis
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
the
p\textrm{\,-value}
,

p_{1}\equiv\int_{-\infty}^{{t_{\mathrm{obs}}}}\mathit{pdf}({t}|1)\textrm{\,d}{{t}}

(231)

is used, which in the example of
Fig.

is given by
the blue fill. Assuming
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
is true,
p_{1}
is the
probability of observing a
{t}
lower than
{t_{\mathrm{obs}}}
for an
ensemble of repeated experiments. The signal plus background
confidence level is defined as,

{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}\equiv p_{1}

(232)

where now a small value indicates a low level of confidence in the
hypothesis. In particle physics, a signal plus background hypothesis
is oftentimes considered to be excluded at a
95\%
confidence level
if
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
is found to be less than
5\%
. Here, the
confidence level is defined as
{1-{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}}
. At this confidence
level, assuming the signal plus background hypothesis,
95\%
of
repeated experiments will produce a
{t}
greater than
{t_{\mathrm{obs}}}
.

Figure 83
:
The test statistic
\mathit{pdf}
s for an example
experiment resulting in a mis-leading exclusion at

95\%~{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
. The black line indicates the observed test statistic

{t}_{\mathrm{obs}}
.

Figure 83
:
The test statistic
\mathit{pdf}
s for an example
experiment resulting in a mis-leading exclusion at

95\%~{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
. The black line indicates the observed test statistic

{t}_{\mathrm{obs}}
.

Using
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
for an experiment with a small signal and large background
can result in mis-leading exclusions of the signal plus background
hypothesis when there is a large downward fluctuation in the observed
number of events. The
\mathit{pdf}
s for the test statistic assuming

{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
and
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
for such an example are given in

Fig.

. Here, the signal plus background
hypothesis can be rejected at a confidence level of
95\%
using the

{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
method, despite poor agreement of the observed test statistic
with the background only hypothesis. One method to combat mis-leading
exclusions like this is to report both
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
so the
agreement with not only the signal plus background hypothesis but also
the background hypothesis is known.

However, this method does not allow for a simple comparison between
different experimental results. In particle physics
the
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
method,

{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}\equiv\frac{{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}}{{\mathrm{CL_{b}}}}=\frac{p_{1}}{1-p_{0}}

(233)

from
Refs.
[
junk.99.1
]

and
[
read.02.1
]
is oftentimes used instead
of the
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
method. Using this method for the example of

Fig.

does not result in a mis-leading exclusion
at the
95\%
confidence level, because while
p_{1}
is less than

5\%
,
p_{0}
is also large, resulting in a larger
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
.

Particle physics lends itself towards a frequentist interpretation of
probability, where probability is defined as the relative frequency
for an event to occur. The
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
method, like the
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
method, also
has a frequentist interpretation as shown in
Ref.
[
birnbaum.62.1
]

, and
when the expected number of signal events is large with respect to the
expected background the limits produced from the
{\mathrm{CL_{s+b}}}
and
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
methods converge. Alternatives to
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
have been proposed, e.g.

Ref.
[
cowan.11.1
]

, but for comparison purposes with other experiments
the
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
method is used in this analysis.

14.1.2

Test Statistics

The above discussion relies upon the test statistic
{t}
, which is
defined here. From the result of Neyman and Pearson
[
neyman.32.1
]

the most powerful test statistic, assuming simple hypotheses, is

t\equiv{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|1)-{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|0)=\ln\left(\frac{\mathit{pdf}(\vec{x}|1)}{\mathit{pdf}(\vec{x}|0)}\right)

(234)

when using the background only hypothesis test of

Eq.
229

and the signal plus background hypothesis test
of
Eq.
231

. Here,
{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\mu)
is the natural
logarithm of the likelihood function,

{\mathit{L}}(\vec{x}|\mu)\equiv\prod_{i}\mathit{pdf}(x_{i}|\mu)

(235)

given the random variables
x_{i}
of
\vec{x}
are independent. The
log-likelihood function is used for numerical stability.

The
\mathit{pdf}
of
{t}
can be determined analytically when the
\mathit{pdf}
s of

{\mathit{LL}}({t}|0)
and
{\mathit{LL}}({t}|1)
are known and

Eq.
234

is an invertible function. However, this is
oftentimes not the case, and so the
\mathit{pdf}
of
{t}
must be built using
a Monte Carlo technique: a large number of pseudo-experiments are
generated, the test statistic for each experiment is built, and the

\mathit{pdf}
is taken as the normalised distribution of
{t}
. This process
can be computationally expensive, particularly for complicated
likelihood functions, and so a numerically simpler alternative which
provides a similar separation power is preferable to
{t}
of

Eq.
234

.

Consider maximising
{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)
with respect to a signal
strength parameter
\nu
such that
{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu})
is the
maximum log-likelihood for a given
\vec{x}
and
\hat{\nu}
is the
maximum likelihood estimator. The first derivative of

{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)
can be expanded about the point
\hat{\nu}
,

\frac{\partial{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)}{\partial\nu}=\left.\frac{\partial{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)}{\partial\nu}\right|_{\hat{\nu}}+(\nu-\hat{\nu})\left.\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)}{\partial\nu^{2}}\right|_{\hat{\nu}}+\ldots

(236)

where the first term vanishes since
{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)
is at a maximum
for
\hat{\nu}
. In the limit for a large number of repeated
experiments
N
, the second derivative approaches the expectation
value of the set of experiments and can be written as
-1/\sigma^{2}

where
\sigma^{2}
is the variance. Additionally, the maximum likelihood
estimator
\hat{\nu}
becomes normally distributed,

\mathit{pdf}(\hat{\nu}|\mu)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(\hat{\nu}-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}

(237)

with the same variance
\sigma^{2}
and a mean of
\mu
; see

Ref.
[
brandt.99.1
]

for further details.

Equation
236

can then be written as,

\frac{\partial{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)}{\partial\nu}=-\frac{\nu-\hat{\nu}}{\sigma^{2}}

(238)

where the higher orders terms have been neglected. Integrating yields,

{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)=-\frac{(\nu-\hat{\nu})^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu})

(239)

where the initial condition
\nu=\hat{\nu}
is used to determine the
constant of integration
{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu})
. Using

Eq.
239

the profile likelihood ratio test
statistic,

{t_{\nu}}\equiv-2\left({\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)-{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu})\right)=\frac{(\nu-\hat{\nu})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}

(240)

is defined. This provides a test statistic that can be written without
likelihood functions but also provides a discrimination close to the
most powerful test statistic of
Eq.
234

. The only random
variable of
{t_{\nu}}
is
\hat{\nu}
and so the
\mathit{pdf}
of
{t_{\nu}}

can be found by,

\mathit{pdf}({t_{\nu}}|\mu)=\mathit{pdf}(\hat{\nu}({t_{\nu}})|\mu)\left|\frac{\partial\hat{\nu}({t_{\nu}})}{\partial{t_{\nu}}}\right|

(241)

where the
\mathit{pdf}
of
\hat{\nu}
is transformed to the variable

{t_{\nu}}
. The inverse of
{t_{\nu}}
,
\hat{\nu}({t_{\nu}}),

is
{\nu\pm\sigma\sqrt{{t_{\nu}}}}
and so

Eq.
241

becomes,

\mathit{pdf}({t_{\nu}}|\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{8\pi{t_{\nu}}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\nu-\mu}{\sigma}+\sqrt{{t_{\nu}}})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{8\pi{t_{\nu}}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\nu-\mu}{\sigma}-\sqrt{{t_{\nu}}})^{2}}

(242)

where
\mathit{pdf}(\hat{\nu}|\mu)
is given by
Eq.
237

,
the first term is from the
{\nu+\sigma\sqrt{{t_{\nu}}}}

solution, and the second term is from the
{\nu-\sigma\sqrt{{t_{\nu}}}}
solution of
\hat{v}({t_{\nu}})
. This

\mathit{pdf}
, further discussed in
Ref.
[
cowan.10.1
]

, is a non-central
chi-squared distribution for one degree of freedom. From a result of

Ref.
[
wald.43.1
]

the remaining terms of

Eq.
236

, which have not been explicitly
written, can be shown to contribute to
{t_{\nu}}
on the order of

1/\sqrt{n}
where
n
is the size of
\vec{x}
.

Figure 84
:
Probability density functions from
10^{6}

pseudo-experiments for the two-sided test statistic
{t_{0}}
,
using the test statistic of
Eq.
240

with the
likelihood function of
Eq.
247

, assuming
the background hypothesis (red) and the signal plus background
hypothesis (blue). The two
\mathit{pdf}
s are compared to

Eq.
242

, a chi-squared distribution with one
degree of freedom for
{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
(solid black) and a fitted
non-central chi-squared distribution with one degree of freedom
for
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
(dashed grey).

Figure 84
:
Probability density functions from
10^{6}

pseudo-experiments for the two-sided test statistic
{t_{0}}
,
using the test statistic of
Eq.
240

with the
likelihood function of
Eq.
247

, assuming
the background hypothesis (red) and the signal plus background
hypothesis (blue). The two
\mathit{pdf}
s are compared to

Eq.
242

, a chi-squared distribution with one
degree of freedom for
{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
(solid black) and a fitted
non-central chi-squared distribution with one degree of freedom
for
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
(dashed grey).

For the case when
\nu=\mu
,
Eq.
242

is a
chi-squared distribution of one degree of freedom, a result first
shown by Wilks in
Ref.
[
wilks.38.1
]

. The distributions necessary for
background and signal plus background hypothesis testing,

{\mathit{pdf}(t_{0}|0)}
and
{\mathit{pdf}(t_{1}|1)}
, are then fully defined by

Eq.
242

as
\nu
and
\mu
cancel and the terms
with
\sigma
are zero.

In
Fig.

the same example of

Fig.

is used and the distributions for

{\mathit{pdf}({t_{0}}|0)}
and
{\mathit{pdf}({t_{0}}|1)}
, the two-sided test
statistic of
Eq.
240

, are generated using the Monte
Carlo technique from
10^{6}
pseudo-experiments. These distributions
are shown in
Eq.
242

, where
{\mathit{pdf}({t_{0}}|0)}

approaches a chi-squared distribution of one degree of freedom and

{\mathit{pdf}({t_{0}}|0)}
approaches a non-central chi-squared
distribution. The two
\mathit{pdf}
s are also compared to the chi-squared
functions of
Eq.
242

which they approach.

In
Ref.
[
cowan.10.1
]

a test statistic for performing an upper limit test
on the signal plus background hypothesis is proposed,

q_{\nu}\equiv\begin{cases}-2\left({\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu)-{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu})\right)&\mathrm{if}~\hat{\nu}\leq\nu\\
0&\mathrm{else}\\
\end{cases}

(243)

which, following the same transformation procedure of

Eq.
241

, results in the
\mathit{pdf}
,

\mathit{pdf}(q_{\nu}|\mu)=\Phi\left(-\frac{\nu-\mu}{\sigma}\right)\delta(q_{\nu})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{8\pi q_{\nu}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\nu-\mu}{\sigma}-\sqrt{q_{\nu}})^{2}}

(244)

for the limit of large
N
where
\Phi
is the standard normal
cumulative distribution function. The cumulative distribution function
is then given by,

\mathit{cdf}(q_{\nu}|\mu)=\int_{-\infty}^{q_{\nu}}\mathit{pdf}(q_{\nu}^{\prime}|\mu)\textrm{\,d}q_{\nu}^{\prime}=\Phi\left(\sqrt{q_{\nu}}-\frac{\nu-\mu}{\sigma}\right)

(245)

and so for the special case of
{\nu=\mu}
the
\mathit{cdf}
is given by

\Phi(\sqrt{q_{\nu}})
. Consequently,
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
can be
calculated quickly by,

p_{0}=\Phi({q_{0}}_{\mathrm{obs}}),\quad\quad p_{1}=1-\Phi({q_{1}}_{\mathrm{obs}})

(246)

without the need to employ Monte Carlo techniques. In the example of

Figs.

and
the

p\textrm{\,-values}
, assuming the signal plus background hypothesis, are found to be

{p_{1}=1.07\times 10^{-2}}
using
{t}
of

Eq.
234

, and
{p_{1}=0.91\times 10^{-2}}
using

q_{\nu}
of
Eq.
243

.

14.2

Likelihoods, Medians, and Uncertainties

The test statistics of
Sect.
14.1

are built from likelihood
functions, which have not yet been defined. In this analysis two
likelihood functions are used, a simple likelihood function for
consistency checks and an extended likelihood function which utilises
the mass distributions of
Sect.
13.3

for producing the final upper
limits of
Sect.
15

. In this section these likelihoods are
described. Additionally, a method for building the median test
statistic with these likelihood functions as well as how uncertainty
can be incorporated into a likelihood function is outlined.

14.2.1

Likelihood Functions

The simple likelihood function is a Poisson
\mathit{pdf}
,

{\mathit{L}}_{s}(x|\mu)=\mathit{pdf}_{p}(x|N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}})=\frac{(N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}})^{x}e^{-N_{\mathrm{bkg}}-\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}}}}{x!}

(247)

where
x
is the number of observed events,
N_{\mathrm{bkg}}
is the expected
number of background events,
N_{\mathrm{sig}}
is the number of expected signal
events, and
\mu
is the signal strength parameter of

Sect.
14.1

. In the test statistics of
Eqs.
234

,

240
, and
243
the
ratio of two likelihoods is always taken for a given
x
and so the
discrete
x
factorial terms cancel. Consequently, while the observed

x
from an experiment must be an integer, the defined test
statistics can still be calculated for any real-valued

x
. Additionally, the
e^{N_{\mathrm{bkg}}}
terms cancel and can be omitted
when calculating the test statistics with this likelihood function.

The simple likelihood only utilises the number of expected and observed
events, without considering any additional information from the
events. The extended likelihood function utilises not only the number
of events, but also any other observables which are measured for each
event. The extended likelihood function is defined as,

{\mathit{L}}_{e}(\vec{x}|\mu)=e^{-N_{\mathrm{bkg}}-\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}}}\prod_{i}\big((N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}})\mathit{pdf}(\vec{o}_{i}|\mu)\big)

(248)

where
\vec{x}
consists of a set of observables
\vec{o}_{i}
for each
event
i
, and the product is over all events. The probability density
function for the set of observables
\vec{o}_{i}
, assuming a signal
strength
\mu
, is given by
\mathit{pdf}(\vec{o}_{i}|\mu)
. For this analysis,
only the invariant mass of the
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
decay products is measured per
event, and so
\vec{o}_{i}
is just the invariant mass for event

i
. The extended likelihood function of

Eq.
248

is the limit of the binned
likelihood function, which is the product of the simple likelihood
function for every bin, as the widths of the bins approach zero. For
further details on the properties of the extended likelihood function,
refer to
Ref.
[
barlow.90.1
]

.

((a))

((b))

Figure 85
:
The example of

Fig.

but now using the extended likelihood
function of
Eq.
248

rather than the
simple likelihood function to calculate the test
statistic.
Invariant mass
distribution for pseudo-data (points), expected background (red),
and expected signal (grey) used when calculating the extended
likelihood for this example.

An example demonstrating the additional separation power between the
background only and signal plus background hypotheses when using the
extended likelihood function is given in

Fig.
85

. This is the same example as

Fig.

, but now the extended likelihood is used to
calculate the test statistic of
Eq.
234

rather than
the simple likelihood function. The invariant mass distributions used
in this example are given in
Fig.
85(b)

. As can be
seen, the additional information from the mass for each event produces
a more significant exclusion of the signal plus background hypothesis.

14.2.2

Median Values

The sensitivity of an experiment in rejecting a given hypothesis is
quantified by the
p\textrm{\,-values}
for the median test statistics when testing
the null hypothesis, assuming the alternative hypothesis. When
rejecting
{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
,
p_{0}
for the median test statistic assuming

{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
,
M[{t}|1]
, characterises the experimental
sensitivity. Conversely, when rejecting
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
,
p_{1}
for

M[{t}|0]
characterises the sensitivity. The
p\textrm{\,-values}
from the
median test statistics can also be used to perform consistency checks.

The median test statistic is determined from,

\mathit{cdf}(M[{t}|\mu]|\mu)=\int_{-\infty}^{M[{t}|\mu]}\mathit{pdf}({t}^{\prime}|\mu)\textrm{\,d}{{t}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}

(249)

where the test statistics for half of all repeated experiments are
expected to fall below
M[{t}|\mu]
and half above, assuming the
signal strength parameter
\mu
is the true signal strength.

For the Neyman-Pearson ratio test statistic of

Eq.
234

, the median test statistic can be determined
by integrating the test statistic
\mathit{pdf}
s. When using the simple
likelihood function of
Eq.
247

the
\mathit{pdf}
of the
test statistic is,

\mathit{pdf}(t|\mu)=\frac{\mathit{pdf}_{p}\left(\left.\frac{t+N_{\mathrm{sig}}}{\ln(N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+N_{\mathrm{sig}})-\ln(N_{\mathrm{bkg}})}\right|N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}}\right)}{\ln(N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+N_{\mathrm{sig}})-\ln(N_{\mathrm{bkg}})}

(250)

which is a modified Poisson distribution. The mean of this
distribution is
{t}
evaluated for an
x
of
{N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}}}
. If the expected background plus signal is large, the

{\mathit{pdf}(t|\mu)}
approaches a normal distribution and,

M[t|\mu]=t(N_{\mathrm{sig}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{bkg}})

(251)

since the mean of the test statistic
\mathit{pdf}
approaches the median.

For the test statistic
q_{\nu}
of
Eq.
243

, the median
test statistic can be found using the Asimov dataset proposed
in
Ref.
[
cowan.10.1
]

and inspired by
Ref.
[
asimov.55.1
]

. The extended
log-likelihood function evaluated with the Asimov data set is,

{\mathit{LL}}_{e}(\vec{a}(\mu)|\nu)=\begin{aligned} &\int\ln\Big((N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\nu N_{\mathrm{sig}})\mathit{pdf}(\vec{o}|\nu)\Big)(N_{\mathrm{bkg}}+\mu N_{\mathrm{sig}})\mathit{pdf}(\vec{o}|\mu)\textrm{\,d}{\vec{o}}\\
&-N_{\mathrm{bkg}}-\nu N_{\mathrm{sig}}\\
\end{aligned}

(252)

as shown in
App.

. Here, the integral is over the joint
\mathit{pdf}
of the observables
\vec{o}
, where the first instance of the
\mathit{pdf}
is
evaluated with signal strength
\nu
of the test statistic
q_{\nu}
and
the second instance of the
\mathit{pdf}
is evaluated with signal strength

\mu
, the signal strength of the Asimov dataset. In this analysis,
the integral is only over the invariant mass
\mathit{pdf}
. As an example, the
median value for the
q_{1}
test statistic of

Eq.
244

can be evaluated as,

M[q_{1}|0]={\mathit{LL}}_{e}(\vec{a}(0)|1)-{\mathit{LL}}_{e}(\vec{a}(0)|0)

(253)

when using the extended likelihood function and assuming the
background only hypothesis is true.

14.2.3

Uncertainties

The test statistics of
Eqs.
234

,

240
, and
243
as well
as the likelihood functions of
Eqs.
247

and

248
do not incorporate any
experimental systematic uncertainties. While there is no standardised
method for incorporating uncertainties, a variety of methods can be
used. For the frequentist hypothesis testing of
Sect.
14.1

the
systematic uncertainties are introduced into the likelihood function
and the method of maximum likelihood from

Eq.
236

is used. The alternative methods of
marginalisation and hybrid marginalisation are described in

App.

, but are not used here.

If the uncertainties factorise, the likelihood function
becomes,

{\mathit{L}}(\vec{x}|\mu,\vec{\theta})=\prod_{i}\mathit{pdf}(x_{i}|\mu,\vec{\theta})\prod_{j}\mathit{pdf}(\theta_{j})

(254)

where the first product is over all
x_{i}
of
\vec{x}
and the second
product is over all
\theta_{j}
of
\vec{\theta}
. The probability density
function for each nuisance parameter is given by
\mathit{pdf}(\theta_{j})
. This
likelihood function is then introduced into the profiled likelihood
ratios which become,

{t_{\nu}}=-2({\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu,\hat{\hat{\vec{\theta}}})-{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x},\hat{\nu},\hat{\vec{\theta}}))

(255)

and,

q_{\nu}=\begin{cases}-2\left({\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\nu,\hat{\hat{\vec{\theta}}})-{\mathit{LL}}(\vec{x}|\hat{\nu},\hat{\vec{\theta}})\right)&\mathrm{if}~\hat{\nu}\leq\nu\\
0&\mathrm{else}\\
\end{cases}

(256)

where
\hat{\hat{\vec{\theta}}}
is the maximum likelihood estimator for a
given
\vec{x}
and
\nu
, and
\hat{\vec{\theta}}
is the maximum likelihood
estimator for a given
\vec{x}
and
\hat{\nu}
. The
\mathit{pdf}
s for

{t_{\nu}}
and
q_{\nu}
of
Eqs.
242

and

244
still hold, as does the method of
obtaining the median test statistic using the Asimov dataset.

In this analysis, each systematic uncertainty is introduced as a
normally distributed nuisance parameter with a mean of
\epsilon
and
a deviation of
\delta
. The likelihood functions of

Eqs.
247

and

248
then become,

{\mathit{L}}(\vec{x}|\mu,\vec{\theta})={\mathit{L}}(\vec{x}|\mu)\prod_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{i}\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{(\theta_{i}-\epsilon_{i})^{2}}{2\delta_{i}^{2}}}\right)

(257)

where
{\mathit{L}}(\vec{x}|\mu)
is determined using
\mathit{pdf}(\vec{x}|\mu,\theta)

evaluated at
\vec{\theta}
rather than the central values
\vec{\epsilon}
,
and the product is over all nuisance parameters
\theta_{i}
.

15

Results

Because no excess is seen in the number of observed events shown in

Fig.
81

and tabulated in
Table
13.2.1

when compared to the
background only hypothesis, upper limits are set on neutral Higgs boson production. Limits on model independent production of neutral Higgs bosons decaying into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs,
\sigma_{pp\to{\Phi^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
, within
the LHCb acceptance
2.0\leq\eta_{\tau}\leq 4.5
, using the Higgs boson phenomenology of
Sect.
12

, the event model of
Sect.
13

, and
the statistical methods of
Sect.
14

, are calculated for the
individual event categories. The limits are set at a
95\%
confidence
level, using
{\mathrm{CL_{s}}}
with the profiled likelihood method and are
determined as a function of the neutral Higgs boson mass,
m_{\phi^{0}}
. Similarly,
upper limits on
{\tan\beta}
from the production of the three neutral
\mathrm{MSSM}
Higgs bosons decaying into
\tau~\mathrm{lepton}
pairs, assuming the
{m_{h^{0}}^{\mathrm{max}}}
scenario, are
calculated for the five event categories as a function of the
\mathcal{CP}
-odd
Higgs boson mass,
m_{A^{0}}
.

The observed limits are calculated using the test statistic
q_{\nu}
of

Eq.
256

and the extended likelihood function of

Eq.
248

. The likelihood function is
determined using the invariant mass distributions of

Fig.
81

. The systematic uncertainty on each background
component, given in
Table
13.2.1

for the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

background and
Table
9.3

for the remaining backgrounds, is
introduced into the extended likelihood function as a nuisance
parameter using
Eq.
257

. Each uncertainty affects
the shape of the expected invariant mass distribution,

\mathit{pdf}(m|\mu,\vec{\theta})
, by scaling the individual background distribution
component associated with the uncertainty. The expected number of
background events is also affected by each nuisance parameter such
that
{{N_{\mathrm{bkg}}}_{i}=\theta_{i}}
. The uncertainty on the simulated mass
shape, determined in
Sect.
9.2

using the calibration of

Fig.
52(a)

, is also introduced as a nuisance
parameter, where the parameter only affects the component mass
distributions from simulation, e.g. the
{Z\to{\tau\tau}}

background mass shape.

These limits are given as the solid black lines in

Figs.
86

through
91

for each of the five event categories, and in
Fig.
92

for the combination of the five categories. In
Sect.
15.1

, the
observed limits for each event category are checked for consistency
against additional validation limits in
Fig.
86

for the
\mathrm{SM}
and
Fig.
87

for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
. In

Sect.
15.2

the final observed limits are compared with the expected
limits in
Fig.
90

for the
\mathrm{SM}
and

Fig.
91

for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
, as well as limits from
previous results in
Fig.
92

.

15.1

for each of the five event categories in
Fig.

for the model independent limits and
Fig.

for the
\mathrm{MSSM}
limits. The dashed green line is the observed limit,
using the same statistical methods, but without uncertainties
introduced. The dash-dotted orange line is the observed limit using
the
q_{\nu}
test statistic with a simple likelihood and with
uncertainties. For this limit, because no mass distribution
information is utilised, the mass shape uncertainty is not
included. Finally, the the dash-dot-dotted magenta line is the
observed limit using the
q_{\nu}
test statistic with a simple
likelihood and no uncertainties. These validation limits can be
compared using either
Fig.
86

or

Fig.
87

, but the results are more easily
interpreted for the model independent limits, as the
\mathrm{MSSM}
limits are
presented in
m_{A^{0}}
and
{\tan\beta}
space. Consequently, only the model
independent limits are discussed here.

The effect of including systematic uncertainties in the limits is seen
for the extended likelihood function by comparing the green and black
lines of
Fig.
86

. As expected, the limit
calculated without uncertainties is lower than the limit with
uncertainties. Additionally, for larger
m_{\phi^{0}}
the difference between
the two limits decreases, as the limit becomes more influenced by the
shape of the mass distribution which has a smaller uncertainty than
the relative scaling of the background components. The introduction of
the systematic uncertainties can also be seen in the magenta and
orange lines, calculated with the simple likelihood function, but now
the difference between the two limits is not dependent upon

m_{\phi^{0}}
. The pulls of the nuisance parameters,
(\theta-\epsilon)/\delta
, where
\theta
is the nuisance parameter,
\epsilon
is its
mean, and
\delta
is its deviation, are given in

Fig.

for
{m_{\phi^{0}}=90~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
and
\mu=\hat{\mu}

when calculated using the extended likelihood function. Here the
nuisance parameters for each of the five event categories as well as
the combined result are given.

(circles),

for which the nuisance parameter is

The effect of using the extended likelihood function, rather than the
simple likelihood function can be seen by comparing the black line
with the orange line or green line with the magenta line in

Fig.
86

. The limits calculated using the extended
likelihood function are consistently lower than the limits calculated
using the simple likelihood function, demonstrating the additional
exclusion power gained from utilising the mass shape information. The
difference between these two sets of limits is particularly pronounced
at large
m_{\phi^{0}}
where the mass distribution expected for the signal
differs significantly from the background mass distribution.

However, in
Fig.
86(b)

, the extended
likelihood function limits are consistently worse than the simple
likelihood function limits for the Higgs boson mass range of
{90\leq m_{\phi^{0}}\leq 145~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
. This behaviour can be attributed to an observed
invariant mass shape, given in

Fig.
81(b)

, that is more
consistent with the signal than with the background within this

m_{\phi^{0}}
range. To check this, the data has been modified to be more
consistent with the expected background in

Fig.
89(a)

which
results in the limits of

Fig.
89(b)

, where the limits
calculated using the extended likelihood function are now found to
outperform the limits calculated using the simple likelihood function.

((a))

((b))

Figure 89
:

Invariant
mass distribution of

Fig.
81(b)

modified to
produce an observed distribution more consistent with the
background only
hypothesis.
Validation
limits for this modified data, equivalent to

Fig.
89(b)

, where the
limits calculated using the extended likelihood function now
outperform the limits calculated using the simple likelihood
function.

The asymptotic approximation made in calculating
q_{\nu}
can be
checked by using the Monte Carlo technique, where a large number of
pseudo-experiments are randomly generated and a
\mathit{pdf}
for
q_{\nu}
is
built. Because this process is time consuming, checks were made for
only a few limit points. The limits calculated at these points using
the Monte Carlo technique are consistent with the limits calculated
assuming the asymptotic approximation.

and

, the

experiment, assuming the background only hypothesis. The dashed red

\pm 1\sigma
range for the median experiment, i.e.
68\%
of
experiments, assuming the background only hypothesis, are expected to
produce a limit within this band. Similarly, the light blue band
provides the
\pm 2\sigma
range about the limits expected from a
median experiment. The expected limit is calculated using
q_{\nu}

evaluated with the Asimov dataset of
Sect.
14.2

, which for the
extended likelihood function is given by
Eq.
252

.

The expected limits can be compared to the observed limits in either

Fig.

or
Fig.
91

, but just as
for the validation limits, the results are most easily interpreted for
the model independent limits. However, for both

Figs.

and

the
observed limits are consistent with the expected limits for the
background only hypothesis and fall within the expected
\pm 2\sigma

band for most of the five categories.

In the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
category, given by
Fig.
90(b)

,
a slight excess of events with respect to the background only
hypothesis is observed, resulting in a higher than expected upper
limit, slightly above the
\pm 2\sigma
band at large
m_{\phi^{0}}
. The
excess of events is particularly visible in the
{55-60~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}

invariant mass bin of

Fig.
81(b)

, resulting in the
difference between the expected limits and the observed limits
increasing for larger values of
m_{\phi^{0}}
, as the mass distribution for
large
m_{\phi^{0}}
becomes more consistent with this excess.

The
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\mu}}
category of
Fig.
90(a)

also has
an observed number of events larger than the background only
hypothesis. This excess is more evenly spread across the mass
distribution of
Fig.
81(a)

than
for the
{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{e}}
category, although a small excess is observed in the two
invariant mass bins from
{100-110~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}}
resulting in the difference
between the observed and expected limits increasing slightly for large

m_{\phi^{0}}
. The
{\tau_{e}\tau_{h}}
category has an overall excess of observed events,
but a slight deficiency in events for large invariant masses,
resulting in an observed limit above the expected limit for small

m_{\phi^{0}}
and below the expected limit for large
m_{\phi^{0}}
. The
{\tau_{e}\tau_{\mu}}
and

{\tau_{\mu}\tau_{h}}
categories both have a deficiency in the observed number of
events with respect to the background only hypothesis, yielding
observed limits slightly lower than the expected limits.

The five event categories are combined to produce the model
independent limits and the
\mathrm{MSSM}
limits of

Fig.
92

. The limits are calculated using the same
methods described for the individual limits of

Figs.
90

and
91
. In

Fig.
92(a)

the expected cross-section

\sigma_{pp\to H\to{\tau\tau}}
from the
\mathrm{SM}
Higgs boson is given by the
dotted black line, with the theoretical uncertainty given by the grey
band. A neutral Higgs boson mass of
125~\mathrm{Ge\kern-1.00006ptV}
is indicated by the vertical
black line. The ratio of this limit to the
\mathrm{SM}
expectation is given in

Fig.
92(b)

for further comparison. As can be
seen, the limit from data is nearly two orders of magnitude larger
than the expected
\mathrm{SM}
cross-section at lower Higgs boson masses. In

Fig.
92(c)

the LHCb limit from this
analysis is compared with limits from ATLAS, CMS, and LEP. The

36~\mathrm{pb}^{-1}
and
4.7~\mathrm{fb}^{-1}
ATLAS limits from
Refs.
[
atlas.11.2
]

and

[
atlas.13.1
]
correspond to analyses performed using the
2010

and
2011
ATLAS datasets respectively. Similarly the
36~\mathrm{pb}^{-1}
and

4.6~\mathrm{fb}^{-1}
CMS limits from
Refs.
[
cms.11.2
]

and
[
cms.12.1
]

correspond to the
2010
and
2011
CMS datasets. The LEP limit is
a combined lower limit for all LEP data from
Ref.
[
lep.06.2
]

. The
LHCb limit of this analysis is competitive with the ATLAS and CMS limits using
2010
datasets, but not with the ATLAS and CMS limits
using
2011
datasets. While ATLAS and CMS maintain or even gain
sensitivity for large
m_{A^{0}}
, the results of this chapter lose
sensitivity due to the decreased acceptance of
{{A^{0}}/{H^{0}}\to{\tau\tau}}
events where both
\tau
leptons are produced within LHCb.
